the debate on the sustainable use and marketing of plant protection products is a particularly difficult one. it's hard to find a balance between good and evil. on the one hand, chemical agents are harmful to the environment and to health. on the other hand, our agriculture cannot do without plant protection products, because otherwise the risk of plant diseases increases too much. this in turn has negative consequences for food production and the economy. the main task for policy makers and politicians is to find a healthy balance between sustainability and agricultural economics. i think that the compromise proposals before us are very much in that direction. that is why i can support the proposals. i am pleased, among other things, that the approach of risk reduction rather than just use reduction is being maintained. reducing use does not necessarily mean reducing risks. if the pesticide is reduced but then used in higher concentrations, there are few environmental and health benefits. i am also glad that the marketing authorization regulation has not become as restrictive as this house had proposed at first reading. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would therefore like to thank both rapporteurs warmly for their cooperation and efforts to achieve this result.
president. - thank you to the rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs. i think that the compromise reached is a step towards more sustainable agriculture. it's very much needed. of course, pesticides play an important role in preventing disease and pest infestation, but chemical pesticides also pollute the environment and can be harmful to health. it is therefore important that we start to reduce the gap between conventional farming on the one hand and organic farming on the other in a responsible way. this legislation does just that, in particular through three measures which are relevant here. first, some of the most dangerous chemical pesticides are banned. an exception may be made if no alternatives are available. so the tulip is not in danger, but a little less poison in the tulip is absolutely not harmful. secondly - and this is at least as important - there are incentives in the legislation to promote development for more sustainable means and means for small farmers. small crops are crops grown in limited quantities, such as tomatoes, sprouts and tulips. many gardeners feared that the legislation would endanger these crops, but fortunately this fear has proved to be unfounded. on the contrary, the regulation prepares the creation of a special fund to promote research into smallholder farming. a third measure is also important. member states should develop plans to reduce the risks of the use of residual chemical pesticides. the use of chemical pesticides is being reduced everywhere, especially in sensitive areas such as near schools. that's important too. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). i think it would have been better if we had also banned the neurotoxics; these substances may affect the functioning of the human nervous system. so you don't want to spray it on crops.
mr president, as a representative of the netherlands, i have followed this legislation with particular interest and care. we are dealing with a very specific situation as a low-lying country. unlike other areas in europe, it is not possible to operate with european-imposed buffer zones along all waterways. it's not working. you're making normal sustainable production in agriculture and horticulture impossible. the netherlands is also known for its small-scale plantations of tulips, onions, white loaf, etc. and therefore the compromise must be looked at very specifically. the compromise now being reached is much better than the one proposed by mrs breyer, which was voted on in committee. i voted against it as a member of the epp group. there are now good transitional arrangements in place where industry can move forward with innovation, with alternative means. we are now also getting the small farmers' fund, which will give a good boost to these alternatives. the three systems, the three zones in europe for recognition and admission, are also much more adapted to the real world. one more point of concern, mr president. this concerns the authorization of products from outside the european union. we are taking the lead in finding a good balance between public health and practicality, but what about imports from outside? i think that this competitive position in the wto, where this kind of thing is discussed, this parallel import and parallel trade should be discussed. we can accept the proposal; i would like to thank the rapporteurs and in particular mrs klaß and mrs hennicot-schoepges for their efforts. however, the one concern that farmers have is: we're doing sustainable, but the others...? so that's an unsolved problem. i'd like your answer to that.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. i wish her well from this place. but politically i fundamentally disagree with her on her interference with national public service broadcasting. last thursday, i organised a hearing of the epp-ed on the commission's communication on public service broadcasting with colleagues ruth hieronymi and ivo belet. i can tell you that the attention of the industry and the member states was great. the directorate-general for competition was also present in large numbers. the subject is officially high on the agenda there. the director-general for competition was present as a panel member throughout the morning. the commission's proposal for a revision of the rules governing public service broadcasting is unacceptable in the present form. public service broadcasting plays a crucial role in protecting cultural diversity, linguistic diversity and media pluralism. i am very critical of the commission's plans. first of all, we must realize that public service broadcasting is a national matter and that it is therefore the national authorities, not the european commission, which should determine policy. this is also clearly stated in the protocol on public broadcasting to the amsterdam treaty. member states organise and finance public service broadcasting in their own way. the plans of commissioner kroes seriously undermine the principle of subsidiarity and the freedom of movement of the member states and replace it with european intervention. i am very surprised that she, as a liberal commissioner, should take this course. secondly, i am concerned about the proposal to have a preliminary market test carried out by an independent regulator. the commercial broadcasters have an important role to play in this. is that the point? a market test will in any case result in additional red tape. the question is whether the smaller broadcasting systems can get enough manpower and who will pay the extra costs. do the broadcasters have to pay for this? the taxpayer? this is not realistic in small member states. moreover, the test is very detailed; the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. the result will be that member states will no longer be able to follow the policies and procedures that are consistent with their national systems. it is also very doubtful whether the prior assessment of the market impact and public value of the new services does not give the commercial competitors too much of a head start. thirdly, i am concerned about the proposal because the public broadcasting systems are not organised in the same way in each member state. there is a diversity of technology and there are differences in broadcasting systems, organisation and scale. there are also differences in language and culture. the rule proposed in the report, 'one size fits all', certainly does not apply in this case. the proposal does not take this into account. after having expressed my concern, i would like to inform you of the epp hearing last thursday. the commission's attitude was very positive. the commission acknowledged that the proposals were perhaps too detailed and that the text needed to be adapted. the director-general informed me that the final communication could be postponed until after 5 march, the day on which the committee on culture will hold a second hearing on this subject. at the end of the hearing, the director-general said that the commission might have to draw up a new draft on the basis of this new information before it could come up with a final communication. that was a very good result. you will understand, mr president, that i am pleasantly surprised by these undertakings. this means that the commission is open-minded and ready to listen to the voice of the industry, the member states and parliament. - very good. i would like to ask the commission a few more questions. firstly, does the commission intend to maintain this detailed market test carried out by an independent regulator - a question which you might perhaps pass on to commissioner kroes - does the commission dispense with this independent regulator and this market test or does it intend to maintain it? my second question: how is technological neutrality guaranteed? does the text of the proposal not distinguish between the existing public service broadcasting and the new media services? for 80 years, mr president, public broadcasting has been the medium for informing the public in all its forms. the continuation of this broadcasting is possible through the use of public funds, which may be raised through a separate tax on listening to or watching radio and television. these funds may be supplemented by association contributions from members of broadcasting organisations, as is customary in the netherlands. in addition, in recent decades, public broadcasters have been encouraged by governments to start earning money themselves. this is done not only by advertising time but also by making available much of the information material collected by the broadcaster in the form of books and audiovisual material. it is expected that this will strengthen the link with viewers and listeners, reach new target groups and reduce costs for the public authorities. this task, which has been extended over the years, will not cause any problems as long as there is no commercial broadcaster who feels that his interests are being harmed. from the perspective of commercial broadcasters, the much older public service broadcaster can be seen as a competitor that is highly favoured by the fact that it can enjoy tax benefits. the question is whether the right of public service broadcasters to exist should no longer be derived primarily from their utility to society as a whole, but from the possible market distortion for commercial broadcasters. public interest and private interest no longer clash. this puts the european commission in the presence of conflicting wishes. the future of public service broadcasting is in jeopardy if its scope is restricted in favour of the profit margins of commercial companies. it is even worse when this happens outside this parliament and the explicit objections of 19 of the 27 governments of the member states are rejected. in recent years i have asked the commission on a number of occasions to abandon this heinous scheme. that seems to me the only possible solution, given the widespread concern that is now being aroused. moreover, it is the competence of the member states, not of the european union, that is at stake. i can agree with the questions and the views of mr visser and the other speakers.
mr president, it is clear that the issue at stake here is the tension between the public interest and its role in society against the impact on fair competition and the functioning of the internal market. the borders between public service broadcasting and private broadcasting, but also other media, are increasingly blurred and this is particularly the case when new media such as internet, ipods, sms etc. are at stake. as some people have said, public broadcasting is often the leading actor in innovation in services, but it is clear that this is an impact which is cross-border and across media sectors. therefore, it is important to have consistency between the broadcasting communication and the general framework of services of general economic interest. this is an important issue which we have dealt with in parliament’s committee on economic and monetary affairs. in particular, the pse group members on this committee have stressed that it is important to create more legal security for services of general economic interest, and we would like to see a framework directive and sector legislation on this decided at a political level. the consultation on the draft communication, which is now before us and has been presented by the commission, can be commented on until 15 january, and it is important to give a clear signal again that this more horizontal general framework would be an important tool to place discussions like this discussion on public broadcasting in a broader context. it is important to see who decides the rules and what balance exists in the rules between market interests and public interests. i have the implicit feeling that the market test mentioned in the communication should give primacy to market and commercial considerations instead of these public considerations. this was our intention with the new horizontal rules on services of general economic interest. i am a bit surprised that the ppe-de group is now focusing so much on these specific issues, whereas they were quite hesitant in supporting our appeal for the more general framework. here is a clear example – also in this case of broadcasting – that, if you do not have a broader clear framework which places more emphasis on and gives more dominance to the public interest aspects, it is always the market interest that will dominate in this type of discussion. i would like to take the opportunity to repeat our appeal for this framework of service of general economic interest, which, horizontally, should reinstate the balance between those interests in favour of the public interest.
mr president, public broadcasting represents pluriformity, diversity, cultural diversity in europe, the heart of democracy - i did not invent it myself, the commission says so too - and public broadcasting is of national interest. there are now plans to test the market in advance, particularly for new media activities. i do not think it is a good idea, because brussels should not want to dictate in advance how member states organise their public services. i believe that the public interest must be assessed at national level and that a mandatory market test is not necessary. i would also like to say something about the bureaucracy. i think it is good to check things, but we must also be careful not to create more rules, more costs for the various member states. furthermore, as regards the offer: i think we should not distinguish between old and new services, because in practice you also see that they are simply intermingled. it 's not one or the other; television programmes are often accompanied by new services and vice versa. so, like in europe, this is confusing. it is not just the market that is at stake, but also the public interest of our public service broadcasters, which everyone, including the commission, supports. i would like to hear from the commission whether this will be reflected in the adapted version of the communication and whether the responsibilities are laid down where they belong, namely with the member states, but are accompanied by rules of the game for services of general interest which should be drawn up in consultation with the european parliament and the council.
member of the commission. − mr president, i thank you all for your very fruitful discussion, which i will certainly convey to my colleague mrs kroes. let me comment on some of your remarks. the amsterdam protocol points to the need to balance financing with state aid and effects on competition. we want member states to take the lead on this, because otherwise we will have to do it in brussels on the basis of complaints. our aim is to give member states more possibilities to develop public service broadcasting, as long as the treaty provisions on competition are respected. public service broadcasting should continue to innovate, educate and, of course, entertain. the commission agrees with these goals. it is up to member states to decide on how to finance public service broadcasting. this is clearly stated in the amsterdam protocol. public broadcasters will be able to experiment freely with new projects. the draft communication makes clear that pilot projects are exempt from the tests. we have always recognised the possibility of providing funding for a public service mission, which includes a wide range of varied and balanced programming by public service broadcasters. this will also remain the case for the future. the commission also wants to guarantee a mixture of public and private. the commission aims at promoting public broadcasters to benefit from new technologies in order to serve the social, democratic and cultural needs of society. the amsterdam test allows member states to provide funding for new media services by public broadcasters. it protects the ability to keep up with changes in our fast-moving media environment. at the same time, it is also in the public interest to maintain the incentives for private operators. innovation by both public and private broadcasters is important for realising the lisbon objectives, and it goes without saying that public broadcasters will also remain free to launch any kind of new service on a commercial basis in the future without prior assessment. i want to assure you that we take the consultation very seriously. we will review the text to take into account legitimate concerns. i will ask our services to take parliament’s and member states’ proposals into account as we review the draft.

in writing. - explanation of vote on behalf of the cda delegation to the european parliament on the cappato report on access to documents the cda delegation in the european parliament abstained today in the vote on the cappato report on access to voting documents. not because we are against transparency. we are in favour of transparency and democratic control. it is not for nothing that former mep hanja maij-weggen was at the forefront of community legislation in this area. we abstained because the cappato report contains too many inaccuracies, incorrect wording and unsound statements. for example, we believe that the public access to council documents should be guaranteed, but there is a clear distinction between documents for legislative and other procedures. the rapporteur does not, however, distinguish between these procedures. we are also concerned about the high administrative burden that the recommendations of the cappato report would entail. we could not agree with the report because of the inaccuracies and vagueness. we did not vote in the end because we wanted to support the principle of transparency and democratic control.

we are talking tonight, mr president, about the eu's policy towards belarus, a policy which places democracy and respect for human rights at its core. i want to focus on one aspect: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the children are not allowed to leave the country, without, of course, prejudicing all the other relevant matters which have been raised this evening. you may be aware that children who were victims of the chernobyl disaster have been coming to the netherlands and other countries of the european union for years to recuperate for a while from the aftermath of this disaster. of course, these are children who were born long after the disaster - they are now about the same age as i was when the disaster happened 22 years ago - but they are still experiencing its effects on a daily basis, as evidenced by the statistics on thyroid disease, cancer and so on. each year, some 30,000 belarusian children are welcomed into 21 countries by host families, voluntary organizations and churches. in october 2008, news came that belarus would issue a decree to stop these children's travel and ban them from going abroad. so, off to christmas. thanks in part to pressure from the european union, the council of europe and a number of foreign ministers, including our dutch minister verhagen, that decree was temporarily lifted from 20 december to 20 january and a number of children were still able to go on holiday, but after 20 january this year nothing has been arranged. the european union has also expressed its support for the efforts of the belarusian authorities to ensure that the belarusian authorities do not continue to use the system of temporary suspension of the visa requirement. we would prefer to settle this for all the member states at once and not, as is the case now, through bilateral negotiations, 27 times over. we therefore call in our resolution on the czech presidency to negotiate with the belarusian authorities an eu-wide solution.
the name srebrenica has become world famous because of the mass murder of 8,000 muslim men in 1995. the survivors, women and young people, rightly call for continued attention to be paid to this. after my visit to srebrenica in march 2007, i asked the european commission to contribute to sustainable income and employment through projects to promote tourism, which would make srebrenica more than just a place of history and great memories. srebrenica is also a symbol of the failure of optimistic ideas about humanitarian interventions and safe havens. it should have been clear from the beginning that a foreign military presence could only provide false illusions. it made srebrenica a base of operations against the serbian environment, when it was inevitable that it would eventually be swallowed up by that same environment. without the dutch military force in srebrenica, there would have been no war situation and there would have been no need for revenge on the serbian side. the victims are not only a reason to put mladic and karadzic on trial, but also a reason to reflect critically on the failure of military interventions and of all attempts to establish a unified state in ethnically divided bosnia.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. a voice from srebrenica, big tears rolling down his cheeks. he hugged me, kissed me, and said, "please, mum, please go!" they grabbed him; i didn't want to go, so i got down on my knees and told them: "kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, kill me, what are you doing? you took my only child. i don't want to go anywhere. kill me and that will be the end of us". the moving account of a bosnian woman who lost both her husband and her 12-year-old son in the srebrenica massacre of july 1995. her voice and those of her fellow-sufferers are heard today, thanks in part to the invaluable research of conscientious scientists like amsterdam professor selma leydesdorff. the horrors of srebrenica remain a sign and a calling, first of all in word and deed, especially now that the european union is offering the western balkans a european accession perspective. in short, real attention to all the survivors of this genocide. srebrenica, july of '95. i was the foreign editor of a dutch newspaper at the time, and i followed the bosnian battlefield closely. how i felt ashamed and defeated by the hollow international concept of safe haven, especially as a dutch citizen. "come tonight with stories, how the war has disappeared, and repeat them a hundred times, every time i'll cry". the words of that famous poet from the memory culture of my country, the second world war, will henceforth accompany 11 july, the day of remembrance of the dear dead of srebrenica and potocari.
mr president, we are again discussing the transport of animals in this house and we will again conclude two things: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. yes, steps have been taken in the field of driver training, improved transport conditions and the use of gps, but it is certainly not enough. secondly, control remains the achilles heel of this legislation. the control from europe is lacking and is still very much organized at national level. there is therefore an urgent need for agreements on cross-border complaint handling and evidence gathering. i would also like to see more control by the food and veterinary office. an amendment of mine to provide more money for this in the budgetary procedure was rejected by the european commission, among others. i am also hearing the european commission speak about national reports which the commission will then assess from behind a desk. the european commission sometimes prefers to keep its hands clean rather than to ensure genuine european-wide controls, ad hoc controls by european inspectors and european supervision. other improvements to be made in the current legislation are: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's fishing fleet is able to compete with the other fleets in the world. despite the lack of a european overview of the actual implementation of this legislation, there are of course signs, for example from austria, where an on-the-spot inspector says: "i see a lot of empty trucks going to poland, the czech republic, for example. i just don't see them fully returning to southern europe". does this mean that when the wagons are full, they will simply take a detour around austria, where the controls may be stricter than in the surrounding countries? i think this is an indication that the member states are dealing with this legislation in quite different ways. another problem is the role of the veterinarians who have to sign for transport. in some cases, commissioner, these veterinarians have become stamping machines. a man of sound mind cannot sign for a transport from romania to southern italy that will take 24 hours. the last 300 miles [500 km] of this horse transportation would take 2.5 hours, according to the transportation plan. were those animals in a ferrari? finally, young animals, young dogs, for example, are being driven across europe today, for which nothing has yet been arranged. i would therefore call on the european commission to look into this as well. so enough of the house's homework and we look forward to the commission's proposals, which we do expect before the next european parliament elections.
we propose to delete the last sentence of paragraph 9, which reads: ‘calls on the belarusian authorities to end their practice of issuing exit visas to their citizens, in particular children and students’. we want to delete this because the authorities have already abolished this system of exit visas, so it is obsolete.
in writing. - i support the establishment of a day of commemoration for the genocide in srebrenica precisely because the actions of the eu and its member states have created a false sense of security, which has prevented the inhabitants from fleeing in time. those who support military intervention do not like to hear such criticism. last night my speech was hammered by the president, perhaps partly because of irritation over its content. the last part, which was hardly understood by the president's hammering, belongs in this report. srebrenica is also a symbol of the failure of optimistic ideas about humanitarian interventions and safe havens. it should have been clear from the beginning that a foreign military presence could only provide false illusions. it made srebrenica a base of operations against the serbian environment, when it was inevitable that it would eventually be swallowed up by that same environment. without the dutch military force in srebrenica, there would have been no war situation and there would have been no need for revenge on the serbian side. the victims are not only a reason to put mladić and karadžić on trial, but also a reason to reflect critically on the failure of military interventions and of all attempts to establish a unified state in ethnically divided bosnia.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the persecution of shirin ebadi is not an isolated incident. for years it has enjoyed a privileged position among the regime's critics. the existence of her human rights centre gave the impression that things are still going well in iran. most of the victims of the theocratic regime in iran are unknown. different political views, protests by discriminated ethnic and religious minorities, homosexuality and the struggle against the disadvantaged position of women are reasons for imprisonment or murder. many things that are normal in europe can cost you your life there. some victims are hung demonstratively from high cranes in the presence of a large crowd. nevertheless, the outside world, including europe, is not very much interested in this terrible situation and how it can be ended. international interest is much more focused on other issues. on the one hand, they concern the limitation of iran's military power and the prevention of the use of nuclear energy. on the other hand, the main emphasis is on the continued supply of oil and the maintenance and/or expansion of good trade relations. the result is that on the one hand there is a constant threat of war against iran, while on the other hand criticism is swallowed up of the lack of human rights. it even leads to buying the cooperation of the iranian regime by continually re-listing the main opposition in exile as terrorists. the council has to put an end to the bizarre situation whereby every time a court ruling declares the listing of a terrorist to be illegal, the council, on behalf of the european union, adopts a similar decision. unlike most other persecuted women in iran, shirin ebadi is not anonymous, but internationally known and respected. the nobel prize status has thus far sometimes afforded her a limited degree of freedom. the fact that this status is now ending shows the need for international support for those forces that seek change.
author. - mr president, on 15 february 2007 we discussed as a matter of urgency the state violence of the dictator lansana conté in guinea. this dictator came to power after a coup d'état and has been in power since 1984. he considered the land his private property, of particular interest because of its natural reserves of gold, iron and bauxite. most parties did not participate in the elections organised under his control. the official opposition, which was still represented in parliament for a time, was forced to leave parliament. this made the cntg and ustg the main force in the struggle for democracy. their demonstration on january 22, 2007 was met with the killing of 59 people and the wounding of 150 others. it was done by the presidential security forces, led by the dictator's son. this appalling regime came to an unexpected end last december with the death of the dictator. the junta has put a banker in charge as prime minister. the question now is what the military junta, which has since taken power, is paving the way for. is this a step towards democracy and equality for all the inhabitants, or does this new coup prepare the way for a new dictator who is once again mainly interested in the resources of the earth and the possibility of enriching himself with them? the outside world is reacting in confusion. the west african economic community (ecowas) has condemned the latest coup. the nigerian president praises the late dictator, but fortunately he does demand a swift transfer of power to a democratically elected government. france and senegal are also pressing for elections within a year. my group has always supported the demands of the democratic opposition in guinea over the years, which still seems to be out of the question. we do not condemn the change of power, but we do condemn the possible continuation of the lack of democracy in the near future. there is no reason to punish or isolate guinea, but to point out to the new powers that their action can only be temporary. the country does not need a new dictator, but the restoration of democracy.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem. after world war ii, when europe was gradually accepting that the independence of african countries was inevitable, kenya was explicitly excluded, as is present-day zimbabwe. the colonial administrators felt that there were too many foreign settlers and too many foreign economic interests in those countries to be left to the predominantly black inhabitants. unlike in west africa, kenya's independence was not achieved peacefully, but after a long and violent struggle by the mau mau independence movement. this need for violent struggle has laid the foundation for a continuation of violence and intimidation. the conquerors were mostly from one large tribe, the kikuyu. other groups of the population have always been kept in opposition, if necessary by means of falsified election results. the recent presidential elections have again shown that a non-kikuyu cannot become president, even if he is elected by a majority of voters. thanks to a compromise, the opposition candidate is now prime minister and internal peace seems to have been restored. while zimbabwe is seen as the country with the bad compromise, kenya was hailed as the country with the good compromise. for years, kenya was seen in western europe and america as a great success. it was the land of relative prosperity, freedom for international enterprises, friendship with the west and attention to tourists. now, kenya is no longer seen as a success. new tensions are being created by food shortages and a new press law. the president's lease of 40,000 hectares of agricultural land for the food supply of the oil-rich state of qatar in return for the construction of the port has contributed to this food shortage. the press law seems to be a tool used by the president to limit the power of the coalition government and to eliminate critical opposition. all the more so because that law was passed without even consulting the prime minister. the coalition compromise between the president and the prime minister is in jeopardy if the president is given the chance to bypass the prime minister, limit the role of the government and protect his own role from the critical press.
the doha negotiations have been going on for some time and it is good that europe has committed itself to making really big steps to bring us closer together. europe has put forward a far-reaching agricultural proposal, but unfortunately it has not yet been answered on an equal footing by the other countries. that is why it is so important to pay close attention to the whole package, including nama and the services. i would like to support your efforts to reach an agreement quickly. we must beware of protectionist measures which are increasingly coming to the fore, especially in the face of the deterioration of the world economy, but also in the name of protecting food security. we must, however, remain firmly committed to reciprocity in europe. we open it, they open it. what can we expect from the new american president and the package he has just launched? what can we expect from china? and that is where we particularly expect your commitment, because in times of economic and financial crisis with mass redundancies and shrinking economies, it is precisely this opening of markets that can take us further. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. you cannot give that answer in two minutes, nor in five or six. i totally get that. but i challenge you to be transparent in the future with the public about what you are doing and what it means to them. it is particularly important in the run-up to the european elections, and i hope that we can count on you in this respect.
author suppleant. − madam president, on behalf of the committee on international trade, i would like to set out what is at stake here. it is about production and employment in the textile and clothing sector in various european member states. the european union and china have agreed to a joint surveillance system relating to exports of certain categories of textile and clothing products from china to european member states, but this system expired on 31 december 2008. during the last two years, 350 000 jobs have been lost, and the number of companies was reduced by 5% during the same period. in the light of the growing number of enterprises that are ceasing to operate, or relocating production, leading to an increase of unemployment in several regions, i would like to put the following questions on behalf of the international trade committee: has the commission or any member state proposed or requested the extension of the dual surveillance mechanism beyond 31 december 2008 or any other measures in that framework? what measures does the commission intend to adopt to protect production and employment in the textile and clothing sector? will the commission continue to monitor real-time market developments, import statistics and customs monitoring and keep the sector informed of the latest developments? what is the current situation regarding the proposed regulation on ‘made in’ labels? what measures have been taken by the commission to address the proposals adopted by parliament in its resolution of 13 december 2007?
the report by mr laperrouze tackles the real problems of energy supply which the european union is facing today. the recent gas conflict between russia and ukraine has not left the european union untouched. the report sets out a number of guidelines for the european energy market which will reduce the european union's vulnerability in the event of a new conflict. i can therefore support the aim of a greater variety of energy sources and partner countries from which the eu imports energy, in short the necessary energy diversification. the gas dispute between russia and ukraine underlines this urgency once again, and i therefore sincerely hope that the nabucco project, for example, will be successful in the near future. in practical terms, this means that the european union and the member states should do more to promote regional integration. the networks of a number of member states are still too isolated and dependent on imports from third countries. we are also making the internal market work better by creating new links between the energy networks of the member states. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive. this is the best way of preventing asymmetric market opening. in the meantime, a number of member states are starting up nuclear power stations that have been closed as a result of agreements with the european union. this doesn't seem like the best way forward. rather, investing in more cross-border connections could reduce the dependency on one or a few third countries in a more sustainable way. other important policies in the report with which i fully agree are increasing energy efficiency and increasing the share of renewable energy. however, member states differ on whether nuclear energy should play a role in reducing co2 emissions. this is therefore a choice which the eu should leave to the member states. however, more clarity on this point would have been good for the report. i hope that amendment will improve this.
i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr angelilli, on this clear report. it is quite clear that the sexual exploitation of children must be stopped. the explosive growth of criminal activity via the internet calls for a coordinated approach. we must, however, be reluctant to go on at european level to prescribe detailed penalties for this abuse. the member states themselves will have to do their utmost to make misuse of modern media a criminal offence. child pornography on the internet will have to be regulated in the criminal law of the member states. the council should make an effort to close the loopholes in the legal net, following the example of the professional bans, so that exploitation and broadcasting via the internet do not move to countries where adequate legislation is lacking. i would like to hear from the council whether it also sees possibilities to discuss this with the countries which are not members of the european union, but unfortunately the council is not there. i also call for intensive cooperation between europol and eurojust. the fight against international child pornography networks must be given priority in their organisation. the possibilities for appropriate action outside the european union are thus increased. if we agree that the sexual exploitation of children is undesirable, i suggest that we also discuss the exploitation of adults. the initiatives to reduce prostitution around large-scale events such as football championships and the olympic games deserve more support in this parliament.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and gender equality. - i too would like to thank the rapporteur for his work, but i would also like to mention our shadow rapporteur on behalf of the epp group, mr simon busuttil, and the rapporteur on social affairs, mrs edit bauer. it is thanks to their efforts that the compromise text finally does what it is supposed to do, namely to remove the attraction of labour to illegal migration by effectively punishing workers who are staying in the european union illegally. contrary to what some parties in this parliament were initially trying to achieve, this legislation has not become an instrument for the regularisation of illegal residents. after all, the debate on legal migration is being held elsewhere. as rapporteur for the committee on women's rights, i believe that more attention should be paid to the gender issue in dealing with this group of people who are in the european union illegally and who are often victims - as has already been said - of exploitation. estimates of the number of third-country nationals illegally residing in the european union vary from 4.5 to 8 million. moreover, these estimates do not provide insight into the proportion of men and women in that total, nor do they provide insight into the gender-specific problems faced by illegal female migrants. i would point out that illegal immigrant women are very vulnerable and that they are often the group that is the victim of forced labour, trafficking and violence. this requires specific training for the authorities responsible. but as always, it's about control. i am glad that the arbitrary 10% control that was first proposed is now off the table and that it is a risky approach in the compromise text. the fact that this is not a far-from-my-bed show is proved by the reports in the belgian newspaper 'de standaard' last summer about domestic slaves in certain embassies in brussels, a stone's throw from the berlaymont building. i would therefore say to the european commission: the proof of the pudding is in the eating. take responsibility for looking critically at how countries deal with this legislation and whether they are effectively implementing it!
on behalf of the alde group the european union has been struggling for years with the presence of millions of illegal immigrants on its territory and the pressure to find a solution is growing. the pressure is great and justified, to be honest. if we are to have a manageable and reliable migration policy, where the refugee has the right to protection and where we create clear opportunities for legal migration, then tackling all the attractive and repulsive factors that lead to illegal immigration is a necessary condition. last summer the council and parliament reached agreement on the so-called 'return directive', which is aimed primarily at the illegal immigrant himself. today, attention is focused on the employer who does not shy away from employing illegals. this is not only a major attraction for illegal immigration, but it also creates undeniably abusive and grossly exploitative situations. the importance of a harmonized european approach is beyond doubt in my view. in the absence of internal borders, member state a can worry endlessly, but if member state b then fails to tackle the employment of illegal workers, or does so only with a limited degree of care, member state a will end up being a voice in the wilderness. after a somewhat slow start, after intensive negotiations with the council, a compromise acceptable to me is now on the table and i thank the rapporteur for his constructive and pragmatic approach. it was a relief. i cannot say the same about some of his colleagues who now insist on the accompanying written statement, a show for the stage which does not do the image of this parliament any good. but my group will accept that too. it should also be clear that, after the adoption of this directive, the member states will be in charge. it should be clear that neither the european commission nor the european parliament has a magic wand when it comes to action, when it comes to enforcement. the council did not want to hear of a binding percentage for inspections, and that is precisely what is often lacking - as has been said by several members of this parliament and by the commissioner. all i can say is: i am not saying that we should not be able to do this, but i am saying that we should not be able to do this. it's not good for anybody. the proposal is another step in the right direction towards the establishment of a comprehensive migration policy. but we are far from there, so we must move on now. i therefore ask the question of the day: why is the council not present at this debate? i think that is unacceptable, to be honest.
it is another two years since commissioner frattini announced plans to tackle illegal work, illegal work that is leading to a continuous flow of illegals to work in the european union. it's inhumane and it has to be stopped. however, madam president, i do not agree with the rapporteur on the question of whether the european union should intervene in criminal law. i am not in favour of making criminal law a european competence. this is where a method of open coordination is needed. i therefore welcome the fact that the amended compromise proposal is reserved about the application of criminal law. the financial sanctions provide a sufficient incentive to be careful in the choice of employees. i hope that the inspections of companies will encourage the member states to take action under criminal law.

president obama kept his word. one of his first acts was to close the prison at guantanamo and of course we are very happy about that. it was, rather, the announcement of the closure of guantánamo, because the new american government is faced with the difficult task of finding a solution for the remaining prisoners. first, their status must be established and whether they can all be released without risk. they have been illegally detained by the bush administration in clear violation of international law. the first is that the problem is american and must be solved by washington. whether the us will seek help from the european countries and the european union is not clear at this stage and to anticipate too much is somewhat speculative. i also disagree with colleagues who think that we should make commitments now on the assumption that the european union is morally obliged to do so because some countries may have been involved in the transport of prisoners to guantánamo. that's based on conjecture. we have never been able to prove that this actually happened and we do not know which countries may have been involved in these transports. so i think that our argument for taking in prisoners is somewhat weak and i am also glad that this is not the case in the resolution. of course, we must not rule out the possibility that the americans will make this request after all, and i agree with my group's chairman. if that request is made, we will certainly have to respond positively to it, for reasons of international law, but also because of the need for the european union to respond in a humanitarian manner in such a situation.

for the pse group first of all, i would like to express my sincere thanks to my colleague, karl-heinz florenz. with great effort and determination he wrote his report. i think it has become a complete report with almost 200 recommendations and really worthwhile. it contains many good recommendations which can be a source of inspiration for the next parliament, national parliaments and local authorities. i shall mention five main lines which are really essential for our group. first of all, this report recognises that climate change is a multifaceted issue. climate change affects all sectors, not just industry, transport and energy, for which we have already set rules, but also agriculture, health, science and technology, ict, education, soil, water and land use. all these issues deserve our full attention and effective action. the second main line is that we believe that climate policy must also be social and based on solidarity. we must take account of employment, income and energy poverty. we need to know how and by whom the new technology will be financed. we want to know how many jobs are being created and whether jobs are being lost. we want retraining programmes for the new green poll workers. without a clear social commitment, it is extremely difficult to maintain political support for climate policy. our third main line is the economic crisis. here too we need an integrated approach. the green new deal has become a household word. we wholeheartedly support this. it's going to take a lot of investment. aid operations to banks and companies must have at least a sustainable component. investments in housing and apartment buildings in eastern europe should be given greater priority. that is good for employment, good for energy security and good for the climate. our fourth main point concerns agriculture. we don't usually talk about that when we talk about climate. today we are showing that it is necessary and it has also proved to be a controversial subject. we have had a long discussion about whether agriculture should have binding targets and we agree that we should seriously consider this. it is also clear that agriculture is not only a problem but also a solution. good agriculture, good land use and good use of biomass can contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gases. our fifth and final point is to involve people. this requires information and transparency. if we want to change our consumption behaviour, we need to know exactly which products are responsible for a lot of greenhouse gases and then we may have to change our consumption patterns. it is not easy, of course, but the problem of climate change - as karl-heinz florenz has just said - cannot be solved by a few technical maneuvers. we will do our utmost to involve as many people as possible in the great challenges we face. local initiatives are very valuable in this respect. free energy audits for homes, better public transportation, local and regional food production. together we can achieve a great deal. ladies and gentlemen, this package of measures gives us cause for optimism. the commission has also proposed that the european union should be able to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, which will contribute to innovation, economic growth, improved energy supply, improved food production, more employment and a more stable climate. i thank all the colleagues and staff who have contributed to this.
on behalf of the ind/dem group it may be an unfortunate time to talk about global warming now, when much of western europe is experiencing a very cold winter. however, as the title of the report indicates, we are looking at the long term in this debate. so we must not be too distracted by short-term events. the final report by mr karl-heinz florenz has been a well thought-out and thorough report, with which we as the european parliament are sending a clear signal. to limit or prevent the negative effects of climate change, strong action is needed in a wide area. hard targets for the period between 2020 and 2050 are needed. no segment of society should be exempt from efforts. in the run-up to the copenhagen climate conference, we will have to maintain our ambition, accompanied by a wide range of measures. this report makes a very valuable contribution to this.
i have had the pleasure of working with mr florenz and all the others on the temporary committee. we have thus laid the foundations for a more integrated and ambitious policy in the future, based on a broad consensus, including here in parliament in the context of the twenty-twenty-twenty objective. the answer lies in greening the economy and making business, households and government more sustainable. businesses seeking support for such an approach - developing sustainable initiatives for green technology - still face a number of obstacles. if they want qualifications for an employee, they run into the policy of the narcotics. the report calls for an integrated approach, including territorial coherence. if you don't, you'll be more expensive in the long run. my amendment on the involvement of regions and cities has fortunately been adopted. next week 150 cities will sign a covenant of mayors with the commission in the parliament buildings. they are taking up the conclusions of this report, close to the citizens and close to the companies. i think that's a good approach. so, support me on this. i do object to the unqualified approach to the agricultural sector in paragraph 189. the epp is right not to be against the consumption of meat. we shall therefore oppose this paragraph. finally, i ask the commission to continue to work more integratedly in the coming period and to use much less sugar. in cooperation with parliament, a major breakthrough can be achieved by combining legislation, incentive policies and decentralized action. there's a lot of profit to be made at that point.
the pvda delegation supports the aim of this directive, which is to make illegal employment a criminal offence, on the one hand to combat illegal work as one of the attractive factors of illegal migration, and on the other hand to prevent and combat the exploitation of migrants. despite some positive elements in this compromise, we felt compelled to vote against it for a number of reasons. first, there was liability from the whole chain down to the main contractor. unfortunately, this was lost in the compromise between the council and parliament. it is now limited to the first link in the outsourcing or subcontracting process. this is counterproductive and encourages further investment and outsourcing to avoid social responsibility. there are also insufficient guarantees that migrants are protected and that workers who make mistakes are punished. migrants are not given the right to receive back pay before being deported, nor the time to wait for the money inside the european union. the chances of this working after deportation are slim to none. an illegal who is a victim of exploitation and wants to fight back is virtually helpless.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the final vote on this report. but this does not prevent me from having serious objections to certain parts. i do not think that the cultivation of fodder crops for intensive livestock farming is detrimental to the climate. nor do i think that there should be a european soil directive on climate change.
for the greens/efa group it took almost a year for eulex to be able to carry out its original mandate. it is good to recall today what that original mandate looked like. the mandate included that eulex, as i said, the largest european mission to date, would be deployed, active throughout kosovo, north and south of the ibar river. it also meant that eulex would take the lead in three areas: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. let alone eulex becoming active and this leading to the country being effectively divided in two, between north and south. none of this was meant to be. for a long time it seemed that the original mandate could not be carried out because of the well-known deadlock in the security council. in fact, it has only been since november last year that it looks like it will work. it is therefore good to see it working now - two or three months after it has actually started - or, let me be careful, that it seems to be working. the kosovo police are pleased with the excellent cooperation with the people of eulex. the customs finally work again, also and especially in the northern part of kosovo, especially at those customs posts that were burned down by kosovo serbs last year. finally, the start has been made to tackle the backlog in a number of cases of inter-ethnic violence and corruption, which once again shows that eulex's activities are in the interest of all communities, not just albanians and not just serbs. what i really hope is that this development of eulex in the last few months will continue in a positive way. i also really hope that serbia, that belgrade, will realise that the current way of constructive cooperation with the european union is much, much more effective than continuing to try to rewrite history. i also hope, and above all, that the kosovo authorities will finally succeed in solving the great pile of problems that still remains. finally, we must tackle the corruption and organized crime which are still far too strong in kosovo. finally, ensuring a sustainable energy supply based on eu rules and finally, getting the kosovo economy off the ground. kosovo is an independent state and whether everyone in this parliament likes it or not, that situation will not be reversed. we, the european union, have an interest in kosovo developing into a viable state. that is why we are there and why we must remain there.
the european parliament has always been a party to the treaty of rome, and it has always been a party to the treaty of rome. i did not get the impression that these different levels always worked together equally well. the european institutions must not shrug their shoulders at this. we're in it together. through the eulex mission, europe is responsible for the situation on the ground. eulex should be more assertive and provide advice and assistance to the kosovo authorities, whether requested or not. i would like to stress two points. first of all, i call on those member states which have not yet recognised kosovo to reconsider this decision. there is no way back for kosovo within the borders of serbia. secondly, i am in favour of a master plan for the western balkans. it is a matter of working with all the countries at a concrete level to prepare them for accession to the european union. that is europe's commitment to the western balkans.
since kosovo declared independence almost a year ago, the european union has been hopelessly divided on the issue. greece has doubts, while spain, romania, slovakia and cyprus reject this independence for internal reasons. the eulex joint project, which the european union hopes to use to gain influence in kosovo, seems to be a means of hiding these internal divisions. eulex may be good for the european union, but is it also good for kosovo? the people of kosovo are interested in early accession to the european union to be an equal member within it. after almost a century of subjugation to serbia, they do not want any new, additional interference from outside. a project like eulex might have played a useful role in preventing possible chaos in the first few months of 2008. that phase is over. the late arrival of eulex now gives the impression that the european union is seeking to turn kosovo into a protectorate with a military presence and administrative influence, as was unsuccessfully done in bosnia-herzegovina. the peaceful and harmonious future of kosovo requires involvement not only of the current government and the ruling parties. the main forces are the self-determination movement vetëvendosje in the south, which sees the european union's actions as useless colonialism, and the representatives of the serbs in the municipalities north of the ibar river, who are doing everything to remain permanently linked to serbia. without these critics of eulex, there will be no lasting solution. a good future for kosovo requires widely accepted internal compromises rather than a demonstration of power by the european union.
first of all, i would like to congratulate mr lagendijk on the very balanced resolution he has adopted. i would also like to thank doris pack, who is doing a lot of good work in the area with her delegation. the objectives of the european union are clear: we cannot allow kosovo to become a black hole. the responsibility for this lies first and foremost with the kosovo authorities. the people must have confidence in the administration, but also in the judicial system. corruption and crime are undermining the state. women and minorities must also be able to participate fully. a second responsibility, i believe, lies with the neighbouring countries and, in particular, with the serbian authorities. constructive dialogue and regional cooperation are in the interest of everyone in the region. finally, the european union also has a great responsibility. the european union has invested heavily in eulex. it is good that the real work has now begun. the next two years will have to show whether eulex can continue to make a real difference in the long term. i hope so. stability, reconciliation and the development of the rule of law in kosovo are of great importance to the kosovars and all ethnic minorities in kosovo, but they are also in the interest of the european union. the effectiveness of aid must come first. i was with mrs. pack not long ago. there is no shortage of help, but it could perhaps be increased and coordinated even more.

i would also like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr graefe zu baringdorf. the liberal group also believes that the compromise reached is a good compromise and we can therefore vote in favour of it later today. the most important thing is that the intellectual property of the feed manufacturers is guaranteed. if we want to have innovation in the sector - we voted yesterday on a climate report - then there is a lot that can be improved in the area of animal feed, so that the emissions of all kinds of gases from livestock are reduced. now, that challenge is ours to take. if feed manufacturers are to innovate, they must not be hindered by overly strict rules on disclosure. i think that this disclosure is guaranteed, as others have said. it could be at the request of the government or of a particular customer. nothing prevents the feed manufacturers from doing this on a voluntary basis. it is not the most important thing to know which ingredients are what they are. far more important is the precise nutritional value of animal feed. this must be indicated correctly. everything on the label, energy, protein, etc., is important. in short, the liberal group will support this compromise. we will also say to the feed manufacturers that if they want to renew their production processes and their feed composition, etc., this compromise is a good compromise which encourages this.
i would like to add my voice to all the compliments already addressed to our rapporteur. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on animal feed are applied in a uniform manner. this means clear rules on the use and labelling of raw materials. the whole thing must of course remain workable and not lead to more administrative burdens or jeopardize - as has already been said - the intellectual property of the producers. we will continue to monitor this critically, but it seems that the new regulation meets these requirements. i will make a note of that, and that is in the area of control. again, this legislation is subject to proper control and sanctions to separate the wheat from the chaff. the regulation states that the penalties which member states may impose on themselves must be effective, proportionate and dissuasive. i call on the european commission to take a critical look at whether this is the case in all the countries of the european union in the future. it is not possible for one country to be more strict than another. i think that the first case in point are the suspended sentences handed down this week, ten years after the belgian dioxin scandal. and finally, a subject which cannot, of course, be left untouched here: the animal meal. after the emotional speech of the first speaker of the unenrolled, i shall confine myself to the facts. animal meal in animal feed has been banned since the bse crisis. in the case of chickens, for example, this leads to a shortage of animal protein in the feed, apart from the justified criticism of the destruction of valuable proteins. in addition, animal feed is the main cost item for the five million farmers in the european union, who are already struggling. of course we do not want to go back to a situation where certain types of animal protein end up in the food. cannibalism, that's it, never again! but to deal with it properly, tests are needed. the european commission has indicated that these tests could be available in 2009, so that animal meal can be safely fed to, for example, chickens or pigs. i would like to hear from the european commission what progress has been made in this area and what steps we can expect to take in the coming year.

in writing. - for almost 20 years, this parliament has been a spectator and commentator on the slowly progressing parting of kosovo from serbia. discussions about kosovo here are usually not about kosovo itself, but mainly about the consequences for the outside world. the main arguments put forward by those in favour of and those against independence were that each decision would set a precedent for other areas and that the european union would thereby be able to make itself more important. i have been advocating the opposite for some 30 years, both outside and inside this parliament. democracy requires that we look first and foremost at the needs and desires of the people themselves. they want to see the end of centuries of turkish rule and the last century of serbian rule, and they want to see the end of external coercion. if they cannot join albania, they want real independence. i have been here for the last 10 years advocating a bottom-up politics, seen from the perspective of the poor, the marginalized, the people who suffer from a lack of democracy and public services, the victims of environmental disasters or wars, in short, all the people who are disadvantaged by a lack of equality for all people. i am voting against the eulex project because it does not seek solutions in the interests of the ordinary people of kosovo.

- i'm the reporter. - i am also glad that mr deva has taken his seat, because now mrs ferrero-waldner can simply listen to me, i am really relieved. the unprecedented economic crisis that is gripping us is a new disaster for developing countries. the crisis is leading to falling commodity prices, less investment, less trade credit, less money being sent home by migrants. the gross national product of every prosperous country is falling, and this is also reducing the budget for development cooperation, because it is 0.7% of gross national product, or at least it should be, and most countries are not keeping that promise. this is the context of the discussion on the new policy instrument that is being discussed here. when spanish students study in latin america for a few months on a scholarship and vice versa, such an exchange is useful, necessary and desirable, but such a project cannot be financed exclusively with money earmarked for poverty reduction. it is fine that eu money is going to it, but poverty reduction is not. it's frustrating to scrap these kinds of projects just because there's no legal way to support them. we have therefore sought a modest instrument which would enable the eu to implement policies in developing countries which are not strictly poverty-fighting. we must find another source of funds, another legal basis than development policy. the legal basis cannot therefore be found in article 179 of the treaty of nice, because that is precisely the legal basis of development policy and it must be avoided here. article 179 does not allow the interests of the eu itself - european students who travel for study purposes - to be financed. in addition, the eu must legally comply with the criteria for development cooperation, namely poverty reduction, when spending development money. with a little creativity, there are other sources. the extension of the industrialized countries instrument is a possibility proposed by the committee on foreign affairs and also supported by my committee. but what is also possible is a combination of articles 150, 151 and 170, education, culture and research. with this combined legal basis, the european parliament has full co-decision power over this instrument and the money, some eur 13 million at present, is not coming from the development envelope. it is not coming from the foreign policy envelope either. i cannot as rapporteur - and the committee on development and cooperation supports me in this - agree with article 179 as a legal basis. this new instrument would be useless, since its aim is precisely to avoid the use of development funds for other purposes. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i therefore urge the epp group to withdraw its amendment. it is contrary to our shared desire to protect the development cooperation budget, even in times of economic crisis.

in writing. − nato played an important role during the cold war between 1949 and 1989. its official task was to defend the common territory of the member states against foreign invasion, without any action of its own outside that territory. the justification was the defence of a pluralist democracy against dictatorship, but in practice it was mainly about protecting a capitalist economy against a socialist economy. the right-wing dictatorships in portugal and greece were allowed to participate, bilateral agreements existed with a similar dictatorship in spain and the french colony of algeria was forced to belong to nato territory until 1962. with the disappearance of the soviet union and the warsaw pact, that nato has lost its raison d'être. its continued existence poses a problem for the eu because six member states choose military neutrality. the current nato is still more a 'coalition of the willing' around the usa than an extension of the eu. this year, on the occasion of nato's 60th anniversary, we shall have to decide what role nato will claim in the future. if it is the world's policeman, carrying out its own projects apart from the united nations with the aim of benefiting the most economically powerful states, then this organization is harmful and unnecessary. that is why i am against it.
in writing. the mediterranean is the southern border of the eu. for many years, the eu has been seeking close cooperation with non-eu coastal states, some of which will never be able to join the eu. this cooperation is considered important in southern europe but is of much less importance for the rest of europe. however, there are no deep differences of opinion on this because no one rejects good relations at the external borders. the commission has recently taken steps, particularly at the initiative of france, to further intensify this cooperation, including the setting up of separate permanent structures. the question is what is the purpose of that. is it an attempt to get closer to the dictatorships in syria and libya, which are constantly violating human rights? is it an attempt to find an alternative to the full eu membership promised in the long term to the riparian states of croatia, montenegro, albania and turkey? is it a means of further strengthening ties with israel, despite the fact that, as a result of the election result on 10 february, israeli cooperation in the creation of a palestinian neighbouring state with equal rights cannot be expected for the next four years? i cannot support this proposal for the time being.
