traditions – something which is a very important aspect of co-determination. we do not want ‘shopping around’ to exist and a new delaware to be created in the european union, where companies can circumvent this type of legislation. thirdly, regarding the fourteenth directive – which we know mr mccreevy is not very much in favour of – you repeated the same arguments he put forward. he should take seriously the fact that parliament really wants him to present this proposal. fourthly, we tabled a resolution together to ask the commission to consult the social partners on the basis of article 138. this would have been proper before we decided on a regulation concerning the spe, because co-determination is an issue which is dealt with under article 138 of the ec treaty and which first requires a consultation of social partners. this has not been done. we now ask for it to be done immediately afterwards. however, if i hear this type of reaction from the commission, i will seriously reconsider and suggest to my group that we reconsider whether we can support the spe product. perhaps you should make some phone calls tonight to mr mccreevy and mr špidla and see whether tomorrow you can present a better proposal on this before the vote. briefly – because i am now using up my speaking time – the pse’s position is not against the chance for small and medium-sized enterprises to get this new statute – we are in favour of that – but it is against this form of abuse. we found a very good compromise with the rapporteur. it is also very up-to-date and is not at all obsolete, but very topical at this time, considering the disastrous workings of shareholders and financial markets, to consider a better way of governance for companies, in which employees have a part. commissioner, you really have to consider with your college and with the commissioners in charge whether you can make a more positive statement to the parliament.
madam president, it is a pity that mr toubon is leaving the room, because i wanted to respond to an accusation he made concerning our compromise: namely that it requires new heavy burdens on smes. in fact, in the compromise, the status quo is being resettled for mergers, divisions of companies and transfers of seats. regarding new companies starting from scratch, there is a fairly high threshold for companies with fewer than 500 employees. those with between 500 and 1 000 employees have exactly what is in the other directives. one cannot say that this is making the status quo even heavier; it is making it lighter. in this consultation with the social partners, we want to look at simplification and a better system which is harmonised all over the eu.
- i'm the reporter. - the report by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on budgetary control, on the implementation of the eighth directive on statutory audits of annual accounts; it is good that parliament is paying more attention to the implementation and transposition of legislation adopted here. we can agree on the most fantastic rules here, but if they are not implemented and respected in the member states, we are sitting here doing our work for nothing. the transposition of the eighth directive has also been marred by many failures. central definitions are interpreted differently and there is no independent public oversight in all states. the report calls on the commission to take swift action to correct these shortcomings. we also wonder whether the harmonisation method of minimum harmonisation is the right one to achieve a uniform application of key concepts. perhaps we should start thinking about whether, when it comes to these kinds of central definitions, we should not choose another method of harmonization which is not open to many interpretations. finally, mr president, the report deals with the commission's recommendation on the limitation of the civil liability of auditors. if further measures are needed to achieve convergence in europe, we welcome the commission's proposals. the objective must and will remain that we strive for effective and independent auditing. the economic crisis has taught us that strict control and supervision are indispensable.
i would like to thank mr schwab for his efforts, because he was simply put against the wall during the trialogue. in the days when i was studying european law, codecision was clearly something different from what it is today. the first reading agreement which is now in place and which has been negotiated by four colleagues - including myself as shadow rapporteur - is very different from the text adopted by parliament. it is different from what the commission has proposed, and the economic crisis has been used to make a very bad proposal. everyone complains that it is too little, and yet it seems to be accepted. we in the liberal group believe that democracy and the role of parliament are being eroded. if the text adopted by parliament at first reading has not been incorporated into the trialogue negotiations, then a second reading will eventually be necessary, because that is how it works. we also think it is a bad sign that if four members of the trialogue agree to it, then the text is also supported by everyone in this house. the liberals will vote against this proposal on grounds of principle. this will be mainly for procedural reasons and not because we think the content is bad; it could be a lot better.
president. - mr president, our industry is important for the economy, but it also often leads to pollution of the air, water and soil. the european union has been tackling this form of environmental pollution for decades, and recently the focus has been on co2 and climate change in environmental policy. sometimes it seems that this is the only environmental problem. al gore has clearly left his mark. the danger, however, is that environmental problems are reduced to climate change. however, we face many more challenges in environmental policy. the creation of jobs is also threatened by the emission of air pollutants, 40 to 80% of which are caused by large industrial installations. the ippc directive is therefore a good way of protecting the environment. we have discussed the proposals in detail in the committee on the environment and have reached an acceptable compromise on many points. i do not think the report is good enough on the emission standards for large combustion plants alone. this is where the greatest environmental benefits are to be found. i have therefore tabled a few additional amendments. for example, the emission standards for power stations should be about as strict as those for waste incineration plants, especially when we consider that power stations also incinerate waste. in addition, i propose to close a loophole in the law. gassing, which is similar to ordinary combustion, must meet the same emission standards. recent case law has shown that this is not the case. the legislation must therefore be reformed.
the new ippc directive requires companies to equip all large installations with the best available technology. in some countries this has already been made mandatory, and it is now time to draw a line and create a level playing field. so that's fine. but, ladies and gentlemen, i ask you in particular to support the amendments which lead to future requirements for coal-fired power stations. there should be an emission ceiling for coal-fired power stations, either by using ccs or by using 40 to 50% renewable biomass. a cap on co2 emissions is the only way we can both continue to use coal and meet our climate goals. some colleagues object to the fact that the electricity sector is covered by the new trading system and that new requirements are therefore unnecessary. but that is not the case, because the ets allows for exceptions for coal-fired power stations and also allows energy-intensive industry to recoup the higher cost of more expensive electricity. so the price incentive to reduce co2 is gone. if you think the ets will solve everything, you don't need to make demands on cars, you don't need to ban light bulbs and you don't need a directive on renewable energy. we have to be consistent. if we have to make demands on cars and lighting, then we should certainly make demands on coal-fired power stations, because they are the real polluters. it is also a fact that easy co2 reductions are not to be found in the steel or cement industry, but in the energy sector.
in writing. - today i voted against the proposal by mr catania on the future of a common european asylum policy. my party, the socialist party of the netherlands, does not believe that harmonization of asylum policy and the creation of an agency to deal with it in the future will lead to a more equal distribution of the number of asylum applications across the various member states. asylum applications are more likely to be determined by the presence of family members and acquaintances in certain member states, which attracts new asylum seekers. i also believe that harmonisation will lead to a lower quality of asylum policy in countries where it is currently relatively well regulated, because member states will use the drawdown to seek the lowest possible level. such a race to the bottom is undesirable and will ultimately affect only the asylum seekers. as much as i appreciate the efforts of mr catania, i cannot support his conclusions here.
in writing. - this afternoon we voted on the statute for the european private company. i voted against the proposal in the end for the following reasons: firstly, i believe that this proposal increases legal uncertainty in the european union. the relationship between the national bv and the european bv, between the applicable national law and the text of the regulation is not sufficiently clear. how is circumvention of useful national legislation prevented? how does the proposal relate to consumer protection? such questions are not answered satisfactorily. we have also voted today on another report by mr lehne. in this report, it makes recommendations for improving the transfrontier relocation of company headquarters. i think this is a much better idea than the european company idea. if the commission were to make efforts to make crossborder seat transfers easier, with less red tape, the whole proposal would be superfluous for the european company.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the safety of ships. this is largely due to the fact that you, commissioner tajani, together with your officials and we as the european parliament have set out to persuade the council to really get serious about a number of crucial points which were so important to parliament. because we must have european cooperation to improve maritime safety. we have now fortunately closed a number of missing links, although we as a parliament would have liked to have gone a step further on a number of points. it is a good thing that in the case of major accidents at sea, independent accident investigation has now become mandatory, because in this way the cause of accidents can really be traced. the airline industry has a lot of experience with that. last week a turkish airlines plane crashed in the netherlands near amsterdam and within a week, thanks to an independent accident investigation, the cause was discovered and speculation put to an end. independent accident investigation, independent of the question of fault; it's really a win that we now have that for shipping. the rapporteur has done an excellent job on this point. i would like to highlight another point in this package, mr president, namely that there should be better regulation of liability for passengers at sea. it is good that inland waterway transport is not included, because small inland vessels cannot be treated in the same way as seagoing vessels. it's not the same. and it is good that this has been confirmed in the proposal.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. mr president, commissioner, our rapporteur, the honourable mr saïd el khadraoui, began by saying something reassuring. no european tax is to be imposed. no, mr rapporteur, it is a question of the rules of the game for the internal market, which are intended to make sustainable transport more possible in europe. but the position you choose as rapporteur gives member states a free hand to pay very high taxes, up to a few euros per kilometre. the congestion charge on top, the mark-up on top. the commissioner has said that this is not a matter of rules for the internal market, but of obstacles to the internal market. the ppe group would like to make its position clear here today. we are strongly in favour of investing in sustainable transport. the transfer of external costs for air pollution and noise pollution can therefore be counted on our approval if the proceeds - and this is widely supported - are also invested in making road transport cleaner. but the proposed congestion charge and mark-up is a bridge too far for the ppe-de. a congestion charge has a very limited environmental impact, while congestion is not being solved and the congestion charge is creating an extra burden, an extra burden for smes in this time of economic crisis, and that is bad for employment. the rapporteur has reached a compromise with the liberals by linking up with passenger transport, which was crucial to winning their support. the council - and this is already clear - will not accept that. so that puts the rapporteur in a position which he himself would like to see, but the liberals do not. i therefore want to end on behalf of the ppe-de group with an old saying: "we may be losing this battle, but that doesn't mean we've lost the war".
the abolition of customs frontiers and exchange rates within europe has led to an expansion of the economy. the government is providing a dense network of ever-widening highways. many goods are now moved over long distances, sometimes even at different stages of the production process. these developments have negative effects on the environment. the cost of these shall not be borne by the transport undertaking. this has helped to make freight transport cheaper over the years. this again calls for more transport and further increases the environmental burden. in the almost ten years that i have been a member of this parliament, there has been much talk of attributing this environmental damage to traffic, but unfortunately it has not yet been sufficiently successful. sometimes it seems that politicians are more interested in computational models than in the results we can achieve with them. previous decision-making on this issue in 2006 yielded too little. as far as my group is concerned, the aim must be to give more opportunities to the most environmentally friendly modes of transport by rail and water and to curb the most environmentally harmful modes by road and by air. in the absence of such a clear objective, calculation models and more european rules will only create red tape and will not benefit people and the environment. my country, the netherlands, is an example of how not to do it. there has been a debate for almost 20 years about charging for distance travelling. the debate has now come to a complete standstill. the only impression left on the voters is that congestion is being taxed without that perspective, that the problems are solved by improving railways and public transport. europe must not repeat the mistakes of the member states. we must remove all obstacles to regional and national measures, ensure that they are well coordinated, improve cross-border toll payments and better inform professional drivers about what measures they can expect outside their own area of residence. the proposals of the rapporteur, mr el khadraoui, provide the means for this and are therefore supported by my group. the rapporteur, mr jarzembowski, also calls for special attention to be paid to noise pollution from rail freight traffic. we agree with him, but at the same time i would point out that the construction of ever higher noise barriers along the railway tracks cannot be the solution.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i am quite satisfied with the result and very satisfied with the cooperation with the rapporteur. it is good that member states are given the opportunity to pass on the external costs to the polluter. let us not hesitate to pass the costs of traffic jams, air pollution and noise pollution on to the consumer. i do believe that member states should retain the room for variation in costs. in addition, the charges imposed must be clearly and transparently traceable to the actual costs. we cannot allow member states to introduce a kind of criminal tax. amendment no 40 will therefore have to be deleted from the report. i would also like to hear from the council whether it agrees with parliament that the proceeds of the eurovignette should be used to reduce external costs. this is a key point for me to be able to agree to the eurovignette directive. the commission has already made a number of proposals. trucks are not the only cause of congestion on the road. other modalities also generate external costs. with the exception of maritime and air transport, which is covered by an emissions trading system, these carriers will also have to pay their external costs. we are thus fairly encouraging cleaner transport companies. i should also like to take this opportunity to ask for support for my amendment no 76. i do not understand why the commission has decided to change the old text of the administrative charge on all urban roads. the imposition of such administrative charges is a national competence. it is for the member states to determine the roads on which they wish to impose such an administrative charge, provided, of course, that it is non-discriminatory. i therefore propose to reinstate the old 2006 text and ask for your support. finally, i would point out that it is of the utmost importance that the commission ensures that member states do not use this directive to impose unjustifiably high charges on freight transport. the commission must take its task under article 11 (1) very seriously. if a member state goes too far in the calculation of the external-cost charge or the maximum value, the commission must take strong action against that member state.
the only reason, mr president, - at least for me - to take this proposal seriously at all is that it creates a european framework within which the member states can operate, which ultimately benefits the internal market. the green paper is a good example of this. the sectoral driving bans in austria are a good example of this. we've come a long way to get here. however, some sensitive points remain the subject of debate. let me be clear: the possibility of a congestion charge for road freight transport is unacceptable to me, and i also regard the removal of the so-called ear tags as a no-go. my colleague dirk sterckx said it before: if it turns out on second reading that there is no majority in this house and in the council for these views, the liberal proposal is off the table. road freight transport is an important engine of our economy. let us not lose sight of this fact - and especially not now. and let me end, too - particularly in the direction of the ppe-de - with an old dutch proverb: "the tactics of an elephant in a china box rarely have the desired effect".
president, now that the original deadline for the lisbon strategy is coming to an end, it can be said that the objectives set by the heads of government in 2000 have not been sufficiently achieved. but it is particularly with the current economic crisis that it is of the utmost importance that the lisbon strategy is taken seriously. if that had happened, europe would probably have been better able to respond with resilience to economic setbacks. one of the main agreements of the lisbon strategy is the aim of spending 3% of gdp on research and development: two thirds of the funding is provided by the private sector and one third by the public sector. that is a target that is being met by virtually no country in the european union, and that is a brake on innovation in the european union. in a global crisis europe will have to find the strength to bring the economy back on track. at the same time, it is of course strange that a large part of the eu budget is subsidising agriculture and regional funds, for example - so still too much of the old economy - while the targets for investment in research are not being met. there are plenty of possibilities. think of clean environmental or medical technologies or the growing sector of, say, european computer games. here, targeted support is effective. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). that's just what you need to crawl out of the valley. the crisis gives us the opportunity and even the duty to implement much-needed reforms. i call on the member states to take their own commitments seriously, because when big goals are set, there must be the intention to achieve them. otherwise the european union will lose its credibility. a common policy requires everyone to do their best and does not allow member states to go off the rails.
the commission's proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products for human use is a very good one. it is precisely in this time of crisis that cohesion policy has its value in terms of community investment. we are now investing some 50 billion euros a year, and 65% of that is going to the priority areas in the lisbon agreements. we are making an active contribution, training workers and taking all kinds of regional initiatives for the years after the crisis. the ppe-de wants to maintain this integrated financial approach and not further fragment it. the flexibility is aimed at speeding up spending schedules, simplifying approval and facilitating the handling of preparatory costs, substantially expanding the eib's capacity with concrete programmes, including sustainable urban regeneration, and increasing energy efficiency opportunities, including in the old member states. ladies and gentlemen, i welcome these intensifications and flexibility. in march ii we will have a priority debate in this plenary on the adjustments to cohesion policy. we will therefore amend the regulations governing the funds and lay the foundations for a new cohesion formula: territorial cohesion, the framework for the period after 2013. we are investing - i have just heard that too - in high-quality activities such as clusters, r & d, innovation, rural development, and we are making sure that the knowledge economy and competitiveness in europe are given a boost. this applies, ladies and gentlemen, to all regions, to all member states. this will help to keep europe visible and contribute to a more united europe, even after the crisis.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). after all, economic growth and low inflation offered room for reform. reforms have been and remain necessary in competition with emerging economies. the current crisis shows that member states that have responded to that call are now doing better than others. these other member states have large budget deficits, and the widening of these deficits by the member states which did not heed our call is now threatening the stability of our currency. i would ask the commission to ensure that the member states comply with the stability pact. this is the only way to avoid the costs of this crisis getting out of hand. temporary incentives that meet the sustainability test can therefore be applied on a limited scale. in addition to all the new plans, the observance of old agreements is evident.
mr president, this crisis is a test for europe. citizens now expect europe to act, and it is therefore incomprehensible that many national leaders continue to be stuck in a 'every man for himself' policy. europe is not the sum of 27 times the national interest. it would also be a capital mistake to divide europe into east and west. mr president, the liberals want to invest in the future, not in the mistakes of yesterday. the objectives of the lisbon strategy should not be put on hold, but rather we should focus more on education and research, innovation, sustainability and a strong european market. mr president, bankers who squander our money are despicable. but, mr schulz, politicians who now allow deficits and debts to mount unchecked and pass the bill on to younger generations are just as irresponsible. the alde group supports the core message of the ferrera report. only with truly european and forward-looking solutions can we overcome this crisis. it is now or never for europe.

kurdish television, about the openings that have been made between turkey and armenia; the will to reform of 2004 will have to return. i am also convinced that when that happens, optimism will return in paragraph 17 mrs oomen-ruijten addresses the whole of turkish society to ensure that freedom of religion is fully exercised. i fully support this call, because it touches on an essential accession criterion for turkey and the european union. in the meantime, turkish education in particular and the turkish media are competing in the dissemination of the stereotypical caricature of indigenous christians, of turkish christians, as enemies of their own nation, as accomplices of western powers, who want to re-colonize their homeland and want to divide it among themselves. commissioner, will you also address the passive turkish government, the responsible turkish government, on this obstacle to accession? and then, commissioner, there is a second question. all turkish identity papers state the religion of citizens. the reason for the many forms of social discrimination against turkish christians. this is a good reason, commissioner, to urge your turkish counterpart to delete this section in official documents immediately.
i would like to continue on the point raised by mrs oomen-ruijten: the political criteria. in the negotiation process with turkey, civil rights have been very clearly put on the agenda. we shall see that in this report. a number of things have clearly improved: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, including the establishment of a kurdish-language television channel, but also the setting up of a women's committee in the turkish parliament, which i have worked hard to promote in recent years as rapporteur for women's rights in turkey. major reforms. the increase in the number of shelters for battered women is also a clear improvement. but what happens to these women when they leave the shelters? how are they and the children cared for? turkey must pay attention to this. with the municipal elections coming up: we need more women on the city council. the election is at the end of this month. i would also like to draw attention to the fight against fraud. turkey must cooperate more closely with the european union in the fight against fraud and trafficking in women, because far too many people are victims of fraud with the green funds or with charitable institutions, for example.
i would like to thank mrs neyts for the completeness of her report. we as a parliament must, as she says, make our priorities very clear. there can be no impunity for war criminals. there must be full cooperation from all the countries in the region and there must also be a careful completion of the work of the international criminal court. the court of justice in the hague, with 116 cases completed and a lot of work in the region, can clearly present a very good balance sheet. no one can say that the guilty go unpunished anymore. i also welcome the emphasis on the full cooperation to be given to the court. that goes for all the countries where suspects come from. the people who are still being sought must also be brought before the court, and all the countries in the region have committed themselves to this with the european union. we would not be credible if we did not hold to that. i shall be tabling an oral amendment to that effect tomorrow, with the rapporteur's approval. the value of the court is great, mr president, because there is still so much to be done in the judicial system in the balkans. it is therefore also right that this report emphasizes the proper functioning of an independent and impartial judiciary. because that is one of the criteria of copenhagen. president. - the next item is the report (doc. the time limit. i think you should not be so rigidly adhering to 2010, 2011; it is much more important that the court should be able to continue to function after that period, if the cases have not yet been decided. the second point is the settlement. even with the arrest and trial of mladic and hadzic, the work of the court does not end. the residual mechanism and the international criminal court can handle tasks, but i believe that good and less good experiences should never be lost.
i would like to make a few points in this short debate. the work of the yugoslav tribunal is of the utmost importance, not only to ensure that those responsible for crimes committed during the balkan wars do not escape punishment, but also to serve the people's sense of justice. the tribunal also plays an important role in european policy towards the western balkans. that is also well emphasized in mrs neyts' report. as the end of the tribunal's mandate approaches, we must think about the completion of the work. we are concerned that the capacity to complete the cases that are already in progress and to bring the last two suspects still at large, mladic and hadzic, to the tribunal, will be maintained. at no time should the impression be given that the length of the mandate and its expiry would leave these people free to go. whether this is done by means of an extension of the mandate or by setting up a residual mechanism is not a matter of principle for us, and as far as we are concerned we might also be able to find a way of putting the judges, the counsellors and the secretariat on standby mode.
i would like to add my support to many of the words that john bowis has just spoken. the green paper we have received from you, commissioner, is a splendid piece of work. of course, with a green paper we are always curious to see all the reactions that come out of it. these are, of course, partly predictable, but it is good to have them in order to be able to take them into account in possible future legislation. it is an important green paper because it is a matter that must be settled. the green paper is the result of the controversy which arose at the time over frits bolkestein's proposals and it is good that the commission is approaching it in this way, cautiously, with a green paper and a lot of discussion about it. because i think there's a lot of fear around this subject. i would therefore not limit my contribution to the subject of health workers alone, because i believe that in other areas too we are repeatedly confronted with the same fear of the unknown of the european dimension in health care. much has been achieved in the european union in the field of public health in recent years, both under your predecessors and with you as commissioner. i would therefore like to take this opportunity to congratulate you, commissioner, on the stamp you have managed to put on this policy in a relatively short time. i did not think it would be possible in such a short time and i think we can all be proud of how you have done it. a great deal has been achieved in recent years, for example in the field of paediatric medicines, a matter which the public does not even know is a problem, but for which a european solution is of paramount importance, because member states cannot solve it alone. the scale is what helps. i believe that the same applies to other areas. for example, tobacco policy and the discouragement of smoking, where the european union is also playing a leading role. not only within the union itself, but also outside it. again, it is precisely the scale that allows us to be effective at this point. we are also very busy with crossborder care and protecting patients' rights in europe, also under the leadership of rapporteur john bowis, and i hope and expect that we can also achieve a positive result on that point. however, time and again, on every subject that comes up, you see that not only ministers, but also fellow parliamentarians in the national parliaments, are reluctant to further deepen european cooperation in the field of public health. with 27 different systems in the european union, we all think we have the best model. whoever you talk to, any system is the best system. of course you can't. you can't have 27 different systems and 27 best systems at the same time. of course, every country has thought through such a system. there are all people and all well-meaning interests involved. the european union is suddenly rushing through this difficult balance with an idea that we now believe is the best. i understand that there is resistance. but i do think that in some areas, such as rare diseases, that scale can be beneficial for both patients and systems. there are many reasons for greater european involvement in public health. almost 40,000 patients are waiting for an organ in europe, and almost ten people on those waiting lists die every day. alcohol abuse kills 195,000 people and damages the european economy by €125 billion a year. a matter that is probably best handled locally, not even nationally. but you also see european trends, for example, in the abuse of alcohol among young people. then you have to consider whether it might not make sense to tackle the european issue. however, we are doing everything we can under the current treaty texts to tackle these problems. but we have to do more. for example - and this is why this green paper is so important - a genuine freedom of movement for health services. i am convinced that if we face all the problems, because there are problems, and come up with solutions, for example to combat fraud, to strengthen the legal certainty of patients, but also of healthcare workers, then in the end it will be better for everyone if this free movement is organised in a responsible way, but it will become possible. better cooperation on organ donation and effective cooperation on pandemic prevention - a subject i keep coming back to. if you do not deal with this in a european way, i am convinced that we will have very big problems if that flu comes from thailand. in fact, the commission should be able to take crisis measures within 24 hours at that time. finally, i think that article 152 is ultimately insufficient to organise further effective european action. if we dare to think of amending the treaty in the distant future, i think we should think of extending the legal basis for public health in a new treaty.
mr president, what is happening in sri lanka now has been perfectly predictable for several years. it is not just a humanitarian problem, but above all a serious political failure. after years of violent struggle for secession from the northeast of the country, a previous government of norway has offered to mediate between the sinhalese-controlled government and the tamil rebel movement. the norwegian negotiator, who has worked for a long time to find peaceful solutions, is now a minister in the new government. unfortunately, that possibility for a peaceful solution has since been abandoned. the sri lankan government ended peace efforts in the summer of 2006 and re-opted to impose a military solution unilaterally. that government is probably thinking now that it has achieved a great success. in reality, peaceful, harmonious and equal coexistence between the two peoples has become even more difficult for the future. without a compromise on a peaceful solution, the way forward will also be horribly violent. we must return to peace negotiations without winners and losers.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1785/92 on the common organization of the market in bananas (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). i don't have much trouble with this in itself. it may be useful to further shape bilateral relations in such a partnership, but there are at least two risks inherent in this construction. firstly, europe cannot declare every country a strategic partner. i think this is a devaluation of the concept of 'strategic'. i therefore advocate that only bilateral relations with key partners should be included in this denominator. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. secondly, i sometimes get the vague feeling that these strategic partnerships have a highly symbolic value. we can have another summit and then a lot of things will stay the same. these partnerships often function as a forum only. the question of the concrete results often remains unanswered. i can also taste some of this in the draft recommendation from parliament to the council on brazil, which we are discussing this morning. i am also very much concerned about the need to identify specific problems. i would like to illustrate this concern, mr president, by using three elements of the draft recommendation. first of all, it is somewhat veiledly noted that this partnership must not be at the expense of the union's relations with mercosur. how is it possible that the european union, which has always been a champion of regional cooperation, is giving priority to bilateral relations with brazil over regional cooperation with mercosur? the union is following the wrong order here. the european union should draw attention to the importance of a strong mercosur in brazil and encourage it to invest heavily in this cooperation agreement itself. instead, the union is sitting down at the bilateral level with brazil and is sending the signal that mercosur is secondary to us. i also think that the draft recommendation is not very clear in terms of trade. the call is made for joint efforts to smooth the doha round. the european union is not a party to the gatt, but it is a party to the gatt. the issue of market access is a point of contention for both sides. i think that the doha round has a better chance of success if this issue is negotiated at bilateral level. i do not want to say that this will be easy, but i think it is a better way than making rhetorically sound declarations. i also looked at the draft recommendation from my geopolitical point of view. i do not see any call for brazil to take a leading role in the region. i would like to conclude my contribution with that. brazil must monitor the political developments in the region and does so primarily on the basis of the ambition of its neighbour venezuela to achieve hegemony on the continent. this is a situation which is neither in the interest of the continent itself nor in the interest of the european union. the controversial referendum on constitutional change in venezuela is a good example of how little european values as a democracy will remain.

author. − mr president, before we vote on the resolution i tabled on the accession process for the former yugoslav republic of macedonia, i wish to make three technical remarks so that everybody can decide on the correct texts. first remark: where it speaks about the macedonian negotiator in paragraph 12, the text should be ‘the negotiator of the former yugoslav republic of macedonia’. this is entirely in line with what we decided on when voting on my reports of 2007 and 2008. second remark: in paragraph 18, the macedonian orthodox church is the official name of the biggest religious community in the country. this is mentioned in order to distinguish it from the serbian orthodox group. this use of the word ‘macedonian’ cannot be replaced by any reference to any state name. as it is the official name of an institution, i propose surrounding the name with inverted commas. third remark: as regards the translation of the word ‘pending’ in paragraph 10 in the phrase ‘pending full implementation of the key priorities of the accession partnership’, the french and italian versions – at least – differ from the texts in english, german and dutch. for all final translations i propose using the original english version.
in writing. - on 9 september 2006, 5 february 2009 and last night debates took place here on the permanent and hopeless struggle between tamils and sinhalese on the island of sri lanka. i've been in all those debates. in it i have always advocated not to take sides in the conflict but to help to bring about a peace agreement between the two camps. the agreement should at least result in an autonomous tamil region in the north-east of the country. last night, mr tannock and mr van orden argued the exact opposite. they point to the atrocities of the tamil resistance movement and wish to offer all possible support to the sinhalese government. it is forgotten that both sides are using unacceptable force and that the government has broken the peace process initiated by norway. i am glad that a resolution was adopted today containing most of the amendments proposed by mr evans, calling for humanitarian aid, mediation and a peaceful solution.
in writing. − there are three positions within this parliament on turkey's future accession to the eu. the first view, advocated by former us president bush, is that accession is highly desirable because turkey can supply a lot of cheap labour and military and is a loyal nato ally. the second view is that turkey's accession will always be undesirable because it is considered to be asian, islamic, too big and too dangerous. we have always defended a third position here, together with our group. turkey should be able to join if it so wishes. this is important for the many europeans of turkish origin. before that happens, this country must become a full democracy, without political prisoners, without banned media, without banned parties. the kurdish language must have equal rights in government, education and the media, the high 10% electoral threshold for parliament must disappear and the kurdish southeast must be granted autonomy in a decentralized state. the mass murder of the armenians in 1915 can no longer be denied, just as it would not be acceptable for the germans to deny the mass murder of the jews in the period 1938-1945. the report by mrs oomen-ruijten is far too weak on this point. that is why we must unfortunately vote against it.
in writing. - paragraph 45 of the ria oomen-ruijten report states that the eu's accession negotiations with turkey should be extended. the vvd has serious objections to this. the vvd believes that turkey has made too little progress in recent years and that there is no reason to speed up the negotiations. the vvd believes that turkey must first comply with a number of hard agreements. the european parliament has also called for the eu to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action against terrorism. the time has come for turkey to send positive signals to the eu, not for the vvd to send positive signals to turkey. despite the major objections to paragraph 45, the vvd delegation decided to vote in favour of the report as a whole, since it can be found in the rest of the text.
in writing. - i voted against this resolution in the final vote. i do not think that the report as a whole is a bad one, but one element is so important to me that i could not vote in favour of it. water is not a commodity to be traded, it is a basic necessity of life and everyone has a right to it. the use of water is not a choice of man, but essential to survival and therefore not suitable as a commercial or economic good. water supply should be in public hands and remain so. the commission has already proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks of chemical pollution.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to play a full part in the development of the world. they are the legal successors of colonies of european countries, territories which were conquered in the last centuries in order to have cheap resources. mining for metals and harvesting tropical crops were the main objectives, and in some cases, people were also traded as slaves. people with very different languages and cultures have thus become residents of such a colony, cut off from those with whom they have much more in common in language and culture. the philippines were taken from spain by the united states in 1898 and then governed as their own colony until 1946. as an independent state, this country has since gained a reputation for bad governance. the question is whether that's a coincidence. such states are not the product of popular will, they do not arise from below. they came from above and from outside. such states are not the best breeding ground for a democratic rule of law and for the peaceful settlement of social conflicts. they are often held together by force and the power of the military is great. there is often also a lot of room for foreign companies, which are predatory and bad for the environment and their employees. they are given the opportunity to misbehave by providing privileges and riches to those in power. such abuses call for counter-movements. if these movements are not seen by the state as a legal opposition that can grow into a participant in a government through peaceful means, there is a good chance that these movements will flee from self-preservation into violence. the government then responds with more state violence, sometimes without acknowledging that this violence comes from the state itself. since 2001, hundreds of activists, trade unionists, journalists and religious leaders have been killed or abducted. opponents who were released by a court were still imprisoned by the state. the perpetrators of murders and kidnappings are not being traced or punished. foreign attempts at mediation have been rejected and eventually abandoned. the resolution rightly calls for greater efforts to promote mediation, compromise and peaceful solutions. without the integration of counter-movements into the democratic rule of law, the philippines will remain a chaotic country where life is bad.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the main objective of these governments was and is to hold together the territory of this vast state, inhabited by very different peoples. these various peoples are being subjected by all possible means to the authority of the capital, khartoum. that is why for years there has been a struggle against the secessionist movement in the south, the area that is predominantly non-arab and non-islamic. it is still far from certain that the south will be free to actually exercise the agreed right of secession in 2011. in the western region of darfur, the government is determined to prevent such a separation. there has always been a conflict of interest between nomadic cattle farmers and settled agriculturalists. the government now has a stake in that conflict. the depopulation of this area by driving the permanent inhabitants to the neighbouring country of chad is an important means of keeping this area under control. in this dirty work, the government cannot use any foreign observers, aid workers or mediators. the committee on foreign affairs of this parliament has already called for a european military intervention some years ago. such remarks do good to sections of domestic public opinion and give the impression that a rich and powerful europe can impose its solutions on the rest of the world. in practice, this is not a feasible solution. moreover, there is no clarity whatsoever as to the purpose of such an intervention. is this a matter of temporary humanitarian aid or the creation of a state in darfur? any variant would be seen in africa as a renewed display of colonial power from europe, primarily designed for european self-interest. a less spectacular way, but probably more effective, is the international arrest warrant against president al-bashir and the investigation of war crimes. the offer of outside help for peaceful solutions and humanitarian aid must always be made. the affected populations, many of whom have fled, deserve our support in their struggle for survival.

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, i have not much more to add. this is the first debate on the implementation of the strategy. the head is off, but we're just getting started. there is still an extraordinary amount to be done. africa is still the poorest continent. the commission has already indicated where our concerns lie and where the challenges lie, whether it is peace and security, economic growth, good governance, capacity building or the role of parliaments and civil society. the commissioner has said a lot about that. i thank the commissioner and the members of the pan-african parliament for the good cooperation. we'll continue to follow this process.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. five important reports by our colleagues are being discussed today and tomorrow and, just before the european elections, are also being voted on. this is the largest budget of the european community and also the most visible to the public. cohesion policy has given europe a coherent and also a solidary face. no other continent has produced so much cohesion among its peoples. the new lisbon treaty also restores cohesion as a central objective. it even adds a third component, namely territorial cohesion. unusual times call for new answers. the financial crisis, the increased competition due to globalisation, the climate crisis and the lagging performance on the lisbon objective call for greater inclusiveness, strengthening and improvement in regional policy. we are talking about this in the green paper. it is not business as usual, but a call for better governance and territorial cohesion, which also exposes a criticism of developments in which there are regions that are taking off, metropolitan areas that are progressing and regions that are falling behind. that is not the europe that this parliament is facing. that is why today, in this joint debate, we are setting the course for the period after 2013, for the legislative review which we will begin in the next parliamentary term after the elections. i shall briefly summarize the main points of this debate and of territorial cohesion. as far back as 2005, mr gullick's report reflected the wishes of this parliament. the new territorial dimension is now a permanent objective in articles 13 and 174 of the new lisbon treaty. it is, as i said, a clear opposition to an asymmetrical europe of regions in full expansion and more rural areas lagging behind. it is a dual entity, between centres of excellence (pôles d'excellence) and the specific position of other regions and cities with their own qualities and pluriformity. territorial cohesion is also complementary to the existing policies of economic and social cohesion. it's an integral concept. it provides an overview of the effects of sectoral community and decentralised action such as research and development, the common agricultural policy, traffic and transport, employment situations and the fight against climate change. the concept of territorial cohesion, as the consultations of the past six months have shown, is widely supported and that is a good thing. the concept includes concentration on the one hand and connection and cooperation on the other, and we would like to develop this concept in the next period.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. - mr president, i would have liked to address the council today, but i understand that no one from the council is present and that this debate may therefore be of less importance. i am a little surprised, because the necessary budgetary commitments were made last weekend and the council is not concerned with the actual financing of these commitments. i'm standing here with the necessary sense of irony. officially we are here to talk about the mid-term review, but in reality all the necessary has been said about the economic stimulus package which was agreed last weekend. that package is just now showing where that interim review, where that multiannual budget is lacking. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. galileo, the food aid for africa, the way it has been financed, did not deserve a beauty award. now, we come to the discussion of the economic stimulus package. the council has made commitments but does not seem to know where the money is to come from. in the energy sector, for example, there is still some 2 billion to be found for energy projects, not only from this year's budget, but also from next year's budget and perhaps the budget for the year after that. the margin of the agricultural budget is already being eroded. they say: yes, that's not farm money, that's the margin. but there is only one animal disease outbreak and we really need that money in the agricultural budget. you may remember the kzk. or what do we do if the prices on the dairy market remain low and intervention is needed? the margin on the agricultural budget will only get smaller in the coming years. the report of the committee on agriculture for the midterm review rightly points this out. i would therefore like to have confirmation from the commission and the council - if they do appear and if not in writing - that we will first look at the existing commitments in the agricultural sector, only then will we look at the margin and see whether that margin can be used in other areas. those are the rules of the game we agreed to. if we don't do that, i think we're throwing out the kid with the bathwater.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. as a liberal, i am naturally in favour of a functioning internal market and the least possible government intervention in that market. on the other hand, consumers must have choices. at the moment, the consumer has no choice when buying a new phone. there he has to buy a charger automatically, and that very often causes great irritation among consumers. furthermore, research shows that consumers have to spend around eur 300 million a year on new chargers because they are automatically included in the purchase of a new telephone. the result is that this also imposes a burden on the environment of 300 million euros a year, and i am not even talking about the waste of energy caused by these cheap chargers, because these chargers often remain in the socket and continue to use energy without a telephone. my colleague marco cappato and i have been advocating for years the introduction of a european standard and i would like to congratulate the commissioner very much on the way he has managed this by flirting a little with the industry, by threatening: "come up with a solution yourself, or we'll come up with mandatory legislation". i would like to compliment him on that, because as a liberal i am against compulsion, but rather for seduction. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. i 've made this joke before . when i met my wife 35 years ago, i seduced her into going with me. if i had obliged her then, she probably wouldn't have seen the added value of now and the idea would have been less popular. i think the example also applies to industry, because if industry is allowed to solve it, it will do better than if politics dictates it. so i think it's a great solution. i hope that in the long term you will succeed in introducing a standard not only for mobile phone chargers but also for more electrical appliances, because it is a nuisance to the point of being a nuisance. when a family goes on a weekend outing, there's 30 loads on a cabinet. there are always people, including in this house today, who have to ask: "does anybody have a charger? for i have forgotten mine". then you have to have a specific charger of a specific model and of a specific brand. once again, my compliments to you, commissioner. i also hope that this will actually happen in 2012, and i also hope that you will take strong action if the industry does not comply with the commitment, because that is also very important. you have to keep a stick behind the door, or rather seduce and self-regulate to find a solution. you have succeeded and i - i'm the reporter. - mr president, first of all i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, because they have helped me very much to make improvements to the commission's report. i should also mention the cooperation with the czech presidency. they have also thought very carefully and we could almost have reached an agreement at first reading, but there were still a few minor points pending and it is also much more democratic to vote first in this house. when i first heard that the commission was coming up with regulations on novel foods, i wondered what the new food was all about. i was also surprised that the commission proposal took the single market as its starting point. to begin with the last one. as with many other matters in this parliament, this subject can be approached from the perspective of the internal market, from the producer's perspective or from the economic perspective. but it can also be approached from the perspectives of food safety, the consumer, health and the environment, in other words, based on the well-being of the european citizen. the eu also talks a lot about the environment and animal welfare, and i thought: let us not just keep to words in this report, but let us also put action behind the words. so when my report on novel foods had to make a choice, i chose food safety, consumer welfare, food safety, the environment and animal welfare as the first priority. i hope very much, and i have heard from the shadow rapporteurs, that they will support me in the vote on this point. of course, innovation is also very important. now i come to my second point: what are new foods, anyway? the commission proposal did not make that very clear. the commission then informed me that the issues involved included nanotechnology and meat from cloned animals. let's start with nanotechnology. i had no idea what it was. i think a lot of consumers don't know what it is either, but it turns out it's already in our food more than we think: in the case of energy drinks, fruit and vegetable packaging, but also in certain oils and teas, for example. so we need to regulate very quickly, because technology is fun, it can be good for the consumer, but we do need to make sure that what's on our plate is safe. it should therefore be covered by the regulation. as regards meat from cloned animals, parliament has already said in a resolution to the commission that it does not want meat from cloned animals to be placed on the market as food. if meat from cloned animals were to be covered by this regulation, we as a parliament would be indirectly admitting that we would still be in favour of such meat being placed on our market as food. this is not possible and meat from cloned animals should therefore be excluded from the scope of this regulation. it's not so much about safety. there's just a lot of animal suffering involved in cloning meat, and a lot of these cloned animals don't even live long. so it has no added value to the food supply at the moment.
member of the commission. − madam president, i would like to elaborate on a few very important points which were made by honourable members. concerning nanotechnologies, i have taken note of recent scientific opinions and i am convinced by the related documents in mrs liotard’s report. the european union will be first in the world to have a regulatory definition of engineered nanomaterials and a coherent and flexible approach to this technology. in order to clarify the commission’s position on the definition of nanotechnologies, i would like to make the following statement on behalf of the commission. the commission notes that work towards a common definition of nanomaterials is still evolving. the commission therefore confirms that, in future community legislation, progress on the common definition should be taken into account, and notes that the comitology procedures included in this proposal also enable the definition in this proposal to be updated. regarding the mandatory labelling of all food produced with the aid of nanotechnology, i would like to note that the commission is indeed in favour of consumer information about the presence of nanomaterials in food. however, under the novel food regulation we have a case-by-case authorisation which also lays down conditions for use of such products, including the labelling requirements. labelling will thus be examined on a case-by-case basis. let me now clarify my position on the important issue of cloning. i have already stated that i consider that the novel food regulation is not the most suitable instrument for managing all the issues of cloning. novel food only deals with food safety and market authorisation. therefore the use of clones in breeding programmes – semen, embryos and ova – cannot be regulated under the novel food regulation, nor can issues linked to animal health and welfare be addressed here. on 13 january, the college of commissioners held an orientation debate on the cloning of farm animals for food production. the commission agreed that there remain a number of unanswered questions. in this respect the commission is working closely with efsa and ensuring that related scientific research is conducted. at the same time, i launched discussions with our main trading partners: the united states, canada, japan, australia and new zealand. yes, we need more information and data on cloning techniques and the way in which progenies of cloned animals should be handled from a regulatory perspective. as some of you have mentioned, in july last year the european food safety authority delivered its opinion covering the scientific aspects of cloning for food in the eu. the general conclusion of the opinion is that the risk assessment is insufficient due to the limited data available. with regard to the health and welfare of animals, the opinion makes it clear that a significant proportion of clones have been found to be adversely affected, often severely, and with a fatal outcome both for cloned animals and for the surrogate animal. the opinion of the european group on ethics also highlights a number of scientific questions that need to be answered, and research that needs to be carried out, on food safety, animal health and welfare, traceability and labelling. in conclusion, i wish to acknowledge that, with regard to cloning, there is certainly an issue to be addressed, but the novel food regulation is not the right place to regulate all the questions surrounding this sensitive matter. nevertheless, i would like to make a commitment, on behalf of the commission, to produce, as soon as possible, a comprehensive report on all aspects of cloning techniques with a view to the production of foodstuffs, including animal health and welfare in relation to clones and their offspring, accompanied, if appropriate, by legislative proposals. let me stress that i believe that a solution can be found on the issue and, in this respect, i thank parliament for its understanding and cooperation. liotard report (a6-0512/2008) the commission can accept amendments 7, 12, 34, 35, 41, 42, 44, 45, 53 and 63. amendments 3, 8, 15, 20, 58, 64, 65, 76, 87, 88 and 89 can be accepted in principle. amendments 1, 6, 10, 25, 30, 31, 36, 40, 66, 67, 69, 77, 82, 84, 85 and 93 are acceptable subject to rewording. the commission cannot accept amendments 2, 4, 5, 9, 11, 13, 14, 16, 17, 18, 19, 21, 22, 23, 24, 26, 27, 28, 29, 31, 32, 33, 37, 38, 39, 43, 46, 47, 48, 49, 50, 51, 52, 54, 55, 56, 57, 59, 60, 61, 62, 68, 70, 71, 72, 73, 74, 75, the rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to thank all my colleagues for their contribution to the debate and also for the support i have received for certain points in my report. of course, there were some critical comments. i am glad of that too, because it is only good for the debate. our aim is to give consumers the assurance that food produced by new techniques or new food coming onto the market is safe. we must make sure that our input and our proposals are successful. it also creates certainty for the producer who wants to innovate, and thus he knows what he or she is up against. the report also addresses data protection in relation to those producers, which also promotes innovation in the field of food safety. i would also like to thank the commissioner very much for what she said about nanotechnology. in this area, there will indeed be many changes, and if we establish a definition at the beginning, it should be possible to revise it again as science progresses. i would also like to thank the commissioner for what she said about animal cloning. on the other hand, i would point out that this parliament has already tabled a resolution in which we say that we do not want meat from cloned animals to be placed on the market as food. that is also reflected in this report and i therefore call for the exclusion of animal cloning from the novel food regulation. that is what the report calls for and, as you have just heard, it is strongly supported by this parliament. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank my colleagues thomas, vivian and jan-jaap, who have worked very hard to produce this report.
- i'm the reporter. - president, closing the ozone hole is important for the environment and for the health of the entire world population. our atmosphere is designed to protect us with two different layers. the co2 in the tropospheric layer is holding the heat so we don't get too cold. the ozone in the stratospheric layer protects us from the sun's harmful uv rays. the ozone-depleting substances regulation is primarily aimed at protecting the stratospheric ozone layer, but also at preventing climate change. the substances banned have both ozone-depleting and global warming potential. the main ozone depleting substances are found in the exhaust gases of spray cans, refrigerators, insulation materials and some specific solvents and cleaning agents. the cfcs and halons which have the strongest ozone depleting effect have been eliminated after a limited number of exceptions. the production of hcfcs is already prohibited and the use will be prohibited by 2020. the preamble to the new regulation states that the production and use of ozone depleting substances shall be stopped or minimised as far as possible. this is an important policy point. the committee on the environment has adopted 64 amendments. two trilogues followed, resulting in an agreement between the council and parliament. the 64 amendments are hereby replaced by a single consolidated text. i would like to briefly recall nine important results of the agreement. firstly, the legal basis of the regulation has been changed in the field of the environment. this gives member states the opportunity to take further environmental protection measures. secondly, the use of the pesticide methyl bromide is prohibited as of 18 march 2010. this also applies to the gassing of containers for pest control. the only exception is for emergencies, like a major epidemic. thirdly, in the hcfc decommissioning programme, the percentage has been reduced to 7% for the last years compared to 1997. fourthly, halons may only be used in establishments designated by the government. fifthly, new substances have been added to the list of substances nominated for restrictive measures. the european commission has committed itself to further studying these substances. sixthly, for the necessary exceptions, such as reagents and laboratory use, labelling is made compulsory. it was also agreed that the use of laboratories should not increase. seventh, reuse and recycling of ozone depleting substances for existing appliances can only take place within the same company. in addition, a logbook of the quantities present should be kept to prevent fraud and illegal trade. eighth, there is attention to enforcement through inspections. the eu is also committed to the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment. ninthly, the control and enforcement system has been strengthened to prevent leakage of ozone depleting substances. however, more is needed to address the problem of stored ozone depleting substances. the european commission has an important role to play in this. i would like to stress once again the need to develop the directive on construction and demolition waste, as we agreed seven years ago in the sixth environmental action programme. all in all, we have achieved a satisfactory result. the ozone layer will have a better chance of recovery, so that harmful effects, such as skin cancer and damage to plants and trees, will be reduced.

mr president, there is a lot of talk about how much money we actually need to spend to combat this economic crisis. we can talk about that for a long time. i believe that future generations must also be taken into account, that they should not be blamed. but perhaps more important than that is the question of what we spend that money on. i worry, though, when i see the reflex to put money back into old industries, old technologies, to keep faltering industries going. it is therefore striking that in the council conclusions the chapter on the economic crisis and the chapter on energy and sustainability are simply treated as two totally different themes, whereas now is the time to link the two together. i do not want to be as cavalier as mr turmes was earlier today, but the time has come to invest in new technologies and also in knowledge. it is intolerable that billions are being poured into the car industry and that cuts must be made in education. then we're really on the wrong end! another point is the solutions for the eastern european countries and the non-euro countries. they are being left behind, and an amount of 50 billion euros is mentioned. i wonder whether the council and the commission are also considering an option of accelerated accession to the euro zone for those countries which are not yet members, because the stability and strength of those countries is in the interest of europe as a whole. finally, i am very concerned about the nationalist language and the tendency of all national leaders to adopt 'own country first' solutions. we should all be concerned about the rise of the far right and make sure we tackle it before the elections.
president. - we are on the eve of new relations between the european union and the united states. we all know how much damage was done by eight years of george bush in the white house. that's why so many europeans were happy with the election of obama and his promise to tackle some things fundamentally differently. the report by mr millán mon deals with all these important issues. for example, the joint approach to climate change and the financial and economic crisis. but also the need for a new strategy in afghanistan and pakistan and the closure of guantanamo. the last issue, guantanamo, was one of the abuses that have seriously damaged the moral authority of the united states worldwide. that includes cases of torture and kidnapping. the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against terrorism. there is another decision, perhaps less striking but equally embarrassing in my view, which must also be reversed as soon as possible. that is the refusal of the united states to cooperate with the international criminal court in the hague. in fact, one month after the international criminal court opened in july 2002, the u.s. congress retaliated by passing the so-called american service members protection act. what exactly does that law entail? that law prohibits american institutions and individuals from cooperating with or providing information to the icc. it requires americans before participating in united nations operations to obtain an international guarantee of immunity. in other words, they cannot be prosecuted. countries that do cooperate with the icc can be punished for this and have been punished by the united states for it. finally, a part that caused the most fuss in my country, the netherlands, the law gives the american president all the means - the ability to use them - to free americans detained by the international criminal court. hence the law in the netherlands is known as the hague invasion act. we can make a lot of jokes about that, and we have, and rightly so. but that would be an underestimation of its significance. this law was a very hostile and very symbolic response by bush to the creation of the international criminal court. what we need now is an equally symbolic, but i hope very friendly, response from mr obama. i would like to ask the commissioner to take this matter up with the president when he meets with him next week.
