president. - my group is not against the debate today, but it has great difficulty in summing up the result in a resolution. it gives the impression that we can adopt a resolution on how to deal with the history of europe and particularly with the totalitarian past. as the negotiations that have just taken place have shown, they quickly became bogged down in the question of what words we can and cannot use in such a resolution. please, let's leave it to historians to interpret our history precisely, knowing that objectivity is impossible. of course, politicians can help to ensure that the past is given sufficient attention, and that certainly applies to the crimes committed by hitler and stalin. they must never be forgotten and we must honor the victims. we must also all realise that our fellow europeans in central and eastern europe have suffered under two totalitarian systems and that this experience does not apply to people like me, who was born in the netherlands. geremek, our former colleague, once rightly remarked: "we have not yet arrived at the reunion of our memories". we as politicians have a responsibility to organise what we particularly want to remember, but let us do that together with historians. a day to commemorate all the victims of the totalitarian systems in europe in the last century is a good suggestion in itself, but let us decide in consultation with historians what the appropriate format and the appropriate date of such a day should be, so that everyone can feel at home. i am a historian myself, and i think that if we just tie that day to the molotov-ribbentrop pact, that does not do justice to all that happened in the 20th century. the discussion is important. what are the lines of demarcation between politicians and historians? what do we want to remember, and how? the debate is undoubtedly continuing. we as a group will certainly be involved in that. as an example of this i would like to show you this book which is due to be published in two weeks' time, entitled 'the politics of the past, the use and abuse of history', in which we have especially left the floor to historians and we hope that this will really contribute to the quality of the debate in this parliament and elsewhere of course.
i would like to join those who have congratulated the rapporteur. he delivered a fantastic report. i want to make a few points very quickly. first, we have seen in recent years a parallel increase in the storage of personal data by companies and governments. governments use the corporate databases, but we see that there are different levels of protection for the first and third pillars. i find that extremely worrying. secondly, and i am glad that my amendment has been adopted here, criminals are of course also profiting in many ways from the internet. identity theft is growing at an alarming rate and we must therefore ask the european commission to set up an identity theft hotline, not just for the exchange of information but also for the benefit of the victims. thirdly, there is a real need for global standards. the commission is also working on this, but such standards should be developed in an open democratic procedure and not by european commission officials negotiating with officials in the united states. finally, the european commission often speaks highly of freedom and civil rights, but i must say that in recent years, under the leadership of commissioner frattini and with the help of the council, numerous measures have been taken to enable citizens to be spied on day and night and to restrict their freedoms. it is high time to evaluate what has happened there and what effect it has had. i would therefore like to conclude with a suggestion to the commission: i urge that a separate commissioner for civil rights and civil liberties be appointed in the next term.
the european parliament has worked hard on the white paper on sport and has given massive support to the request to the european commission to come up with clear guidelines on the application of european law to sport. not only parliament but also sports organisations from all over europe have called for clarity. it is inhuman that they remain in doubt about this because in the present situation the courts have to decide on the application of european law time and again. in biarritz, the european ministers for sport repeated the request to the european commission. it is time we heard from the commission itself whether it has already started to draw up these guidelines. can the commission indicate when these guidelines will be available? is the commission prepared to organise a conference with the relevant stakeholders, including representatives from the sports world and the european parliament, in order to start or accelerate this process and provide more clarity to the european sports organisations as soon as possible? that brings me to another point which is closely related to this. the practice shows that there are no or hardly any good dialogue mechanisms in sport. the most obvious example is the debate on the amended wada anti-doping code. they cling stubbornly to unworkable rules and there is no room for any real dialogue with the european institutions and the athletes themselves to find solutions that can lead to an effective anti-doping policy that does respect the civil rights and privacy of athletes. i speak a lot to athletes and interest groups who complain about the lack of consultation and complain that wada and the international olympic committee are making demands but are not open to input themselves. the reaction of wada to yesterday's proposal from uefa and fifa already indicates that there is no question of an open attitude at all. to what extent has the european anti-doping committee itself taken the initiative to establish consultations on the protection of the civil rights of european athletes and how does the commission think a consultative framework for sport should be set up in general, but also in such cases? does the commission also believe that this consultation should be reciprocal by definition?
member of the commission. − mr president, that is true. usually questions are easier than answers and usually there are more questions than answers, but i believe that together we can find more and better answers. this is all about cooperation, not only between parliament and the commission, but especially with the world of sport associations, federations, member states and stakeholders. if they are responsible and responsive they come together and look for solutions which are acceptable and compatible with the law. otherwise we adapt and change the law, whether at national or european level. so that is how we should proceed. i look forward to maintaining this approach. i think much has changed since 2007. i do not want to repeat myself or to once again recommend reading the white paper. there is a shorter version and also a version with annexes. the first time we tried, we put together descriptions of all cases and all decisions related to sport, which made a kind of map showing how sport relates to european community law or policies. of course we are proposing further guidelines. we have proposed – and in biarritz i introduced – physical activity guidelines, which could help to promote the volume, intensity and quality of physical education training in our societies and especially in schools. it should be adopted by the council of health ministers. we do not have a sports ministers’ council, but in fact the main reason is that it is related to public health. we cannot have one large specific set of guidelines for everything. i have said that we can address specific concrete issues theme by theme rather than case by case. next i hope we can deal with licensing – how to make a more transparent and more sustainable system and create a set of principles for our licensing policies. of course uefa or its partners should have the concrete responsibility for this, but we can help. it is in our interest to promote principles such as self-regulation, transparency and sustainability, and we can make several other important contributions to improvements in licensing. we will support the anti-doping conference. this offers a great opportunity to say much more on this, but i am against the establishment of another agency or a european wada. i have also said publicly that there is nadas, the european network of cooperation between national anti-doping agencies in our countries, whether they are public or private. europe needs to be more active and more united. there are lessons to be learned from wada and we should do so quickly. there are disputes. we have spoken to ms bozkurt about this code of conduct. today i saw on television sepp blatter and john fahey, the chairman of wada, discussing how anti-doping and football competitions could go hand in hand after the code of conduct has been adopted, and there is a problem. so on anti-doping i think we need more internal cooperation in order to make europe more credible. we have the council of europe, we have a convention on this. we have many reasons to be more coherent in our contribution to the world of anti-doping or a world which fights more efficiently against doping. you have said that dialogue is not easy in this area – i mean not just regarding anti-doping but sport issues in general. it is true that sometimes it is difficult, even at international and europe level or between different disciplines or segments, but i can assure you that a culture of dialogue and openness and a readiness to engage in dialogue is growing. i took part in the opening of the eoc office in brussels, which is very close to rond point schuman and to the commission, council and parliament. this means the permanent presence of the european olympic committees and the ioc in the european institutions for the purpose of dialogue and cooperation. it says something about the importance of this and the mutual readiness to work in dialogue to find solutions. with regard to international transfers, i am also in favour of the protection of youngsters and the promotion of training and education. in 2001 we adopted decisions or agreements on international transfers. the general age level is about 18 years, but in europe there is a specific arrangement for the period between 16 and 18 years of age. unless severe or very critical problems become evident, we do not need to change but rather to promote better and more training and to work with talent in europe. that is why, for example, we supported the home-grown player rule because this mainly promotes this sort of treatment. of course it is not an absolute rule but it concerns specific arrangements. we give preference to, or promote, specificity under certain rules. we have said that we will come back to this decision in five years’ time to see what the real impact has been – not just the theory – of this new rule. ‘six plus five’, as it stands now, is simply non-compatible with eu laws, as has been shown in a study. we have spoken to them and have read the papers but there is nothing new there. in substance we cannot say that we agree with the study. but i have to say that dialogue – especially following recent events in biarritz and lausanne – is real and very concrete between the european commission and its partners, including fifa. on this specific matter of ‘six plus five’ we will continue further. even fifa says that there is no reason for a hurried approach but rather a need for very concrete and credible answers. they do not want to get into formal disputes. we agreed to make room for more expertise and for more exchange. i think that is very beneficial. on the lisbon treaty and the recognition of specificity, two special clauses are included in article 149 of the treaty on european union regarding education, youth and sport. it also concerns the level of competence. this is only supportive action so the union will not have any power to decide but only to support member states in the promotion of the integrity of sportsmen and sportswomen. it could be helpful to organise a sports council as we have for education and youth, which will perhaps be the enlarged format. it could help to build the first eu sport policy and sport programme but will not mean a change of architecture or a general derogation. theme-by-theme specificity, which is real and does not mean business as usual, must be defendable vis-à-vis the system or the european court. my opinion only reflects the commission’s opinion. our opinion is not the final one when it comes to explanation of european union law. especially in this area many issues are interlinked. we would like to promote an increased legal certainty, which is why we started the process. the white paper is there and the pierre de coubertin action plan helps to make it more readable and more concrete but it is a process. with the treaty we can help even more but, without it, we will have to stay only at the level we have reached now. i spoke about transfers under 18 years of age. with regard to this, fifa made a very good decision, not only on transfers but on the promotion and protection of minors in sport – in football – and i think this is the right approach. so in some areas uefa sets a good example, for instance concerning home-grown players, and in some areas fifa sets a good example, for instance concerning the protection of minors. i think we can help both and it is important that this is also compatible within the world of football. last but not least – and i am sorry to have given a long answer – with regard to a forum on sport, which mr heaton-harris mentioned, we have actually established this. in biarritz we had a ministerial conference with major partners, but before that there was a forum with more than 200 stakeholders from very different areas of sport. it was a very positive, communicative and open forum and we want to continue with this. i believe that, under the swedish, or especially under the spanish, presidency, we can hold another one to reflect on the progress we have made and to make a commitment to go further. that is where i was when i started answering the questions – working together in a transparent, regular and credible manner. if that is the case, europe is a leading area where sport is protected and promoted, and where we fight against negative phenomena such as doping, corruption, violence and racism around sport. i would like to invite you to share this approach because we do not have a better one. we do not want to impose anything again but rather to propose, protect and support. that is my answer.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the pollution of the sea by ships. however, on various beaches in south asia, huge discarded seagoing vessels are still being scrapped under conditions that are not only unfriendly to the environment but also to people. in bangladesh, for example, none of the 36 sites for demolition meets the minimum environmental and safety standards. the positive thing is that a week ago the supreme court ordered the government of bangladesh to close these so-called demolition yards within two weeks. the same supreme court has also ruled that no ship with dangerous substances may enter bangladesh. in addition, the bangladesh minister for the environment must adopt rules on the recycling of scrap vessels in accordance with the basel convention within three months. these are precisely the important measures which the european parliament is proposing in accordance with last year's resolution. you'd think the supreme court in bangladesh would have read our resolution. i regard this ruling as an important victory in the fight against the unacceptable practices mentioned earlier. it is very important that the government of bangladesh follow up the legal rulings. this is not only true of bangladesh, but also of other asian countries. i have already pointed out that the efforts of individual states will not be sufficient. a global solution to the problem of the scrapping fleet remains urgently needed. in may of this year, a global conference will be held in hong kong by the international maritime organization. the aim of this conference is to reach a global agreement on the recycling of scrapped ships. the resolution by the committee on the environment should send a clear message to the council and the commission for the negotiations to be conducted with the aim of achieving the best possible result. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. firstly, it should be clear that discarded ships containing dangerous substances should be considered as hazardous waste and thus be covered by the basel convention. it is not in the interests of environmental protection to continue debating the definitions. amendment no 1 by the epp group slightly weakens this position and i cannot therefore support it. secondly, the beaching of ships for on-site dismantling must be considered unacceptable. thirdly, it is necessary that measures be taken before the end of the long ratification period, particularly in connection with the phasing out of single-hull tankers. fourthly, the certification of demolition yards is welcomed. fifthly, a compulsory fund should remain part of the measures under producer responsibility. this was stated by the european parliament last year. however, it seems that the european commission is coming back to this position. i would therefore like to hear from commissioner figel that the commission is still prepared to set up a demolition fund. with this resolution, which is added to last year's resolution, we as the european parliament are setting out a clear line towards a solution to the problems mentioned. i am glad to see that there is a great deal of agreement on this subject in the committee on the environment and in the committee on transport. i hope that the council and the commission will make the same concerted effort to conclude a good treaty in hong kong in two months' time, with the interests of safety, the environment and public health properly safeguarded. i would like to hear from commissioner figel whether this clear line will be used by the commission as a basis for negotiations at the imo conference and what measures the european commission will take to ensure the timely implementation and execution of the convention to be concluded.
on behalf of the pse-group. – mr president, i totally agree with our colleague, mr caspary, that we should postpone not only the final vote on the resolution but also the vote on his report – the assent report – because it was very clear in yesterday’s debate that the council in particular was not prepared to give the european parliament sufficient opportunity and the possibility to really monitor the situation in turkmenistan and have an impact on the situation there on the basis of such an agreement. since we did not get sufficient guarantees, we also support mr caspary’s proposal to postpone these votes.

in writing. - on behalf of the dutch cda delegation, i would like to make a statement of vote on the batzeli report. as far as we are concerned, that report certainly does not deserve a beauty award. many points are either clumsily described or are doubled in number. we also question certain demands which tend too much towards socialist state intervention. we also have questions about an absolute ban on selling below cost. that may sound sympathetic, but it's hardly feasible. in agriculture we consider this an important point. in the case of dumping, the competition authority must of course take action. we have voted for the batzelli report, however, because it contains a number of important elements which the alternative resolution is seeking to remove, in particular the study of the margins in the various links in the food production chain and the call to the european commission to look at the power of supermarkets in the field of competition, as has been repeatedly requested by the european parliament. we understand from the european commission that the marginal study can be carried out partly on the basis of existing data known within the commission and therefore also assume that this study will not entail a significant increase in the administrative burden.
president. - there is no doubt that russia is today fully involved in the global economic crisis. this morning i heard on the radio a scathing report from st petersburg about medicines that are unaffordable and therefore unavailable to the average russian citizen. the latest forecasts from the world bank indicate even more severe weather for the russian economy. what about the gloomy prediction that by the end of this year more than 20 million russians may fall below the subsistence level of 4,600 rubles (about $185)? indeed, europe and russia both face the urgency of vigorous crisis management. joint efforts to improve the global economic climate therefore deserve priority. but the precondition for this is mutual trust, and i would like to stress that, because the kremlin's foreign policy unfortunately stands in the way of that. think of the crisis in moldova, which is continuing and getting more complicated with a bad role for smirnoff. and think of the turmoil in ukraine last week. in short, the lack of mutual trust is hampering joint efforts. in this report parliament is sending that honest and clear message to the council and the commission for the negotiations with moscow and, i hope very much, is turning its back on the russians.
president. - as the chairman of the delegation for relations with russia, i have spent the last two and a half years working very intensively on russia and europe. i have not only dealt with incidents, but i have also looked at the long-term strategy with an open mind. so i really do not understand what graham watson, the chairman of the alde group, has said here. because i have not been one-sided in all these debates, and neither have my colleagues. we discussed the many problems with russia. but let us not forget that if we want to conclude a partnership agreement, we also want to be partners. this means that both sides must consult each other on difficult matters. indeed, a partnership agreement can only come about when you trust each other and not when you have only one eye on the problems. the united states has a different strategy at the moment. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. that's not wise. we need a partnership agreement, a strategic partnership, because we have only one big neighbour. we depend on that neighbour for our energy supply and they depend on our financial resources. but because we have values to defend in europe, we also talk to them about shared values and human rights. for the new strategy we really need with this great neighbour, it is not a good start to look at everything from the negative side. i therefore thank the commissioner for her reply, which contains positive elements for actually achieving something that is good for 500 million european citizens.
i would like to begin by congratulating mrs oomen-ruijten on what she has just said. i wholeheartedly agree with her argument and i hope that it will also influence the voting behaviour of her group tomorrow when our amendments to this report are put to the vote. i wish her the best of luck. my group has great difficulty with the present report by mr onyszkiewicz, and that is why we voted against it in the committee on foreign affairs. it is comprehensive - and rightly so - and the agenda for eu-russia relations is indeed extensive. the rapporteur, and i congratulate him on that, has tried to bring all these issues into the report. but the tone is wrong. we cannot say that these relations are of crucial importance - as the rapporteur does - and then just give examples of everything that is wrong and wrong in russia, without also having regard to the mistakes we ourselves have made in the past, over the last 20 years, with regard to the russian federation. russia is not a candidate country but a strategic partner willing to cooperate in areas of common interest. you have to be constructive and rational. and i agree with minister vondra that that should be the basis of our approach. the copenhagen criteria do not apply here, although that impression is sometimes given. i am in favour of a pragmatic approach based on interdependence. they need us and we need them. whether it's in the area of trade, energy cooperation, climate, or nuclear non-proliferation, solutions can only be found together. that is the underlying strategic importance - we use the word strategic in this debate - of the negotiations on a new agreement. we must do so in good faith, with due regard for russia's interests. our rapporteur rightly pays a great deal of attention to the neighbouring countries which the european union shares with russia. here again, cooperation is more effective than confrontation. we want to avoid a battle for spheres of influence at all costs. instead, the european union should be working to revive a weakened osce, the organization for security and cooperation in europe. in that context, we can also look at how to better address these frozen conflicts that we still have in europe, whether in georgia, azerbaijan or moldova. of course, not everything is cake and egg. the report rightly addresses this. we condemn and condemned the russian invasion of georgia. we are concerned about authoritarian tendencies in russia. the dialogue cannot therefore be only positive, and as a member of the council of europe russia can do better. but nevertheless, with all due respect to the rapporteur, where is the reset in his story? continuing to polarize now that the u.s. government is moving to a new approach to russia is counterproductive. our problems are global, and to solve them, we need everyone.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, on monday the daughter of a friend of mine received a letter. she got rejected for college. not because she's intellectually incapable, but because she's disabled. according to the letter, the university can't provide her with the care she needs. she did well in high school, so she could. now she's on the sidelines. the report we are discussing today touches the heart of our society. do we want people to be second-class citizens because of their age, their sexual orientation, their religion or belief or disability, or do we choose a society in which everyone can participate fully? it is not only the people themselves who are wronged when they are denied a house or a loan because of who they are, but society as a whole is wronged by writing people off. i've been looking forward to this day with great anticipation. there's a lot at stake in the vote tomorrow. since 1995, the european parliament has been calling for european directives on equal treatment for men and women, and in the treaty of amsterdam we have finally obtained a legal basis for this. in 2000, important guidelines were issued: the directive on equal treatment irrespective of racial or ethnic origin, whose scope includes both the labour market and the supply of goods and services, and the directive on discrimination on grounds of sexual orientation, age, disability and religion or belief, which is confined to the labour market. this was beginning to change, because discrimination against women is also prohibited in many areas. as a parliament we have always opposed this hierarchy of discriminatory grounds that has arisen. because why is it that a person can be denied a loan because he's gay, but not because he's black? the protection must be equivalent. we have been advocating this horizontal directive together and there are differences between us: i think that the commission has a duty to do more than just to give the people what they want. but so far a large majority of parliament has wanted to see the present skewed situation corrected and that is the message we must give to the council tomorrow. i hope for the greatest possible majority. there are many people i would like to thank for their contribution to the report. first of all, the rapporteurs for opinion, and in particular liz lynne of the committee on social affairs. many of her suggestions are now in the text. i also want to thank the shadow reporters patrick gaubert, emine bozkurt, sophia in the field and yvonne kaufmann. in english, we have a saying "jumping over your own shadow", which means you want to look beyond the point you've always hammered, a good point for shadow reporters. i think we've done that. i am very proud of the compromise which was adopted by an overwhelming majority in the libe committee. it's getting better. i want to thank a lot of other people who have contributed to this, but one in particular, and that's michael cashman. michael, i want to thank you for all the advice you've given me, for the lobbying, and also for the inspiration and friendship over the years. the contents now: the report states that discrimination on the four grounds is prohibited. we had already done so in the labour market, but now we are doing so in the provision of goods and services, social protection - including social security and health care - and education. but not every difference is also seen as discrimination. for example, insurance companies may still discriminate on the basis of age and disability, provided they can objectively justify this. many disabled people need to be provided for, but there are limits to reasonableness. so there is a conditional derogation, but the rule is equal treatment, and that is what the vote will be on tomorrow. do we think that europe is only a market, or is it also a source of civilization? i must say that amendment no 81 at least shows where mr weber and 41 others stand. you do not want equal treatment legislation anyway, because no matter how i try to compromise, you are simply opposed to an anti-discrimination law on principle. that's why you're not amending it, but you're taking the whole proposal off the table. then our paths part here, too, there's no middle ground. we shall see tomorrow which way the majority of this house will go.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action against discrimination. we have a rather strange situation at the moment. there are differences in protection against discrimination. it is not possible to explain why a person who is black and gay is protected by the law against discrimination outside the workplace on the basis of the colour of his skin, but not on the basis of his sexual orientation. tomorrow we can show that we as a european parliament will no longer tolerate discrimination on grounds of age, disability, sexual orientation or religious belief. europe is for everyone. it is not possible that someone who wants to rent a car or a house will be refused on the basis of his religion. and people in wheelchairs must be able to use the keys of the atm machine or have access to a train or station. it is not possible to explain why someone over 65 can be in the red for thousands of euros at a bank, but cannot get a modest loan from the same bank. today we all get a day older. think about it . it's about things that will affect us in the future. these differences of opinion did not make the negotiations easy, but we can be proud of the result that came out of the libe committee, to which all the parties committed themselves. the proposal is reasonable and realistic. in the case of people with disabilities, adjustments may be necessary to provide them with access to goods and services, for example, but it does mean that they can again participate actively in society. these adjustments should not be disproportionately burdensome and due account has also been taken of a good implementation timeframe. adjustments do not have to be made immediately. we do not expect member states to adapt railway stations directly. we do ask the member states to consider accessibility for people with disabilities in future building and transport designs. and i cannot stress enough how important this report will be to the citizens of europe, the people it is about. please remember that according to eurobarometer, 87% of europeans would like to see measures to address the discriminatory grounds for this directive. so are your constituents, mr. weber. our socialist group is very pleased with the proposals against the problem of multiple discrimination which are now part of this report. can you imagine a black woman in a wheelchair feeling discriminated against? very few countries have the concept of multiple discrimination. in most cases, the woman would have to choose the grounds on which she was discriminated against when filing a complaint. it is more likely that she is not discriminated against on one ground only, but that the various grounds are interrelated. this woman must be able to complain and to get justice. we therefore call on parliament to maintain these important provisions. i ask you, colleagues, to support this directive. this is an opportunity for parliament to say clearly and unequivocally that discrimination must no longer be tolerated and that the european parliament attaches equal importance to the rights of all its citizens. let's take that step.
i would like to thank the rapporteur for her excellent report, which i think is a very good one. my group is very pleased that, almost five years after mr barroso's promise, a proposal for a directive is finally on the table. discrimination is contrary to the european treaties, the charter of fundamental rights and the convention on human rights. but treaties and solemn declarations are of little use in court. european citizens must have an instrument to enforce their rights. that is the raison d'être of the european union, mr weber, not the milk quotas or the procurement rules or the structural funds, but a european area where everyone can make his own life as he sees fit and in freedom. a single european area, where everyone is equal before the law, has equal opportunities in society and is treated with respect. a directive is not enough, but it is a prerequisite. this directive is about europe as a community of values, and values cannot be negotiated by 27 governments in the usual exchange of national interests. we define values together as citizens, in open debate, and the european parliament is the appropriate arena for that. yes, mr. weber, some issues are very sensitive, particularly the grounds of sexual orientation and religion. but we have a responsibility to all citizens, all european citizens, because europe must not become an animal farm: "all europeans are equal, but some europeans are more equal than others". freedom of religion and conscience are fundamental rights for which i will go on the barricades. in a free europe, everyone must be free to follow his or her own beliefs. that is the cornerstone of democracy. but freedom of religion should not be misused as a license to discriminate against others. yesterday, the european agency for fundamental rights published its second report on homophobia in europe. it is shameful that in europe in 2009 millions of people still have to fear discrimination, hatred, violence and even murder, purely because of their sexual orientation. i can reassure manfred weber: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the right to marry is and remains a national competence and this directive does not alter this. but in 21st century europe, banning marriage on the basis of religion, race or sexual orientation is an anomaly. many people find it perfectly acceptable for the government to ban marriage or partnership between two adults of the same sex. but would we accept - as has happened in history - that the government should prohibit marriage between jews and non-jews, between catholics and protestants, between whites and blacks? that is unacceptable. ladies and gentlemen, please vote for this report in the interests of the citizens whom we all represent. compromise is not ideal for anyone, including us. but let's, as kathalijne says, jump over our own shadow. finally, i urge the council to follow parliament's recommendations. each member state has its own issues, but the european parliament is showing that differences can be bridged and that we can agree on rights for all european citizens.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). one of those freedoms is the freedom of education. an important freedom is the choice of school for their children by parents. christian schools and institutions in my country consciously choose to formulate an admissions policy consistent with the identity of that school. in the netherlands, there is scope for an admission policy in accordance with the foundation of the school. requirements may be made which are necessary to achieve the purpose and basis. parents choose a school that is principled in that regard and that takes the bible seriously. this is a consequence of freedom of religion and belief for parents, in the interests of their child's education. amendments nos 29 and 51 restrict the freedom of schools to make their own choices in principle, and i also share the view of mr weber and others. this proposal is not in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. apart from the administrative problems, this seems to me to be sufficient reason to reject the commission's proposal. i shall vote against the report by mr buitenweg. i hope that other groups will also see that this is a serious infringement of the freedoms of our citizens. if we value the freedom of choice of parents, we will not allow it to be restricted.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). since the regulation came into force, complaints have been made about its unclear and unreasonable application. that is where action must be taken. the regulation needs to be revised quickly. what's better than that? the legislation must be predictable. i am not advocating the harmonization of all sanctions. that should remain a competence of the member states. i do advocate a clear, predictable and reasonable system. it is the only way to avoid completely irrational fines, unreasonable fines and extremely long fines. the current legislation seems to allow for discrimination against mainly foreign truck drivers on european roads, and that is unacceptable. this problem is nothing less than a serious disturbance of the internal market. finally, a brief example of the unreasonableness of the current regulation on driving and rest periods. a driver drives through france and is fined 750 euros for not getting 15 minutes of rest. the fine will also take six hours to process. this means that the driver cannot load and unload that day, with all the consequences of that. the actual damage of the fine for the 15-minute violation is thus approximately eur 1,750. the commission must take a much tougher line against member states which are guilty of such distortions of the internal market. from the dutch perspective, i think it is mainly in the southern direction.
