president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on transport, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97) for a regulation amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). the first question is: what is the government doing and what are we letting the market do through tendering? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. new rules on the carriage of passengers by bus must not lead to the disturbance of the freedom of local authorities to organise their own public transport. the choice is always between two alternatives as regards passenger rights. the first is to emphasise maximum information on the continuation of the journey in other member states, the availability of cross-border transport licences and well-regulated long-distance connections, so as to avoid any unpleasant surprises during the journey. the other emphasizes ex post financial compensation for delays and interruptions in services. i always support the first, but parliament votes in the majority for the second, especially since the same choice has been made for air transport. the increased attention to people with disabilities is an important reason to support the proposals, even if they are not perfect.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. he has produced a good report in close cooperation with the shadow rapporteurs. the report makes it very clear that urban mobility is a part of the transport sector where there are many challenges and opportunities. challenges in the field of european climate objectives, congestion reduction, road safety and user-friendliness, and opportunities in the field of sustainable economic development and, closely related to this, the growth of inland navigation. the report is of excellent quality and rightly takes account of the principle of subsidiarity, so i should like to briefly touch on the relationship between urban mobility and the inland waterway sector. sustainable economic development in europe, including in transport and urban areas, will not be possible to a large extent without the use of inland waterways. many european cities have inland waterways and therefore the capacity to meet growing transport demand in a sustainable way. the growth of inland waterway transport does not require major infrastructure investments, does not contribute to congestion in european cities and does not lead to an increase in environmental and climate problems in european cities, provided that inland waterway transport has clean engines and clean fuel. if inland waterway capacity is naturally present in european cities, it must be used and stimulated. the future of urban mobility is thus closely linked to the future of inland navigation. i therefore ask the european commission to keep the interests of inland waterway transport in mind when drawing up new legislation on urban mobility.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. i am grateful to the european commission for coming forward with proposals to lower the thresholds for this fund and i am glad that mr stockmann has taken up these proposals with a sense of urgency. i would like to thank the commissioner for his report. there is, however, a problem. this reduction is not yet large enough. in the committee on transport, mr wortmann-kool and i have successfully remedied this problem. i regret, however, that mr stockmann considers this amendment no 24 to be undesirable. i know him to be a great supporter of inland navigation and i had hoped that he would be pleased with such amendments. the commission's proposed threshold is still far too high for the small businessman, which is almost by definition the inland shipowner. i do not understand why the other institutions are opposed to amendment no 24. we have a fund with a lot of money for sustainable transport. inland waterway transport is by far the cleanest mode of transport. why should we not lower the threshold for this sector even further? i think the european commission is afraid that such proposals will encourage some member states in the council to demand other reductions as well. i would ask the european commission to keep its back straight and to recognise unequivocally the importance of inland waterway transport as the cleanest mode of transport here too.
for the pse group crossborder freight trains are moving at an average speed of 18 km per hour across the european union. in 2007, only 60% of all freight trains arrived on time. - why? - why? because international rail freight transport is still very inefficiently organized. the rail freight transport can of course never compete with road transport in this way. nor will it affect europe's environmental objectives and our commitment to reduce co2 emissions by 20% by 2020. the european commission's proposal aims to create a competitive rail freight network in the european union. this can be achieved by creating cross-border corridors, by increasing cooperation between infrastructure managers and better coordination of investments between member states and by better agreeing on priority in the event of delays. this will greatly improve the capacity and competitiveness of the rail network. the commission's original proposal to give priority to freight trains in the event of delays went too far. but i have, thanks to good cooperation with the rapporteur, reached an excellent compromise which provides flexibility and a pragmatic approach. unfortunately, the albertini proposal leaves this important aspect of the proposal empty-handed. the social democrats will therefore vote against amendment no 71. however, the proposal as it stands will encourage people to cooperate and will give users and market players a say in planning and implementation. it is time to finally work together on a genuine single market for rail transport and thus invest in a green, sustainable future.
this directive concerns patients. i cannot stress this enough, because we have of course talked about a lot of other things in the context of crossborder health care: the free movement of medical services and what should happen to the health care market. we are not talking about that very explicitly. we are talking about a pragmatic approach. how can we make the system work for the benefit of the patients? and if we don't, who will? patients are in such a vulnerable position! you don't want people who are sick to have to fight another battle with cold-blooded health bureaucrats, who look at health policy on their spreadsheets, on their computers with numbers and statistics. that can't be the case. it is therefore also a social directive. crossborder care has of course been possible for a long time for those who can afford it, but for those who cannot afford it something must also be done. that is what we are talking about today, mr president. it is also for this reason that our group attaches importance to prior authorization, which is of course a crucial element here, madam minister. i am very grateful to the council for being here today. prior authorization, not to make cross-border care impossible, not at all. but it is to prevent serious undermining of national systems. we agree with that and we go further than we might normally have done. however, a compromise is necessary. we do believe that exceptions for rare diseases or life-threatening situations on waiting lists are necessary. we would like to see the definition of hospital care adopted at european level and not in the individual member states, simply to ensure legal certainty for patients and security for those national systems. we also believe that patients who are already so ill should not be subjected to a lengthy legal procedure if something goes wrong, but that a system of a european patient ombudsman should be set up. we have of course had a conflict in the committee on health and i call on the colleagues on the left to put that ideology aside and to ensure that we come up with a good directive for patients and a pragmatic approach. i have listened with great respect to what mrs roth-behrendt has said here. finally, a big thank you indeed to rapporteur john bowis. he has done a great job and i wish him well from here.
i would also like to thank mr bowis very much. he has worked very hard on this report and i hope he will be better very soon. i must say, however, that under the misleading label of 'patient rights', the european commission is trying to introduce market forces in health care for the whole of europe. my group is naturally in favour of more patient rights and patient mobility in border regions. we go much further than that. we believe that everyone, rich and poor, has a right to have good access to good quality health care. i have very serious problems, however, with the fact that the proposal is based on the internal market article, which already indicates that economic interests are overridden by the interests of patients. the proposal is also superfluous. the reimbursement of costs has already been arranged. the fact that some insurance companies and member states do not comply with this should be regulated better. the proposal also disproportionately interferes with a matter within the competence of a member state, thereby leading to people with large pockets having access to better care. the proposed system of fees and charges in accordance with the rules of the country of origin introduces a form of patient mobility which is contrary to the principle of equal access to health care for all. the proposal also carries a great risk that it will not be the patient's right to seek care abroad, but that insurance companies or member states may oblige the patient to go to the cheapest provider. it becomes a duty rather than a right of the patient. the fact that we have 27 different health care systems in 27 countries means that the commission's proposal, which is based solely on article 95, the infamous harmonization article, will lead to the dismantling of national health care systems and thus remove the responsibilities of the member states. we are for equal access to patients as a starting point and not for more market in care.
the report on patients' rights in crossborder healthcare has been the subject of much work over the past few months. i thank the rapporteur, mr bowis, for all his efforts and wish him a speedy recovery. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the healthcare sector. we must be careful not to go too far. health care should remain the prerogative of the member states. cooperation at eu level should not be at the expense of quality of care and of fundamental ethical choices made by member states. the diversity of ethics must be safeguarded and i am therefore pleased that this issue has been included in the report. i think the legal basis is a very difficult point. i find it unfortunate that article 95 has been chosen. the committee on legal affairs also gave its opinion. in my view, this is contrary to subsidiarity in this area of policy and makes it difficult for member states to make independent choices without the intervention of the court of justice.
president. - i have received the following motions for resolutions: the directive puts the patient at the centre, the able citizen who can and wants to choose the best treatment, preferably close by, but also far away if it is distant. we owe this legislation to very brave citizens of the european union who have gone to court to get that care across the border where their own member state has failed them in the matter of good treatment. the court of justice has found them in the right. today we are recording the decision of the court of justice and we are also formulating the conditions under which this right to crossborder care for this able-bodied citizen can be exercised. president. - this directive is great news for all people in border regions, great news for people with rare diseases, great news for people facing waiting lists, because in september the hips are going to run out. these people have a choice now. we have improved the information system, we have defined the rules for reimbursement, we have established european reference networks which ensure that the quality of care is improved. we have arranged for dispute settlement through an ombudsman, who could do otherwise. i refer to the dutch model for dispute settlement in health care which has recently been launched. we have the possibility of pilot areas which we in the border region of limburg, where i come from, would like to participate in. we also established that the health systems in the member states will not be affected. the citizens will have the right to choose, and i think that freedom of choice is very important.
i would like to begin by thanking john bowis for his very dedicated and committed commitment to public health and patient mobility and i would like to wish him a speedy recovery. health care is a national competence, but there are links with europe. patients are aware of treatment options in other countries and want to use facilities in other countries. this is especially true for patients in border regions or if there are long waiting lists in one's own country. there is nothing wrong with seeking care and treatment in other countries, but it does require proper arrangements. first of all, no forced health tourism. insurance companies should not be pressuring patients to accept cheap care elsewhere. secondly, there must be minimum guarantees of quality. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. thirdly - and this is very important - member states should retain the right to require prior authorization. health care is not a free market. to maintain facilities, planning is needed, and hospitals need to know what patient flows to expect. the most important thing for me is that this directive will help to ensure that treatment methods in particular cross the border. there is a great disparity between member states, but it is not solved by sending patients across the border, but by exchanging treatments, and this directive can contribute to that.
on behalf of the pse group, i would like to thank mr trakatellis for his excellent report. if there is one area where cooperation in europe is useful and adds value, it is in the field of rare diseases. in the case of rare metabolic diseases, muscle diseases, but also rare cancers, it is practical and useful to work together to exchange information on treatment techniques and pool forces. all very important. that information must also be made available and all these cases are covered by the trakatellis report. i would like to draw your attention to three points. first, patients must have a voice in europe. in recent years we have seen more and more well-organised patient groups finding their way to europe, to brussels. this is all very important and informative for politicians, because many of these rare diseases are, of course, very unknown. however, it is good to look at how these patient organisations are funded and it is very important to ensure that these organisations are independently funded and not dependent on the pharmaceutical industry. that is why i am calling for a system of funding for these patient organisations. secondly, the development of medicines for rare diseases, the so-called orphan medicines, is of crucial importance. we have guidelines for this, but it would be good to pay some more attention to it to see if it really works well. thirdly, a controversial subject which has also been raised by peter liese. many rare diseases are hereditary. research and embryo selection can prevent a great deal of suffering, but it is important that member states continue to decide for themselves on possible treatments such as pre-implementation and embryo selection. we support amendment no 15, but we want to remove the reference to the eradication of diseases. that, as peter liese has said, evokes very unpleasant associations. we also think it is important that treatment should be voluntary and within the limits set by national governments. if these conditions are met, we are in favour of that amendment and we are actually calling on everyone to support that amendment, but to remove the reference to the eradication of diseases. in those circumstances we can fully agree with the report and we are enthusiastic about the work of mr trakatellis. thank you for your attention.
i have a point of order under rule 168 (2). our group has tabled an amendment on the change of the legal basis, and a number of other groups have done the same. the change is that only article 95 on the internal market and therefore economic interests will now be taken as the legal basis and not article 152 on public health, which takes the patient as the starting point. in principle, the committee on the environment has asked the committee on legal affairs for an opinion, but this was only based on the original commission proposal. the bowis report has now clearly been amended, which brings patients' rights into the report and thus changes the legal basis. but now these amendments are not accepted and the fundamental change which parliament wants to make - from being market-oriented to including patient rights - is being jeopardized. i would ask that the report be referred back to the committee on the environment.
in writing. − we voted against the casaca report on the 2007 discharge of the european parliament for three reasons. firstly, we are against the situation that the meps voluntary pension fund is being funded by taxpayers’ money. secondly, we are against the situation that taxpayers’ money can be given to a private pension fund where the list of participants and beneficiaries is kept secret and is not published. thirdly, we are completely against using even more taxpayers’ money to cover the pension fund’s current deficit as a result of speculative investments. we support paragraphs 105 and 109 of the casaca report, which remedy some objections we have concerning the voluntary pension fund for meps, but because the casaca report does not change the existing situation, we have voted against giving discharge in respect of the implementation of the european parliament’s budget for the financial year 2007.
in writing. - i regret that i missed the beginning of the vote, but i fully share the thrust of the reports on discharge and the casaca report in particular. it would be irresponsible, especially in these times, to use taxpayers' money to supplement deficits in the pension fund. any shortfall in the fund is a matter for the fund and its members, not for the european taxpayer. parliamentarians have a role model and must be careful in the way they handle community funds. this applies to their income, pensions and allowances. i am therefore pleased that parliament has today adopted the report.
in writing. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has also called for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the directive is implemented in a uniform manner. we voted in favour of the amendments which seek to give 100% of the revenue from the extension to artists. the compromise agreed is a step in the right direction, but it is certainly not yet optimal. the pvda is therefore seriously concerned about the position of smaller artists who, in exchange for the recording of a record, have to give up the income from the recording that exceeds the advance. we therefore hope that the european commission will come forward in the near future with proposals which will structurally improve the position of artists vis-à-vis record companies, including contracts covering the first 50 years of the neighbouring rights.
in writing. − more and more crossborder long-distance freight transport has shifted from rail to road. the main reasons for this are the increasing amount of motorway space, the abolition of direct rail links to businesses and the relative decline in the cost of road transport. those causes are usually forgotten. all the attention is on two other reasons. one is that there is a lack of coordination between railway companies in the different member states, which means that freight wagons have to wait unnecessarily long before being coupled behind a locomotive that will take them further. the solution is already in place in the form of fixed-shift shuttle trains. the other criticism is that this transport is slow because it has to wait for passenger trains to take priority. the duchoň report sought to abolish this priority for passenger transport. on busy routes, this may mean that the eu imposes an obligation to break through fixed hourly schedules by cancelling some trains. the voters will soon know that they owe this deterioration to europe. instead of restricting passenger transport, the railway bottlenecks and capacity shortages need to be addressed. it is good that the text has been toned down on this.
in writing. - the vvd voted against the discharge to the european commission. the vvd believes that the commission has made too little progress in encouraging the introduction of the national declaration in the member states. so far, only four countries have done so, including the netherlands. the vvd also believes that the european member states still make too many mistakes in spending european money, as has been shown by the european court of auditors' audits. the court of auditors issued a negative opinion on rural development, cohesion and structural policies, among other issues. the vvd believes that the control systems in these areas need to be improved. progress in the last five years has been too little.
the international coverage of the unrest and its aftermath in moldova at the beginning of april raises a number of questions which the european institutions should address to the moldovan and romanian authorities. starting with chisinau. does the moldovan government really intend to open fire on protesters in an emergency? watch the prime minister's announcement on state television. and how does the moldovan government explain the radical change in the national security agencies' attitude towards protesters and other opponents? a shift from incomprehensible passivity towards vandalism, arson and looting of government buildings to brutal physical violence against defenseless civilians, with three dubious deaths. above all, how is the republic of moldova discharging its responsibility for these violations of basic fundamental rights? in order to answer this last crucial question, madam president, we must certainly take the open letter with nine recommendations from 14 moldovan civil society defenders to the czech presidency yesterday. i hope that the european institutions, commissioner rehn and the czech presidency will take note of this. prominent figures in moldovan society are asking for clarification. we must confront the moldovan authorities with this. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the situation in the country, and to provide information on the situation in the country. the european consensus on the far-reaching consequences of such a major decision is certainly warranted.
the president-in-office of the council. - madam president, madam member, ladies and gentlemen, i am grateful to the european parliament for hosting this important debate this afternoon. i read with great interest both mrs anna ibrisagić's report on the future stability and prosperity of the balkans and mrs doris pack's motion for a resolution on bosnia and herzegovina. the council can agree with much of what is stated in the report and we share many of the views and concerns expressed in relation to the situation in bosnia and herzegovina. i would like to focus my opening remarks directly on bosnia and herzegovina, both because its stability is crucial for the future of the western balkans as a whole and because the current situation continues to raise considerable concerns. the council has been active both in formulating and implementing a strategy to promote the security and integrity of bosnia and herzegovina and in supporting the reforms it needs to secure a peaceful and prosperous future. therefore, i cannot accept the claim that the council is not paying sufficient attention to bosnia and herzegovina. we all know that we are still living with the consequences of the tragic events of the 1990s, of which mrs ibrisagić spoke. bosnia and herzegovina, which for decades had been a symbol of the peaceful coexistence of peoples, cultures and religions, has become a zone of devastating conflict. the union's policy since then has been to work towards stability and reconciliation based on the promise of a european perspective for the entire balkan region. nevertheless, we continue to witness often strong nationalist rhetoric aimed at cementing national divisions in bosnia and herzegovina and preventing national reconciliation. time has not yet resolved these differences, nor has it healed the wounds between the three constituent peoples of bosnia and herzegovina. however, it is surprising that this nationalist rhetoric and these attitudes are accompanied by a common interest of all bosnian communities and their political representatives in the european future of bosnia and herzegovina. the people of bosnia and herzegovina are simply seeking a safer life in prosperity. it wants to move forward and relies on its country's integration into european and wider structures as a guarantee of its future stability. although local political leaders talk a lot about bosnia and herzegovina's future in the eu, their actions hardly indicate a real commitment to this agenda. this conflict between the interest in the european direction of the country and nationalism creates a real risk that while the rest of the western balkan region is moving forward, bosnia and herzegovina, mired in internal strife, will be left behind. concerns about these developments for the future of bosnia and herzegovina have kept it at the forefront of our agenda and in our permanent attention. bosnia and herzegovina has been and remains the subject of intensive negotiations at all levels of the council. the commission and the council secretariat are deepening contacts with their partners in the country in an effort to move the political process forward and help bosnia and herzegovina to keep pace with the rest of the region. member states shall complement this european programme through their own efforts at bilateral level. we also very much appreciate the attention this parliament is giving to bosnia and herzegovina. and i would like to express my gratitude to the many members present who have given their support to all the work that has been done to promote stability and political maturity in bosnia and herzegovina. the eu continues to strive for a european perspective for the whole region as well as for bosnia and herzegovina. however, meeting the eu accession criteria requires considerable effort. it means developing a consensus approach and a willingness to make far-reaching changes. it's not something that can be accomplished in a day. it means nothing less than a complete political, economic and also social transformation. bosnia and herzegovina needs to make significant changes to its internal structures and decision-making processes. we are disappointed by the lack of progress within the bosnian council of ministers and the parliamentary assembly. they fall far short of what is necessary. state authorities urgently need to strengthen and improve their work in a way that starts to deliver real results, including substantial progress on the eu-related agenda. this is fundamental, as the union can only deal with bosnia and herzegovina as a whole, not with individual parts. moreover, the priorities of the european partnership are clear. the union is always ready to help, but it cannot and will not take over the work that belongs to politicians in bosnia and herzegovina. despite the continuation of nationalist political agendas, we are aware that compromise and agreement are possible in bosnia and herzegovina. we have seen this before, for example with the adoption of two police laws which paved the way for the signing of the stabilisation and association agreement, or the brcko solution which fulfilled one of the essential objectives set by the peacebuilding council. however, even in these cases, progress was always made and agreements were reached only at the last minute and under considerable pressure from the international community. it takes a much more advanced approach. it is of the utmost importance that the local political leadership shows responsibility and initiative and awareness of who bosnia and herzegovina really belongs to and who is really responsible for its future. the people of bosnia and herzegovina deserve a better result for the votes cast in the ballot box. this is an aspect that you as members can help to strengthen more than anyone else. such a development would lead to a significant shift in the international community's presence in bosnia and herzegovina. change is necessary. after so many years since the signing of the peace agreement, bosnia and herzegovina must now stand on its own two feet, end its 'protectorate' mentality and become a credible full-fledged state. to achieve this goal, the peace council steering committee has drawn up, on behalf of the international community, a list of five objectives and two conditions that bosnia and herzegovina must meet before any changes can take place. it's a real test of maturity, fully supported by the eu. the 5+2 list is not just another set of additional conditions. it is a carefully drawn up list of requirements which are essential if bosnia and herzegovina is to be transformed into a modern and complete state, allowing the presence of the office of the high representative to end. every modern country needs a properly functioning legal system, effective tax authorities, the resolution of all issues related to state property, and equal access to the constitutional court for all citizens. we have already welcomed many times the initiative of the three political leaders in prud last november to work together to promote the development of bosnia and herzegovina. we support the agreements reached and encourage these political representatives to continue their efforts with a view to the next meeting of the peace council steering committee at the end of june. we believe, above all, that the unresolved issues of state property have a solution and must not become an obstacle to a solution. however, initiatives at the political level require wider support. for this reason, i would like to call on society in bosnia and herzegovina as a whole to be part of the reform effort. the media in particular should play a more constructive role. the eu's message is clear. it is of the utmost importance that the political leadership in bosnia and herzegovina cooperate even more closely to overcome historical divisions and to lead their country towards closer integration within europe. the eu will remain ready to assist in this effort, which is essential not only for bosnia and herzegovina itself but also for the wider stability and security of the region. i know that we can count on the support of the members of this parliament in this process, and i am grateful to you, madam president, madam member and members of this house, for that support.
member of the commission. − madam president, i am glad that the western balkans are back on the european parliament’s agenda this week. in recent years a steady stabilisation in the region has taken place, not least thanks to the region’s european perspective, with the ultimate goal being eu membership once the conditions have been met by every country. croatia’s negotiations are quite far advanced. the former yugoslav republic of macedonia is a candidate looking forward to opening its negotiations, and we have a network of stabilisation and association agreements in place. stability in kosovo was maintained throughout last year’s important developments. we must not put these achievements at risk through any kind of complacency or distraction by other – sometimes perhaps more urgent – matters. many will have questions about eu enlargement in the middle of an economic crisis, and discussions are likely to intensify as we approach the next elections for this parliament. this is understandable and i, for one, feel the pain of our citizens concerning their future, jobs and welfare. at the same time, let us not make eu enlargement a scapegoat for something it is not responsible for. we should not make it a scapegoat for our own domestic economic and social problems. therefore, a well-informed public debate is essential to keep us all engaged and making progress in this important region. there have been calls for consolidation of the european union. this is exactly what we have been doing for the past years since the renewed consensus on enlargement which was adopted by the european council and endorsed by the european parliament in december 2006. the key of this renewed consensus is not to take on new commitments but to stick to the existing commitments and respect them. in other words, if the countries of the western balkans fulfil the established conditions, they can move towards eu membership. in this context, i very much welcome mrs ibrisagic’s report. it rightly emphasises the fundamental importance of offering the western balkans a european future. it is the main driving force of much-needed reform and greater stability in the western balkans. ten years after the horrific events in kosovo, we should remind ourselves of the power of the european perspective. it still helps today to consolidate stability and peace in a region that is, effectively, our own front yard – not back yard, but front yard. we cannot take a sabbatical from our work for peace and stability in the continent. while the european union pursues its own institutional reform, we need to keep on working in parallel on a carefully managed and gradual accession process in the western balkans that strengthens both the institutions and civil society there. the accession negotiations with croatia have, until recently, been going well. this is why the commission proposed, in november 2008, an indicative road map to reach the final stage of accession negotiations by the end of 2009, provided croatia fulfils the conditions. there is still plenty of work to do, and many reforms need to be stepped up by croatia. unfortunately, the negotiations are currently blocked because of the border dispute between croatia and slovenia. this is a bilateral issue which has de facto become a european problem. since january, working closely with the czech presidency and the trio of the czech, french and swedish governments, i have taken the initiative to help facilitate a solution. the aim is to find a solution to the border issue and allow for the continuation of croatia’s eu accession negotiations. this is still work in progress and we have needed a great deal of patience and determination to keep up the momentum and progress. we had a full day of talks yesterday with the foreign ministers of slovenia and croatia, as well as of the trio countries. i want to believe that we are close to finding a way forward and overcoming these obstacles so that we can continue croatia’s eu accession negotiations shortly. concerning the former yugoslav republic of macedonia, i welcome the overall satisfactory conduct of the presidential and municipal elections. over the past months we have insisted on the importance of these elections for the country’s european future. the country has responded positively to our message, thus confirming its willingness to move forward in its accession process. however, the key priorities for reform should not be forgotten. it is indeed now time to step up efforts in order to meet the benchmarks which have been set for the opening of accession negotiations. i would like to thank doris pack for her motion and welcome the opportunity to discuss bosnia and herzegovina with you today, at a very crucial moment. last year, bosnia and herzegovina made progress on the path of european integration, especially through the signature of the saa and the entry into force of the interim agreement. there have been positive developments in recent months as well, including the prud agreement, progress on the brcko issue and steps towards a census in 2011. also, the implementation of the saa interim agreement is broadly on track. while we need to be firm on the fulfilment of the ‘5+2’ conditions for the closure of the office of the high representative, there is now a possibility that they could met in the coming months. recent steps towards the establishment of a state property inventory are also positive in this regard. however, here, as for the rest of the region, there is no room for complacency. overall reform has remained slow, including on the key eu priorities, and challenges remain. nationalist rhetoric is still very present, creating unnecessary political tensions. this needs to change if bosnia and herzegovina wants to continue its progress towards the european union and avoid falling behind its neighbours. the serbian government remains committed to advancing on its european agenda, and there have been a number of positive developments recently. it will be essential, however, as the country is increasingly feeling the negative effects of the global financial crisis, that key reform measures are not overlooked. the process of structural adjustment must continue and the country needs to follow through its commitments, particularly in the area of judiciary and the rule of law. we are currently examining ways in which to alleviate the impact of the financial crisis, working closely together with my colleague, joaquín almunia. for instance, we are looking at our ipa programme, for which we are considering converting part of the 2009 national envelope into direct budgetary support, also with the support of the international financial institutions. we appreciate parliament’s sustained support for the eu’s efforts in kosovo, which remains a european priority and central to regional stability. the european council has repeatedly confirmed that kosovo shares a european perspective with the rest of the western balkans. the council has asked the commission to use community instruments to promote economic and political development and to propose measures to advance in that direction. this autumn, the commission will present a study to this effect. we shall examine how kosovo can progress as part of the wider region towards integration with the european union in the context of the stabilisation and association process. finally, looking at 2009 overall and the western balkans region as a whole, there has been quite good progress in the area of visa liberalisation, which proves to my mind that, when the incentives are right, the countries respond with effective reforms. this is most likely the single eu policy area that matters most to the ordinary people – the ordinary citizens – of the western balkans. we hope to table a proposal for visa-free travel by the end of the czech presidency for those countries that are most advanced in this field and have fulfilled the established conditions. this could enable decisions by the council to achieve visa-free travel for the most advanced countries by the end of 2009. dear friends, i count on your support on this essential visa issue as well as, more broadly, on the european perspective of the western balkans.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on international trade. the committee on international trade, in its opinion on the laudatory report by mr ibrisagic, underlines the importance of a tangible prospect of eu membership for the political and economic development of the western balkan states. however, when the region itself has a monopoly of market power in key economic sectors, this situation creates a double blockade, especially when it is accompanied by party political interconnection. internal development is stagnating and european firms are being left out. the unimpeded operation of delta holding in serbia with its influential director miroslav mišković as its 'octopus' is a prime example. the commissioner met with him in october. commission, what action have you taken so far against belgrade? as early as may 2007, a leaked report from the us embassy in the country called for an urgent end to the monopoly of delta holding, for serbia's well-being and for serbia's european integration. the commissioner spoke of an engine for development. well, there's a lot of sand in that serbian engine.
i shall only deal with the subject of bosnia and the resolution by mrs pack, which we shall be very happy to support tomorrow. talking about bosnia is always frustrating and i am glad that the commissioner has also managed to mention some positive points about developments in bosnia. yet, you may wonder at times whether the glass is half full or half empty. i wonder where the glass went when you talk about bosnia. mr swoboda has just said that a problem with the developments there is that there is a feeling that whatever changes are made, they are not acted upon. i actually get the opposite impression sometimes when i talk to people there, which is that they say, "even if we don't change, we'll still join because they want us so badly". i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. if reforms are made and if we start working on a proper legal system and on fighting bureaucracy, then the european perspective is real. but if it doesn't, it's not. that message must be made clear, and i think mrs pack's resolution does that very well.
bosnia-herzegovina is a mini-yugoslavia, a federation of peoples who have to choose between living together peacefully or fighting each other over territory. since the break-up of yugoslavia in 1992, attempts have been made to make bosnia-herzegovina a unitary state, but they have not succeeded. i expect that this will not be possible in the near or distant future. the agreement between the three peoples and their political leaders on effective governance will only be possible when no one feels threatened by the others or by the outside world. only after the withdrawal of the high representative of the european union and the withdrawal of foreign troops from this country will an internal compromise be possible. until then, the stagnation will continue. that is why i do not agree with the resolution on this country, which can only lead to the continuation of the protectorate and therefore to stagnation. we must bear in mind that bosnia-herzegovina is mainly inhabited by three peoples, none of whom are the majority in this country, some of whom feel a connection with serbia, some with croatia and some of whom want to emphasize their own bosnian identity. we must take that into account.
i would like to thank the rapporteur first of all. i admire and appreciate his commitment and his commitment, and i also greatly appreciate his speed. i think it would be better if we could move things along a little faster, and it is a pity that he is being held back. europe is now struggling with an economic crisis, but in fact we have been struggling for many years with a lack of innovation. other continents are often more innovative than the european union. the united states because it spends much more money on research and development from the public sector, but also from the private sector. japan is very innovative, partly because of its top runner approach. our ecodesign directive is about innovation. it's good for the environment and good for the economy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected. innovation must be carried out in several directions: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents at work. it is not just energy-consuming products that are concerned, but also energy-related products, and i fully agree with the rapporteur: it should be all products. the resources are particularly important. if the earth is to have nearly nine billion inhabitants in about 20 years, and if all of them want a reasonable level of prosperity, then a careful and good management of natural resources is necessary. there are some inspiring concepts, for example the cradle to cradle idea of michael braungart, radical ideas to make full reuse of materials possible. the big question today is: does the ecodesign directive actually work? the most famous example of the ecodesign directive is the ban on incandescent light bulbs, a decision which is not very popular but very positive. unfortunately, there is still some uncertainty about the energy efficiency of led lamps, because it remains somewhat less favourable than the manufacturers did. another sad example of the ecodesign directive is energy labelling. you have aa, a+, a20, a40 now, and all products will soon be a-rated in some way. now, it's not entirely clear what that means, and that's not a good example of ecological design. i am sorry to say, colleagues, that this review cannot solve everything. we are stuck with our own procedures and our own agreements and, indeed, it is a missed opportunity. i hope the evaluation will provide an opportunity for improvement. it is important that the ecodesign directive be thoroughly evaluated in the near future. does it really bring the innovation we want? does it put the european union and european producers at the top of the market? does it save energy? does it minimize waste and use of natural resources? and most importantly: can the operation be extended to non-energy-related products and to all products?
for the pse group product standardisation is not just a technical matter. i was involved in the first standardization 20 years ago from a very different responsibility, namely construction. i must say that i am pleased with the result our colleague mr neris has now achieved. the performance of a construction product should be determined not only on the basis of its technical capabilities and essential characteristics, but also on the basis of the health and safety aspects of the use of such a product throughout its life cycle. we have therefore, as a group, advocated the inclusion of provisions which benefit both the safety and health of workers and users and the environment. i thank the rapporteur for her support. our group believes that all information known to the manufacturer should be included in a declaration of performance, including information on dangerous substances. member states should ensure the correct application of the rules and provide for penalties for infringements, including criminal penalties for serious infringements. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am glad that we have been able to counteract that in this dossier and i hope that we can decide this tomorrow.
- i'm the reporter. − first of all, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their good cooperation. let me put things in perspective. the fight against fraud, although largely the responsibility of the member states, cannot be carried out at national level alone. it must be a priority for the european union and we must ensure close cooperation between the member states and the european commission. since the reform of vat is currently on hold, the commission has concentrated on the so-called classic measure, i.e. changes to vat legislation which make technical improvements but do not fundamentally change the existing system. i agree with the initiative of commissioner kovács because it is the right one. tax fraud leads in particular to breaches of the principle of fair and transparent taxation and can lead to distortions of competition if one company pays vat and the other company does not, in addition to all costs to the public. this affects the internal market, whereby honest companies suffer from competitive disadvantages due to tax fraud. i therefore warmly welcome the commission's efforts to tackle the deliberate abuse of the vat system by gangs seeking to take advantage of shortcomings in the system. vat is an important source of revenue not only for the member states but also for the eu. the european union receives some 20 billion euros in vat revenue. it is estimated that €100 billion of vat is evaded in europe each year. it is not then handed over at the border by the importer. the fraudulent importers and exporters must be dealt with. the european commission, however, goes too far in seeking to hold the bona fide supplier and the fraudulent importer jointly liable. i have therefore tried to increase the legal protection of the bona fide exporter. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods are respected. if member states are given only an additional power to prosecute exporters on a cross-border basis, there will be little incentive for member states to improve administrative cooperation. our amendments seek to avoid any unnecessary attack on a bona fide exporter. therefore, the bona fide exporter should be given a warning two months before the actual inspection, so that he can still prove that he acted in good faith. in this case, he must be contacted through his own tax office and not that of the importing member state. the socialists are calling for a maximum period of five years for the levy. i don't agree with this. the period of vat liability at national level is not harmonised. in belgium, for example, it is three years, except in the case of demonstrable fraud. a longer period of joint and several liability for vat on cross-border supplies is not desirable, as it would require companies to carry out a lot of additional administrative work, leading to high costs for businesses, which are certainly not desirable in the current crisis. in addition, from 2010 onwards, companies will have to submit monthly summary declarations for crossborder intra-eu transactions, with the result that the tax authorities will automatically receive the information necessary to cross-check intra-eu transactions. this information should be used by the tax authorities in an appropriate and targeted manner. why should it be necessary to give them five more years to carry out these cross checks, when they are already receiving monthly data? i am afraid that as a result of a long five-year claim period the tax authorities have acted too late and the fraudsters have disappeared. the recovery actions may then target companies that may have acted in good faith. mr. president, i'm going to close. the fraudulent importer must be dealt with swiftly. the bona fide exporter must be contacted by the tax authorities of the country concerned with two months' notice and a maximum period of two years. this is because the deadline will reduce the administrative burden on the reliable business community as much as possible.
i would like to begin, against my custom, with a political statement, because i find tax evasion and avoidance in this age of rising unemployment and rising income inequality really shameful. the elections will show which parties are really willing to make this work. the report by mr hamon on the taxation of savings income is an example of the issue at hand. i realize that it is difficult for the commissioner to get real agreement on this with the unanimity rule. the pse group has more ambition than was in the commission proposal and that has been incorporated in a number of amendments which we have tabled. a final comment on mrs lulling's contrast between the two systems of information exchange and withholding tax. i don't think that's the point. it is about the loopholes in the law in both systems and those are the points we must concentrate on. the commission has taken steps, but more needs to be done than what is now in the report.
first of all, i would like to thank baroness ludford for this excellent report. the report could have been stronger, but it is an asset in the debate on this subject. in some cases, profiling may be necessary, but these cases should be clearly defined and safeguarded against abuse. the possibilities of storing, exchanging and interpreting information have increased more rapidly in recent years than the necessary restrictions that democracies impose on themselves. the us intelligence agencies were not given better and more reliable information, but above all a much higher workload, as a result of data mining and profiling. one security guard even compared it to filling a water glass with a fire hose, which is very ineffective. of course, ethnic profiling has been used for years, even though we usually call it otherwise. even at five feet, not the most threatening appearance, i am regularly sidelined at customs. a small personal annoyance, but many law-abiding people experience this discomfort not regularly, but systematically. these people are given the message by our society that they are always suspect, inferior and unwelcome. we will not therefore deal with real criminals, who come from every population group.
the european union has a duty to help the afghan people. one argument is self-protection for the world outside afghanistan. since 2001, americans have feared that if al-qaeda were to use the territory of afghanistan again to prepare attacks, new disasters would ensue. in that case, then, the self-interest of other states is at stake. this objective has been largely achieved. the second argument, on the other hand, concerns the position of the people of afghanistan themselves. the intention was that they would be freed from coercion and backwardness. it is a question of freedom of the press, the rights of religious minorities, individual freedoms and, above all, the protection of equal rights for women. for years, international news on afghanistan emphasized that girls were going back to school, that women were no longer required to wear the veil, that they could live independently of their husbands as equal citizens, and that women's participation in politics was increasing. the invasion seemed like a feminist project. we are now seeing in afghanistan something like what happened in chechnya. in both cases, a fundamentalist islamic group was in power. in both cases, the aim was to bring an end to it from outside. in both cases, a monster alliance was formed, one by the americans, the other by the russians. in order to combat a particular group of islamic fundamentalists, agreements are concluded with other islamic fundamentalists. the result is that the pursuit of liberties, which was a major justification for the invasion, is being sacrificed to it. in afghanistan, women are increasingly being pushed back into their taliban-era status. girls are no longer going to school, women are disappearing from politics. now a law even protects men's right to sexual pleasure, without any control by the women themselves. that's rape. meanwhile, journalists are also threatened with the death penalty by the state. that's a dead end. europe must not give further support to this course of events.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: "paper is patient", an expression for the gap between high ideals, or regulations, and everyday reality. applying the quoted saying to women's rights in afghanistan gives a very clear picture. the joint resolution rightly refers to the afghan constitution and to international conventions ratified by kabul, all of which claim equal rights for men and women, equality of both sexes before the law. put that aside and consider the real position of afghan women. in summary, the position of afghan women is roughly equivalent in 12 points to an average life expectancy of 44 years, a high mortality rate in childbirth, namely 1 600 per 100 000 births. only 14% of all women over 15 can read. a low status, because women are the property of the man. frequent, increasing threats and intimidation of women in public office, yes, including murder. barely any protection by the afghan women's organizations' own authorities or foreign troops against targeted attacks. the family is the main determinant of girls' education. continued attacks on girls' schools. for example, in november 2008, eight schoolgirls and four teachers in the city of kandahar were mutilated by acid that the taliban sprayed in their faces. continued threat of sexual violence within and outside of marriage. about 57% of all girls are married off before their 16th birthday. few report crimes against women for fear of retaliation by family, tribe or perpetrators or even by the police. self-mutilation or even suicide by afghan women because of their hopeless situation. ladies and gentlemen, this depressing picture of the position of afghan women is far from complete. it underlines the utmost need to make the paper reality of the legal status of afghan women a national and international, including european, political priority.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. many civilians have been killed, severely disabled or mentally disturbed for the rest of their lives. the criminals who had child soldiers cut off the arms and legs of innocent civilians must be punished and given no chance to repeat their crimes. it seems that the attempt to regulate this penalty between 2000 and 2010 will fail. the united nations special tribunal for sierra leone is not able to function. any convicted persons cannot be held in sierra leone indefinitely. the question now is what we can do to improve the outcome. without outside funding, without the extension of the special tribunal and without accommodation in prisons outside sierra leone, it will not succeed. the resolution rightly draws attention to these possibilities. this verdict must lead to action soon, otherwise it will be too late.

in writing. - bosnia and herzegovina is mainly inhabited by three peoples, none of which is the majority in the country. some of these people feel very strongly connected to serbia, others to croatia and a third group wishes to emphasise their own independent bosnian identity. in fact, this country is a miniature yugoslavia, a federation of peoples who have the choice of living together peacefully or fighting each other over territory. since the break-up of yugoslavia in 1992, attempts have been made to make bosnia-herzegovina a unitary state, but they have not succeeded. i expect that this will not be possible in the near or distant future. the agreement between the three peoples and their political leaders on effective governance will only be possible when no one feels threatened by the others or by the outside world. only after the eu high representative and the foreign military have withdrawn from this country will a compromise be possible. until then, the stagnation will continue. that is why i do not agree with the resolution on this country which can only lead to the continuation of the protectorate and therefore also to stagnation.

mr president, the socially-minded english entrepreneur robert owen already advocated the introduction of the eight-hour working day in 1817. in his view, eight hours of work and a good organization of labor made it possible to create prosperity for all. and it was exactly 125 years ago that the eight-hour workday was introduced in the united states. it is part of our common european democratic philosophy that the shortening of the working week contributes to a decent life. fortunately, in recent decades more attention has been paid to the division of labour and care. in this context, mr president, it is a great shame that we in europe still have to fight for the limitation of the maximum working week. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not distorted. robert owen was right: working more than eight hours is counterproductive; this is also shown by various studies. in this period of rising unemployment the majority of the council of ministers and the commission have taken a completely wrong direction.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, you know that road safety is one of the priorities of my work as commissioner for transport. it is important to stress at the outset that while road safety is of course an important aspect when discussing a matter such as the working hours of road hauliers, your debate today, our debate, is not about road safety but about social legislation, not about driving time but about working hours. the question to be answered today is this: should an independent contractor be subject to restrictions on working hours on the same terms as an employed person? this is a matter which must be assessed very carefully because there is no precedent in european legislation for determining how long a selfemployed person can work in his own office or laboratory. in 1998, when the council and parliament first discussed the directive on working time for mobile workers, the situation in the road transport sector was completely different from today, as rapporteur panayotopoulos recalled: at the time, it was common practice to circumvent the current driving time regulations, with the consequence that professional drivers spent excessive hours at the wheel. in fact, under the old 1985 driving time regulations, effective control of driving time was practically impossible. in this situation, between 1988 and 2002, legislators discussed a commission proposal to regulate working time not only for employed but also for self-employed road hauliers, and this discussion led to the adoption of the sectoral directive on working time for mobile workers. it was hoped that the negative consequences for road safety resulting from inadequate legislation on driving time could be reduced by extending the scope of the working time rules to self-driving drivers. however, the problem was not resolved and following a conciliation procedure between parliament and the council, the commission was invited to examine the advantages and disadvantages of extending the working time rules to self-employed workers and to submit a proposal in 2008. the commission complied with this request and published a detailed study in 2007 which reached the following conclusions. first of all: working time must not be confused with driving time. the situation has changed radically with regard to the second. as you know, this house has adopted, together with the council, new rules on driving time. these are rules which include the use of the digital tachograph, a highly reliable monitoring instrument and a specific directive on implementation. the new rules, which have been in force since 2007, apply to all truck drivers, including self-driving cars. with the new digital tachograph, which records every minute of the truck's movement, a truck driver can drive no more than nine hours a day and an average of 45 hours each week. in fact, it is now possible to control the application of these rules much more strictly than in 1985. second point: there is no precedent in community social legislation for regulating the use of selfemployed labour. a selfemployed person cannot be forced to work overtime, as he is, by definition, free to organise his work as he wishes. in addition, it is almost impossible in practice to control the working hours of this category. third element released: the overall balance of advantages and disadvantages of extending the working time rules to selfemployed workers is very uncertain and it is not possible to demonstrate that the application of the directive to selfemployed road hauliers would bring clear advantages. finally, it is very important to stress that the application of working time rules to self-employed road hauliers is ineffective and very difficult to achieve, as these workers do not have to indicate working hours for pay purposes, and the administrative costs of applying such rules would be very high. fourth element: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies, and that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. socially they are vulnerable. now, in theory, they are covered by the directive, but its non-application means that in practice this does not happen. the commission has therefore proposed to strengthen the implementation of the directive and to provide 'fake' self-employed road hauliers with the social protection they need. in a period of economic crisis, imposing an additional administrative and financial burden on small and fragile enterprises, which have to cope with the consequences of the recession, would not be a positive sign. the commission therefore welcomes the amendments tabled by the epp, the alde and the ind, which are in line with the council's common position adopted at the last transport council and which send a clear message to the sector: the commission will not tolerate the phenomenon of 'fake' autonomous road hauliers and the legislator will ensure that the legislation is applied throughout europe.

president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur for the great work she has done on this dossier. after this parliament, as mr mccarthy has already pointed out, called for a ban on seal products in the eu as early as 2006, i am very pleased that it is now proposing a compromise with the council which is in line with the wishes of this house. every year, some 900,000 seals are brutally slaughtered in commercial seal hunts. this is not only horrible, but completely useless. the pse group was convinced from the outset that the commission's proposal did not go far enough. the broad exemptions in that proposal would have been impossible to control, because the large-scale hunting takes place everywhere in a ten-day period every year. the capacity to control this is inadequate and there is often a reluctance on the part of the governments concerned. it was crucial for our group to limit the scope for exceptions in the negotiations considerably and i am very pleased with the current result of a trade ban, which has largely achieved this. the pse group also believes that it is important to intervene as little as possible in the traditional livelihoods of indigenous peoples. the compromise exception for the inuit and other indigenous peoples meets the wishes of my group. while we cannot force non-eu countries to stop seal hunting, hopefully the trade in seal products will become much less profitable and will reduce the hunting itself, and hopefully eventually stop altogether. in that sense, i am very pleased with the result achieved.

draft report by the committee on culture and education. - president, the importance of the renewed social agenda is clear, especially in the light of the current economic crisis. the rapporteur, mr silva peneda, has made a great effort to produce this report. the committee on culture and education has contributed to this opinion. the focus is on four themes: the european commission has recently launched a new action programme on the integration of young people into the labour market. first of all, the relationship between education and business. a broader dialogue between business, education, trade unions and associations is needed to identify the skills needed in the economy in the future. adult education has a role to play in developing these skills. the content of education must meet vocational and practical requirements. it is also necessary to promote cooperation between universities and industry. a bridge must be built between educational programmes and the business community, which must also be able to complement educational programmes, offer internships, and organise open days for students. secondly, lifelong learning is very important. it is crucial that there is a balance between family life, work and learning. public and private childcare also plays an important role and needs to be expanded to enable parents to participate throughout their lives. sport is also an additional instrument, and i would like to mention that from the committee on culture, where we also promote sport. it promotes the development of values such as honesty, solidarity, respect for rules and team spirit, and it is also important for health. it is important that the member states be encouraged in these areas.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs and security, on the situation in turkey. it bombed turkey into membership in 1999 and continued this path by opening accession negotiations with ankara in 2005. the council and the commission consider that they can ignore the historical heritage of the republic of turkey. this historical legacy - or rather, this tax - is today manifested in a veritable cultural struggle between the secularist-kemalist camp and the conservative-religious front around the ruling akp party over the proper direction of the republic of turkey. the most toxic consequence of this is the startling ergenekon lawsuit against many kemalist prominent figures. kemalists and prime minister erdogan are now bitterly close in their nationalist stance towards the country's religious and ethnic minorities. the continuing persecution of these minorities - a historical fact of the turkish republic - is of course contrary to the political conditions of accession to the european union. the council and the commission, a well-known dutch proverb says: better half turned than lost. the european institutions and the turkish authorities should finally learn this lesson. this is the only way to a more realistic and mutually beneficial relationship.
mr president, listening to this debate, i have the impression that most people are satisfied, and yet i have the strong impression that it is a meagre package. the main thing, i think, is that the honour of president barroso and the honour of the commission have been saved. the measures proposed are certainly useful, but the financing is still uncertain. i have a few objections to the fact that the agricultural budget is always used as a cash cow for all sorts of unexpected events that occur. i believe that the agricultural budget must take account of unexpected events, even if there is a surplus. i believe that the commission and the european union have learned very little from the outbreaks of infectious animal diseases that have occurred in the past. if that happens again, it will have to be paid for from the agricultural budget. my question is: what's the priority? the payment of the proposed measures to combat animal diseases, or this package which has yet to be settled? there is still a great deal of uncertainty in this respect, but i understand that income supplements will always be guaranteed, and that gives me some peace of mind. on the measures themselves: it depends on the countries. they are without a doubt useful. i too am in favour of greater energy security and i think that everything we do in this respect is useful. finally, i would like to thank the two commissioners for their work, and i would like to thank in particular mrs fischer boel, with whom i have had a very good cooperation over the last five years.
mr president, we are now in the third year of our financial perspective, and we are here for the third year to discuss an interim adjustment. in 2007, galileo, that was still to be explained; in 2008, the 1 billion food facility, where we had to do all the necessary tricks to get it done, because it had to be funded within the ceilings of the existing categories, when there was really no room for that. and now we are here to talk about an economic stimulus package, which is indeed a welcome addition to the national efforts in this area, and which in the energy field and for broadband is also hopefully an incentive for projects in the north of my country. but there are two additional remarks to be made. i am glad that we are playing by the rules this time and that we are actually adjusting the financial perspective, but we have had to open the box of tricks again by using the budgets for 2010 and possibly 2011. it is of course nice to say that this does not affect the codecision programmes, but what about agriculture, which, as we know, is not yet covered by codecision? what, and it has been said, if there is an outbreak of animal disease or a serious market crisis and we need those resources in agriculture itself? can the commission give a guarantee that these commitments will not be breached? mr president, i am standing here with mixed feelings in my last debate in this house. i am very happy with the decision that we took on the hoppenstedt report to increase the funding of the level 3 committees. this is a step towards strong european supervision of the financial markets that have developed far beyond national borders. i have been a strong supporter of this and i hope the debate on this will continue very strongly in the next term. the other dossier on the capital requirements directive (crd) is not, in my view, a good example of better regulation under the lamfalussy process that we have developed in the 10 years i have been active. i will support the results because we need to send a strong signal to the market, but i would have preferred a more principles-based approach and more transparent consultation in the political process. this was the pressure of obtaining this hasty result. i hope that, at the end of the year when there will be a fuller review of the crd, the lamfalussy process will also be taken properly into account. i strongly recommend that the committee on economic and monetary affairs reinstate this process. in my last speech, i would also link to what ms berès has said and would say to mr mccreevy that it is a pity that what he has done to regulate these financial markets is really too little and too late. i want to express my appreciation to my colleagues in the committee on economic and monetary affairs for their cooperation over these 10 years. i hope they get a commissioner in the new term who will be exclusively devoted to the financial markets, handling a portfolio which really focuses on this very important subject that brought us into these dire straits nowadays, and who will really be devoted to regulation and proper european supervision of the financial markets.
