the rapporteur. - mr president, first of all a few general remarks. in recent years i have been working intensively on behalf of my group, the alde group, on the establishment of a european asylum and migration policy. the usefulness and necessity of this are evident to practically everyone. a europe without internal frontiers calls for a common approach. the fact is, however, that the standards agreed and the results achieved so far are in stark contrast to the ambitions expressed in the tampere programme, the hague programme and, more recently, the french pact on asylum and migration. the problem is that the greatest common denominator suddenly turns out to be the minimum when the council has to take concrete decisions, and so the desired harmonisation effect is actually missing. even when it comes to transposition into national legislation, many member states fail to meet the deadline, either in terms of timing or accuracy. as a result, in practice, there are still huge differences between member states. this not only creates confusion but also promotes abuse. the council does not seem to have been sufficiently impressed with the fact that improving the quality of asylum seekers, that more consistent and more solidary action is not only in the interests of asylum seekers but also, and above all, in the interests of the member states themselves. as regards my own report, the following: the existing dublin regulation is also the product of a shaky political compromise in the council, resulting in many ambiguous passages and the necessary gaps. i therefore wholeheartedly support the commission's efforts to achieve a truly uniform and efficient dublin system. the most important political point in the proposed recast is, in my view, article 31. as i said a little while ago, i regard the lack of consistent and solidary action by the council as the main obstacle to the establishment of a common asylum and migration policy. i therefore have a good understanding of the provisions contained in article 31 of the commission proposal from this point of view alone. the fact is, however, that the dublin system was never designed or intended as a burden-sharing instrument. it is also clear that the dublin system does not in itself create any particular pressure on asylum seekers or create an excessive burden for certain member states. i am therefore afraid that, however well-intentioned, the commission's proposal will be of little effect in this respect if we are to achieve a more consistent and united approach by the member states. the member states which are now faced with an excessive burden, due to their demographic situation or, for example, their geographical location, are ultimately not helped or not helped enough. the question of solidarity must therefore be considered in a broader context. in recent years it has become clear that member states need a stick behind the door. it is time, as far as i am concerned, for a breakthrough. this solidarity between the member states will have to be enforced in some way. i know that some member states have reacted very negatively to the proposals adopted in the libe committee, and i am quite frankly still cautious. i am also aware that i am on slippery ground when it comes to the commission's right of initiative. but fair is fair: i'm actually sick of all the beautiful words in this. the stockholm programme of the next president will probably contain some of the most beautiful paragraphs. i'm sure you will. but, mr president-in-office, that programme could be stolen from me if the member states do not give in again in practice.
in my first contribution i already indicated that the differences between the member states are still enormous and that the much-desired harmonization has in fact failed in this respect. we can no longer deny that. directives provide a set of procedural standards rather than a standard procedure. a world of difference, which we are now trying to close and the liberals are very clearly choosing a pragmatic approach. further approximation of the laws of the member states, including, of course, proper enforcement, is the only way forward in our view. but again, with the necessary realism and the necessary pragmatic conviction. we therefore consider the establishment of a european asylum support office, the proposed recasting of the reception facilities directive and the eurodac regulation to be crucial. in this respect, and this is a small point for the commission, it is regrettable that the publication of both the recasting of the procedure and the recognition directive is still very much in the offing. this one is scheduled for june 24th. but from the point of view of greater coherence and better legislation, it would have been more logical to adopt these two proposals in the current asylum package. but, of course, the council will have the floor later. i would like to stress once again that greater coherence, better quality and consistent and united action are important for all member states. i shall not soon forget our visits to the external borders of europe, and to the well-known hot spots in particular. the credibility of the european union has been at stake for some time in this respect. do what you promised!
president. - tomorrow, on the last day of this house in its present composition, we shall be voting on a package of proposals to improve our asylum policy. after five years of debate and visits to asylum centres, it is high time to come up with concrete measures. if we have to wait for the implementation of this, the final reaction will come very late. the events of 2005 and 2006 led to a crackdown on illegal immigration, but the asylum seeker was literally thrown overboard. although i support the establishment of a cooperation agency, i have my doubts about its form and its role. how do we arrive at a reliable list of safe countries of origin? what resources are we going to use for that? and how can we adequately protect the sources of information from countries that are not safe? can these sources be made public and what say does such a list have to the independent judge? i would like to hear from the council how to avoid that problem. why was it not decided to make practical cooperation a task for frontex? the agency is small in size and can handle such a task well with sufficient expansion of resources. this will enable an adequate response to the actual developments already faced by frontex. the practice of asylum seekers and migrants entering the country can then be used to ensure that asylum seekers are properly welcomed. i think that's very practical.

president, vice-president of the commission, rapporteur, after a period of 15 years, this will be my last contribution to a debate in the european parliament. so i think it is a very special contribution to a very specific debate. the suspension of the airport slots scheme for six months. i have been campaigning for a green transport policy in recent years, with the aim of securing the future of the transport sector. i think we have succeeded in doing that again with this report. a reduction from 80% to 75% would not solve the problems. airlines would still have no qualms about stopping empty-flight operations in the event of such a change. the compromise reached fortunately provides solutions which are good for the environment but also provide a boost to the aviation sector, which has been hit hard by the economic crisis. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on airport slots are respected. as long as those slots are so expensive that empty-flighting pays, the current legislation is useless. i am therefore satisfied with the text which indicates that any further suspension of the lock system must be accompanied by a complete change in the legislation. of course, it must be two separate dossiers, so that emergency measures can be taken quickly and there is enough time for a comprehensive review. i would like to hear from commissioner tajani whether this last point will be respected. president. - i shall now close. i am not going to go into the details of the matter. it has always been a pleasure to work with the colleagues in the committee on transport. i would therefore like to thank all my colleagues, and in particular mr costa, for his report which we are now discussing and for his willingness to work towards a sensible compromise on airport slots.
in writing. - with regard to the recent protests against the election results in moldova, my group has tabled a separate resolution which departs from the compromise between four groups. there is no difference between the two resolutions in the choice of free and fair elections. there is a difference in the assessment of the government and the majority party in that country. my group argues that the protests are being organized by undemocratic forces which want to challenge the repeated choice of half the electorate for the communist party. moreover, it is assumed that these protests were organized from the neighboring country romania, which moldova wants to incorporate. on the basis of that consideration, my group votes in the majority against the joint resolution. i shall, however, vote in favour of this resolution. many moldovans have applied for romanian citizenship. political contacts with the current ruling party must not prevent us from respecting the wish of a large part of the population to join romania. this desire is encouraged by the fact that further enlargement of the number of member states within the eu no longer has the support of public opinion. accession to romania will then be the only way to enter the eu.

- a writer. - two and a half minutes, mr president. in the last five years, there have been many debates about iran. my group was sympathetic to the revolution in the late 1970s, not because of any relationship with the religious zealots, but because the previous government of the pahlavi family did not represent the people. that government could only survive by a close bond with america and europe. the fact that a previous government led by prime minister mossadeq, which had much more popular support, had been ousted by foreign pressure, gave the broad popular movement against the government a decidedly anti-western character. the west was not seen as an ally in the struggle for democracy and progress, but as a colonial profiteer and oppressor. there is no longer any doubt that power has been taken over by groups that are not only eager to seek conflict with america and israel, but are also outright conservative, intolerant and undemocratic. they oppress their people, have brought the judiciary and the military into the grip of religious fanatics, and prevent voters from voting for those with more moderate views. the rights of women, ethnic and religious minorities are being ignored, and the death penalty is often carried out in the most brutal public fashion as a means of eliminating deviant behaviour. opposition behavior inside iran leads to imprisonment. opponents who have fled abroad are persecuted and made suspicious by the governments, media and public opinion in the countries where they now live. we have seen this in the attempts to put the opposition in exile on a list of terrorist organisations and in the attempts to close the ashraf refugee camp in iraq. this parliament has rightly spoken out against these two cases recently. - a writer. - mr president, in madagascar the incumbent president was forced to resign after popular protests. he made deals with foreign companies that brought short-term revenue to his government but were harmful to his people. that made his position untenable. then, with the help of the military, the opposition leader, who was also the former mayor of the capital, was appointed interim president, despite the fact that he was too young to be appointed by law. this procedure, and especially the intervention of the army, has been criticised. the african union considers this an illegal coup d'état and rejects the new government. i think we could also draw a parallel with recent events in a european country, iceland. there too, the government was forced to resign after popular protests. a minority government with a completely different party composition came to power. no one thought it was a coup. in the meantime, new elections were held, in which the new government coalition won a large majority. a similar outcome is possible in madagascar, provided that elections are held within a foreseeable time.

mr president, after ten years of talking, we are now close to the copenhagen climate conference. we have 50 days to agree on ambitious targets that are binding not only on europe but also on the united states and countries like china and india. an ambitious agreement requires adequate financing - which was also discussed in the debate - and not just from the european union. all countries will have to come up with funding so that developing countries can also participate in a climate agreement, because solidarity with the poorest of the poor must be firmly anchored in a climate agreement. the commission has rightly taken the lead in financing. but what mr florenz was referring to is that we are in this parliament, in effect, competing with each other for the 'higher, the more'. the question is whether that really helps. you have rightly challenged the member states to join in, and i also commend the swedish presidency for the efforts it is making. but the united states and the emerging economies will also have to come across the bridge. you can count on our support. however, there is still much work to be done in europe as well. while we are among the world's leaders in our emissions standards, compared to the united states, private investment in technological innovation and sustainability lags far behind. i expect more from positive incentives for green investment and innovation by companies than from a new european tax. because at a time when we desperately need more activity and employment, we need to challenge european investors and businesses more to play their part in this necessary transition in europe and globally.
president, the climate summit must succeed and that is why europe must speak with one voice and play a leading role in the negotiations. copenhagen is important because god's creation is very fragile and has been profoundly affected by human activity. particular attention is needed in the developing countries. the people of these countries are not contributing much to climate change, but they are paying the price. crops are dried up or washed away by floods, villages are destroyed by cyclones, entire regions are set back years in a single night. our industrialized countries have contributed greatly to climate change. we therefore have a responsibility to provide adequate and sustainable financial and technical support to developing countries. the bali action plan has put that very well. it is therefore also important that sufficient european money is made available, because that is what it is all about. that is why we say that the climate fund must be replenished with at least 15 billion euros a year, plus a contribution from the other major economies in the world. they too must not shirk their obligations. finally, i am glad that the president-in-office of the council has referred to deforestation. we think that is essential. stopping deforestation is the way to reduce co2.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on agriculture, fisheries and rural development, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in milk and milk products (com (97) 0406 - c4-0173/97). fortunately, that is not the case in the debate today. forests and tropical rainforests offset a significant proportion of co2 emissions. land-use change, such as deforestation and rainforest degradation, are responsible for at least 18% of today's co2 emissions. an equally ominous consequence of deforestation is that it drives many indigenous peoples in developing countries from their lands and from their livelihoods. the cdm is also not working, which is funding projects in developing countries to be able to emit more co2 themselves. unfortunately, the world still operates in such a way that the interests of rich countries and large industries are put before socioeconomic justice. deforestation must be stopped, but industrialized countries have a moral obligation to support developing countries technically and financially in this endeavour.
mr president, in the pacific region thousands of people are being displaced because their islands are being submerged, and in sudan livestock is dying of thirst. these are images that we've all seen, and in the face of these and other impacts of climate change, one thing must come to the fore: the polluter pays. in december, in copenhagen, the world will face the historic task of giving meaning to those words. so far, however, poor countries, according to oxfam's calculations, have been self-financing three quarters of the adjustments, while oil has been disappearing unimpeded from poor countries, often without a reasonable payment going to the developing countries' coffers. climate change is costing developing countries more than 100 billion euros a year in the future. that's not development money, it's just creating the conditions for development, because maybe an island in the pacific is not going to sink because of climate policy, or because desertification is being stopped, so that people can continue to live and work where they want. of course, there are climate measures that can also boost the development of poor countries. whoever plants trees is fighting desertification. but at the moment, climate policy is being funded mainly by development funds, and that is unacceptable. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the funds are used to the full. the developing countries are now being hit three times. most eu member states are not living up to their own development policy promises, the economic crisis is reducing investment in poor countries and the development budget is falling. fourthly, you can add that the poor countries themselves have to pay for the climate damage they did not cause. that logic must be broken in copenhagen with new financing methods. development policy will now also have to be aligned with climate policy. the two will have to be matched like never before. the first priority must be to give the developing countries themselves a voice in the transparent spending of a climate fund. then the fund must be created, for the eu and for the world.
mr president, in the end there is only one solution to the climate problem, and that is technology. only with new technology will we be able to maintain our way of life. but the development of technology itself is too slow, and especially the spread of technology, especially to developing countries, is far too slow. climate is a global problem, but using the best available technology together is not one of them. under kyoto, this has failed utterly, so let's learn from it. of course, we have to take intellectual property into account. it is precisely those companies which are at the forefront of development that should be encouraged and rewarded. but we need to spread that knowledge faster. the montreal protocol created a fund for this purpose and there are ideas for a multilateral climate technology fund. can the commission and the council explain their views on this? finally, stopping deforestation is of particular importance for developing countries. i understand, however, that there is now a division within the european union over the role of forests, mainly due to sweden, finland and austria. how can we stop developing countries from deforesting if the eu itself is ambiguous about its forests? i would like to thank the commissioner for his comments.
president. - we are talking about climate change, but we should be talking about the people who are most affected by climate change, and those are the least developed countries. in the maldives, they are fighting rising water, in sudan, they are fighting for fertile land. the commission has always assumed that market players will make a significant contribution to climate adaptation. but if you talk to all the investors, they always say the same thing: "yes, we give money, but mostly to emerging economies. we're not going to invest in the vulnerable countries". these are volatile markets, and climate adaptation spending is on small projects. that's not what investors put their money in. it must now be very clear that public money must go to the least developed countries. the world bank clearly states that it should be at least 80 billion. it is time for the eu to show serious leadership with a serious offer and not the vague offer of 2 to 15 billion. the eu simply has to follow the european parliament and come up with at least 30 billion.
madam president, ‘success or failure, that is the question’. all types of crises have already been mentioned, but one has been forgotten next to global warming, which is the crisis of public confidence in politics. i agree with mr carlgren. let us not make this a beauty contest about who is more or less ambitious, more realistic or less realistic or more or less willing to pay. we need four clarities, because too much is at stake: we need a clear and ambitious political stance; we need a clear commitment; we need a clear path and clear deadlines; and last but not least we need clarity on financial support. if copenhagen is not a complete success let us not cry wolf and start naming and shaming, but let us set out a clear process and time path in order to come to a result. let us show we are reliable and responsible, both in target-setting and in a fair financial perspective.

rapporteur. − mr president, i will be very brief in order to make up for mr nicholson and ensure that we do not run late. the lisbon treaty will be in force – if i calculate correctly – seven days and five hours from now. i must say that the rush by the council to adopt a whole series of decisions is somewhat embarrassing in the light of this. what i also find embarrassing is that there is an empty seat here, where i would have hoped to see the council so that we could actually have an exchange of views. i basically endorse the proposals made by the previous speaker. of course we support the development of europol. we want a strong europol. we want a europol that is able to operate and fight against crime, but we also want a europol that is subject to democratic scrutiny. i therefore endorse the proposal made by the previous speaker to ask the council to withdraw the proposals on europol and present a new proposal within a maximum of six months – and preferably earlier – under the lisbon treaty. finally, when it comes to the specific subject that i am rapporteur for – which is europol and the transfer of personal data and confidential documents to third parties – i would like to hear from the council – which is absent – on how it feels about the analysis made by the european parliament’s legal service to the effect that the legal basis chosen for this particular proposal is the wrong one. i do not know who is going to answer for the council, but maybe it can come back on this and send somebody who can provide us with a reply.
i would like to thank the vice-president of the commission for his answers and for his reflection on the various subjects. judicial cooperation in the european union is of great importance. when we talk about the europe of the people, we are talking above all about judicial cooperation. within this framework, the fight against crime is a top priority, especially as it is increasingly transnational. national crime is almost a felony; it is often the great transnational crime. in this connection, the group of the european people's party is also in favour of a firm position on europol, but also on eurodac, and in this context on the forensic laboratories. an important element in the fight against organised crime is democratic control of those who fight crime. i don't want to take any of that away. i would like to draw attention to the position of the victim in this context, including the proposals we are going to be discussing in the coming months. crime prevention is often about the rights of the accused, and rightly so: the privacy of the defendant and his position in the criminal process are important. but i want more attention to the victim's position for the next five years. fundamental rights, mr president, are not absolute; they must always be exercised within a framework. as the dutch constitution says: i'm not responsible for the law. that goes for the suspects as well as the victims. i understand and i agree that parliament is saying at this moment on these four dossiers: stop right there! we have to wait until 1 december, then the new proposals and then the reality: the commission has already made a number of proposals.
rapporteur. − mr president, before i make my intervention, i too would like to extend my gratitude to commissioner barrot. i can only say that he has set the standards very high for his successor. i will not go into the substance, because i think we should have that debate later, but i will briefly come back to the procedure, because i would like to clarify one thing. i have heard indirectly, as it were from the empty chair over there, that there is a very good reason why the council is in a rush, namely because we do not want to stop europol working. it has to become an agency by 1 january. but if the council were sincerely interested in the views of the european parliament it could have been here. it could have started the procedure much earlier than the end of july. it could even have accepted the european parliament’s offer to work very quickly, because the parliament always shoulders its responsibilities, and we work very quickly if we have to. we offered that to the council by way of a procedure that would be in the spirit of lisbon at the very least. we asked for one month’s extension, but the council was simply unwilling to give us that extension; and the fact that the council is absent today, and has not been interested in talking to the european parliament, only demonstrates that they simply do not want to involve the european parliament; it demonstrates their insincerity. i regret that, because, by now, it is seven days, three hours and forty minutes away: we will have to work together. i would just like to point out here that, as has been said very well by one of our colleagues, this is not about our egos. it is not about the european parliament being sidelined; it is not even about the national parliaments being sidelined. this is about accountability to the citizen, because it concerns very important decisions. we are talking about the transfer of personal data and confidential documents to third parties, about the data and confidential information of our citizens, the people that we represent. those are the ones that the council is accountable to, more than to us. finally, i heard what the commissioner said, but i would like to have a statement from the european council promising – given that they have rushed through this whole package – that they will withdraw or review the decision as quickly as possible, within a maximum of six months, and then fully involve the european parliament in a full codecision procedure and be accountable to the citizens.
on behalf of the ppe group the clock is ticking. we are faced with the important challenge of securing an ambitious agreement on climate change in copenhagen. and as december approaches, there are setbacks. obama can't deliver on his campaign promise yet. but there are also hopeful signs, such as the ambitions of the new japanese government. i would like to commend the swedish presidency and the european commission, and in particular commissioner dimas, for their efforts. we are your allies. and i would also like to commend you for your efforts to bring european leaders to a standstill. because more than ever it is important that europe speaks with one voice in these crucial negotiations. i can say on behalf of the epp group that we hope that you and i will reach an ambitious agreement which will lead to binding reduction targets for the european union, the united states and japan. but equally for countries like china, brazil and india. equal ambitions are important to effectively tackle climate change and to create a global level playing field. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). europe must play its fair part in this. but with you i say: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. this could be a concrete, visible outcome of the copenhagen summit, a hopeful signal. at the same time, it is very important that agreements are made to ensure that these funds will actually and effectively contribute to the reduction of climate change, as well as agreements on technology transfer and protection of intellectual property rights. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the climate convention can be an important catalyst for this, also for us in europe, so that our social market economy is increasingly transformed into a sustainable social market economy.
we have two weeks before the copenhagen conference begins. a crucial moment to reach an ambitious climate agreement. the european union rightly says that an agreement must be reached in copenhagen, our climate cannot be delayed. the science is clear. to meet the two-degree target, the target that the european union has said for years it wants to meet, rich countries need to reduce their emissions by 40 percent. the european union must therefore strengthen its own target if we are to achieve the two degrees objective. our climate calls for it. but the european union also holds the key to getting the united states on board. as long as the european union does not clearly indicate how much money it wants to make available to developing countries, the united states can hide behind it. so come up with a clear offer of 30 billion for developing countries, and all the pressure will be on the united states to come up with its own reduction target. copenhagen can succeed, copenhagen must succeed and the european union still holds the key to that.
i'm going to list a rough list of actions that emit co2 from someone i just ran into on the street yesterday. she lives (remember that!). she took a shower. she drove to work. she bought a bunch of flowers from a greenhouse, wrapped in plastic. she had the laptop on all day. she cooked a very big, delicious steak and she turned the heat up a little bit. how can we, after such a fine day of luxury, insist that an indigenous woman who was driven from her land by deforestation for our luxury, must reduce co2 emissions, when her list only contained: "i'm alive" is what? industrialized countries are responsible for high co2 emissions, and they will have to pay for them and support developing countries. we can't just leave it at a pittance. we must let go of the urge to 'do something if everyone else does something'. the united states and china must be strongly reprimanded. ambition is one thing, but taking responsibility is something that really matters.
i would like to thank minister carlgren and commissioner dimas for their contributions, not only for their content but above all for the tone of their contributions, a positive tone, a tone which reflects the belief in the real achievement of an international agreement, an agreement with international binding standards. president, at least 60 heads of government will be coming to copenhagen. let them not come for nothing. let them realize their great responsibility. let them leap over their own shadow and look beyond short-term economic interests. let them take a big step towards the economy of tomorrow, an economy with minimal use of raw materials. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council and the commission. minister carlgren, commissioner dimas, you don't let the big players take each other hostage. we no longer have time for the game of who can sit still the longest, that time is behind us. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the situation in the middle east.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, officially i could now sum up the parts of our 27-page resolution that are most relevant for the other groups, but i will not. i would like to share the following story with you. on a british airways flight from johannesburg, a well-off, middle-aged white south african lady found herself sitting next to a black man. she called the stewardess over to complain about her seating. ‘what seems to be the problem, madam?’ ‘well, can’t you see?’ she said, ‘i can’t possibly sit next to this disgusting human. find me another seat!’ a few minutes later the stewardess returned. ‘madam, unfortunately, as i suspected, economy class is full. i have spoken to the cabin services director and club is also full. however, we do have one seat in first class.’ before the south african lady had a chance to answer, the stewardess continued, ‘it is quite exceptional to make this kind of upgrade, but given the circumstances the captain felt that it was outrageous that someone should be forced to sit next to such an obnoxious person.’ she then turned to the black man sitting next to the south african lady and said, ‘sir, if you would like to get your things, i have a seat ready for you in first class up at the front.’ the surrounding passengers gave a standing ovation while the black man walked up to first class. now, what has this got to do with us? do we consider others inferior? the white south african lady would shock us all. but that was obviously an extreme example. however, i do think that some of our colleagues in the epp in particular, but also in the council, understand perfectly what i am trying to say here. the other group, my group, firmly believes in a europe which people can understand, trust and believe in. such a europe must be based on human rights, fundamental freedoms, democracy, the rule of law and, yes, true equality for all. it is high time to tackle all forms of discrimination on all grounds, including on the basis of sexual orientation.
first of all, i welcome the warm words which the council has spoken about cooperation with parliament, but i would rather see the council taking over the recommendations of parliament, because i too, like mr albrecht, find that the council is actually quite deaf and just continues on its own course, and that parliament is working hard to take a position, without this being reflected in the council programme. secondly, the point which has been very well made by my colleague, mr hennis-plasschaert. i would remind parliament, and in particular the two large groups, once again: equality is indivisible. we cannot give a little equality to some groups and not to others. there have been times when the state interfered with the choice of a spouse, and when the state prohibited marriage between people of certain religions, when the state prohibited marriage between people of different races. fortunately, those days are over, and i believe that no member state in the european union should refuse to recognise a marriage legally entered into in another member state, even if it is between two people of the same sex. i would therefore also like to call on the two major groups: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. and i am thinking in particular of our friends in the socialist group. this is the 21st century and i think that all european citizens, regardless of race, religion, age or sexual orientation, deserve our protection. it is high time that this was recognised in this parliament.
for the greens/efa group the request by ireland for european money for the unemployed left behind by dell in the closure of the irish branch has raised a number of questions which need to be answered. the fact that a company like dell closes a branch in one country and opens another is, in principle, the result of normal market forces. but the various forms of government support that come into play change this. dell established itself in ireland with government support a number of years ago. now dell is closing its irish doors and opening a branch in poland, again with state support. in the meantime, the irish unemployed left behind by dell are not being paid for by a company social plan, but by the european globalisation adjustment fund. i think that this shows a serious lack of coherence between industrial policy and employment policy. how many jobs have been created with all the public money that ireland, poland and the european union have poured into dell? the question is therefore how the commission and the member states will work together to ensure that their policies are in line. and how can we ensure that the globalisation fund supports the efforts of a company for its employees and does not largely replace them?
first of all, i would like to thank the swedish presidency for its efforts in recent months. the way in which the rubik's cube has been put in order with the appointments of mr van rompuy and mrs ashton is particularly worthy of respect. i am very pleased with these appointments, because mr van rompuy has already said that his personal views are completely irrelevant. this statement shows a wise and sensible insight. i congratulate mr van rompuy on this eurorealist approach and will keep reminding him of it, when necessary and when it is not desirable. unfortunately, i also hear some disapproval, as if the first permanent chairman does not have enough profile. some people apparently wanted to see the emergence of a kind of european superman. i certainly do not share these views and i am also reassured by the first statements made by mr van rompuy. he has indicated that he never wanted to become such a european superman. the european council meeting will be a crucial moment for the european heads of government to set out the details of the copenhagen strategy. the aim is to achieve the best possible result. i wish technical president van rompuy and president reinfeldt every success on this very important subject. i also wish us all a good and, above all, a future-proof result.

for the epp group in the more than ten years that i have been a member of this parliament, i have heard big words used here on a regular basis. in the run-up to the rome food summit, commission president barroso is also using big words. he said: "we have collectively failed in the fight against hunger. it is a moral scandal, it is a great stain on our collective conscience". end of quote. and rightly so. but then the result is disappointing. i am disturbed by the feeling that rome was not concerned with the interests of those one billion starving people, but with the political interests of the rich countries. to be specific, i will give a few examples: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the use of biofuels, and the promotion of biofuels is increasingly recognised as a contributor to high prices and hence to hunger. but there is a taboo against criticizing this policy. i have already pointed out in this house the danger of large-scale investment by third countries in, for example, the continent of africa, in order to secure their own food security. how can countries with millions of people dependent on un food aid export food to third countries at the same time? i don't see any mention of this in the closing statement. rich countries get away with well-intentioned, urgent appeals and further studies of controversial issues. i also read that developing countries are primarily responsible for their own actions. in the light of the failed policies of the world community in the past, i would call this a disgrace. i've been looking back and looking at the closing statements of previous food summits. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. they all talk of urgency, and there is a constant call to fulfil previously made promises. but should not a repetition of all these calls be a warning to us? in the words of un rapporteur de schutte, "poor people do not eat promises". food security should be a human right, so it is often said. i would prefer to reverse it, madam president. in my bible as a christian, i read the command to feed the hungry, and that is my personal duty and our collective responsibility.
madam president, my colleagues mr bové and mr belder sketched the problem and the political reality, but there is a new trend in farming. rich countries ensure their staple food or their biofuels by buying up land in poor countries – land grabbing or, as it is euphemistically called, ‘farmland acquisition’. it happens in madagascar, for instance. this seems to be too sensitive for world leaders to discuss. europe and its leaders have a moral duty to go against this new form of what i would call colonialism. the declaration of the fao food summit did not touch upon the issue of land grabbing and in that respect really missed out on an opportunity to target hunger in the world. why did you not do that? then there is the common agricultural policy of the eu. we produce a lot of food. europeans have food to eat, but that cap ruins chances for both small-scale farmers and industrial farmers in developing countries, and they lose the chance to earn a decent living. that causes food shortages and brings a need for food imports. when can we see a free and fair european agricultural policy?
commissioner, as the last speaker on the official list, i will try to summarize this debate. i think i can do that with a good dutch proverb that says: "equal monks, equal caps". i apologize for the late-night interpreters, but what we mean by that, despite the fact that we barely have any monks left in the netherlands, is that you have to treat equal situations equally. so the same requirements you're putting on manufacturers here should apply to products that are trying to get into our market. otherwise you'll simply make it impossible for our farmers to compete. this is the case for the identification and registration of bovine animals and for animal disease measures in brazil. but so is american chlorinated chicken and hormone milk. or for cloned animals, and the list of examples is long, commissioner. i have the impression, from what i hear from our colleagues, that this parliament will be taking the new commission to task for this, for the equal treatment of equal cases, and not, as we are doing now, five years later, but actually before the new commission takes its seat. and you mentioned smes, commissioner, and steps have indeed been taken there, i agree with you. for example, the fact that vaccination has been given a greater place in the fight against, fortunately. because in my country too, 285 animals were killed for 26 cases of scd. but, commissioner, the next commission will be reckoned with in terms of the fact that we can also sell the products of these vaccinated animals within the european union. so i look forward to the next commission's action on this point too.

a few words about the european commission's expenditure in the context of external relations. according to the court of auditors, more errors were made in this area in 2008 than in 2007. and the mistakes are being made in all areas of foreign policy. i find it offensive that many errors occur in the tendering of projects. the commission is very keen to monitor the tendering procedures in the member states, and i regularly receive complaints from our representatives at municipal and provincial level about complicated and unclear procedures. the fear of being wrong in the eyes of the commission is great. but who is the commission to control our local authorities when it itself repeatedly makes mistakes in the tendering of its own projects? an example. i read in the report that budgetary aid is not sufficiently controlled to ensure that the conditions for payment are met. in other words: the commission is giving a bag of money to countries without adequate control. but what conditions does the commission actually impose? and to what extent does it measure whether budget support actually helps the development of countries? i do not read anything about that in this report, and i cannot read anything about that in the report because of the way it is designed. more generally: i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the development of the community, but that it should be involved in the development of the community. it is clear that the commission has not followed the recommendations of the court of auditors very closely, and i would therefore ask the president of the court of auditors whether he is not discouraged by the fact that he has to see year after year that the commission is failing to get its affairs in order in this area.
the european court of auditors finds many errors in the structural funds, rural development and the research programme for the financial year 2008. too many and too complicated subsidies are the result. the european court of auditors rightly stresses the importance of simplifying the rules. in october, bodies from four member states issued a position on reducing the eu regulatory pressure on the president of the european commission. they recommend the establishment of an external, independent commission to reduce regulatory pressure at eu level. in the context of the better regulation strategy, this is a step in the right direction. what is the position of the european commission in this matter? however, the pursuit of better financial management must not be limited to simplifying the rules. the same is true of the need to strengthen supervision and control. to this end, the european commission and the member states should draw up an action plan. so first of all national management declarations, which must then be followed by a positive declaration of assurance from the european side. does the european court of auditors consider such an action plan to be a useful tool for improving financial management? i think that such an action plan will help to give financial management the political priority it so needs. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval.
i would like to thank the court of auditors for the very clear annual report it has produced and i would also like to thank commissioner kallas in particular for his efforts over the past few years. i think he can rightly say that he has made a lot of progress in the last few years. i would like to highlight three elements of this annual report: the first is that the main problems still lie with the member states. many of you have already mentioned the example of regional policy, and i can only emphasize that. i would therefore also ask the commission, with regard to the national declarations of member states which exist in some member states: can you not come up with a concrete proposal to make it compulsory for all the member states of the european union? the second element i would like to stress is the volume and complexity of european regulation. that has been brought up by many colleagues. can we not start a fundamental debate on how we want to spend our money and the rules underlying it? i think that it is now based too much on mistrust and too little on trust, and that in the end it only leads to more irregularities. finally, mr president, the agricultural sector. of course, it is wonderful that for the first time the entire agricultural sector is given a green light. but let us not beat ourselves up too hard. it is rural development that is the big problem, and let that be the policy area within agriculture where we see major expansion in the coming years. and then it is not just a question of legality, but also of efficiency, because with regard to agricultural funds we are achieving the objectives in the field of nature and the environment to a much lesser extent.
mr. chairman, have you seen the movie groundhog day, about the man who lives the same day over and over again? now, i am only in my third year as a member of the european parliament, but i already feel like the main character in that film. every year the court of auditors comes here to tell us that it cannot give a declaration of assurance. and every year the european commission does its utmost to highlight the bright spots. admittedly, there has been progress, for example in agriculture. but the underlying problems remain. and then, of course, as the european commission proposes, you can increase the margin of error. but there's something about moving the goalposts. if you don't get your players to score, you're not going to make the goal bigger as a coach, but you're going to make the game better. it takes teamwork. we can only get a positive statement here by working together as a team, between the european and national auditors and with those national management declarations, as has already been said. unfortunately, some people find european money a little easier to handle than their own national money. it's easier to go shopping with someone else's credit card than with your own. but you will be presented with the bill, commissioner. if you and your successor fail to get the audit completed, that will not only be bad for budgetary control in the european union and for you as the european commission, but for the legitimacy of all of us and of our work. so you can be sure, commissioner, that we as a parliament will be watching you and your successor very closely in this respect.

the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against terrorism. the globalisation fund, a very special, tailor-made instrument, is a european instrument that we are putting into practice. the mass layoffs of more than 700 permanent employees at heijmans construction - and we are not even looking at the numbers abroad, for example in belgium and the united kingdom - call for targeted action. the construction sector is important in the brabant region, where i come from, and the consequences of this kind of mass redundancy are clearly felt in the region and far beyond. europe's action is rightly directed towards preserving expertise and craftsmanship. the crisis is now putting people on the streets, whereas we know from experience that there is a great shortage of skilled workers, particularly in the construction sector. it is therefore good that heijmans has chosen to use a mobility centre as an instrument to preserve expertise and, if possible, jobs. both the dutch ministries and the regional authorities, for example in the province of brabant, are now working to make investments in projects, infrastructure and construction very targeted and accelerated. in this way we try to show the people who come to the streets that we are actively involved in their thinking and that we are also investing money in this. i would also like to point out, in the spirit of copenhagen this week, that we are in dire need of energy supplies and energy measures in buildings and in transport. we have adjusted the rules in parliament in other areas, and i would like to mention the regional funds, which are being paid out in an accelerated manner and are being advanced in an accelerated manner. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council on the basis of a proposal from the commission. a company like heijmans, always focused on continuity and quality, will also benefit from this. it is in this spirit that we wholeheartedly support these three proposals, but in particular the heijmans proposal.
i would like to say that we are in favour of the use of the globalisation fund for these three applications. the applications meet the criteria and therefore the redundant workers are entitled to support from this fund. i would like to take this opportunity to point out a major shortcoming in the globalisation fund. we are now in the process of coming to a common vision of the future of the european economy and the labour market. the various political currents in this parliament may not agree on exactly how that should be done or how far we want to go, but we are quite agreed on the broad lines. it needs to be more sustainable and it needs to be more inclusive. if we are to achieve these goals, we must seize the opportunities now. we need to take steps now to tackle the crisis that will bring us closer to that sustainable economy. the current use of the globalisation fund leaves opportunities open to me. it is a pity that the fund, as one of our instruments for dealing with the crisis, does not provide direction. we must have the courage to make real choices. if we ensure that people who are losing their jobs in polluting industries such as the car industry are not stuck in the past but are being retrained for jobs in sustainable sectors for the future, then we are clearly showing the direction we want to go. and that's how we bring that innovative, inclusive and sustainable economy a little bit closer.
the adoption of the 2010 budget takes place against the backdrop of the economic crisis. the budgetary resources of the european union must be allocated with particular care. in this context, the council is forcing the european parliament to set priorities. the struggling economy and investment in sustainability are rightly high on the agenda. the rapporteur, mr surján, has shown that his approach is consistent with the priorities of economic growth and employment. these priorities are reflected in the shifts between the headings of the multiannual financial framework. it is also reflected in the financing of the european economic recovery plan. it is rightly investing heavily in new technology. this includes support for projects involving renewable energy sources and improvements in energy network links with third countries. to this end, we are contributing to the much-needed diversification of energy suppliers. this priority has been achieved. i do have a footnote here. we should have been a little more strict. if the european union is to operate in a credible way, it must not increase its own role in combating the crisis, but must confine itself to its core tasks. the european union is not a political union, but a political union. however, the yearly demand for more money to be spent is counterproductive. the flexibility instrument should not be required to provide the missing funds. on the other hand, stronger prioritisation ensures that we are a constructive partner for the council of ministers, especially at a time of economic crisis when member states are running short and have to cut back drastically. in conclusion, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr surján, for this step in the right direction, with an emphasis on economic recovery, without losing sight of the social aspects of eu policies such as childcare, especially in bulgaria.
mr president, we are discussing the last budget under the treaty of nice and next year will be a very different one. next year parliament will for the first time be able to take a stand on, for example, agricultural policy, but also on fisheries spending. this half of the budget will then finally be within the reach of parliament and i can tell you i look forward to it. but anyway, we are talking about the 2010 budget. a few brief remarks. it is good that more money is going to energy and research and also to the dismantling of the bulgarian nuclear power station at kozloduy, but my problem is with the main lines of the european budget. are the european contributions really preparing our economy for the future? - no, please! let's not fool ourselves! let us use the forthcoming budget review of the financial perspective to set our sights on the future. i therefore call on the council and the commission to take this review seriously and not to treat it as a joke. the current budget is still investing too much in yesterday's economy, too much old support for agriculture, too much old support for regions and far too little in sustainability and innovation, which is what it's really about. we are at a turning point. are we making europe an open-air museum where americans, chinese, indians can see beautiful culture and eat good food or are we making europe a dynamic, prosperous region, one that the rest of the world looks on with envy? in other words, do we choose stagnation or progress? my answer may be clear. let us also look at the financing of the european union. we will have to go to a system of own resources anyway. the commission is going to make a proposal to that effect at the request of us liberals. i'm looking forward to it. the current system is not giving the union enough leverage and has the perverse effect of making member states more interested in how much they can get back rather than whether the european contribution is actually having an effect. i believe, moreover, that we must do much more to stop the loss of biodiversity in the world.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, the expectations for europe 2020 are high in our group, but also in the european citizen who expects prosperity and jobs and a sustainable and innovative social market economy in 2020. concrete proposals for a strong sme and an open european internal market, including in the area of capital and knowledge development. a 2020 strategy with a strong and transparent european governance structure. no freedom of movement for the member states. the european parliament, mr president, wants to be informed not only of the commission's and the council's proposals, but to participate effectively in the creation and implementation of the 2020 strategy. how will you provide for this and what time path will you pursue? the president of the council announced in march that he would draw conclusions. do you take the initiative? can we expect a concrete framework with principles and objectives on the basis of which this parliament can participate in that discussion? can you inform us of the time path you're pursuing and who's responsible? are you mr. 2020?
the european parliament has been very active in the debate on the middle east, and i am sure that the european parliament will be very active in the debate on the middle east. it is important, however, that all parties continue to work for peace. at the same time, that could be the problem. can and will all parties work for peace? israel has taken steps in the past to trade land for peace. i am thinking of the evacuation of parts of the gaza strip. unfortunately, that did not lead to peace. in fact, rocket fire increased and israel invaded the gaza strip in 2009. now israel has announced a temporary construction halt for the west bank. a series of roadblocks have also been lifted. this as a gesture to bring about peace talks. these steps by the netanyahu government are already quite far-reaching in israeli terms. i don't see much movement on the palestinian side. they keep saying that what israel is doing is nothing. i miss the palestinian gesture to negotiate. is it the palestinian will and ability to negotiate differently? hamas seems to be caught in the crossfire with fatah and chained to iran. mahmoud abbas looks like a paper tiger with barely any influence left. i think it is time that the palestinians too made it clear that they want peace.
on behalf of the group of the european democratic alliance mr president, have they gone mad in europe? this rhetorical question was put by the americans at the beginning of december. the sharp criticism from congress was the unilateral designation of east jerusalem as the capital of the envisaged palestinian state in a circulating draft council resolution. as chairman of the ep delegation for relations with israel, i had hoped and wished for a more constructive start to our transatlantic dialogue. and, i hasten to add, close acquaintance with the draft and final council declaration touches me, as well as my colleagues in congress, deeply. i am particularly opposed to the partition of jerusalem. the council statement on the middle east is a serious diplomatic and political setback for the peace process. i am therefore very disappointed in the foreign ministers who have let themselves be dragged along by the swedish presidency, given its strained relationship with the jewish state. mr president, i have a number of fundamental reservations about the council statement. it is certainly not encouraging the palestinian authority to resume negotiations with israel. in addition, it is contrary to the quartet's principle of 'no third parties should intervene in bilateral negotiations', a principle of 9 november 2008. the official american response to the council statement of 8 december reiterated this principle. is there then only resignation to the impasse of the peace process? no, not at all! recent working visits to israel have certainly convinced me of the need and possibility of a gradual, solid process of rapprochement between the state of israel and the palestinian authority. the gradual transfer of authority in the west bank, such as area c, closer economic cooperation, while strengthening and consolidating reliable palestinian institutions, will really help the peace process. i therefore call on the council and the commission to refrain from making counterproductive statements about the peace process and to invest instead in concrete cooperation projects between israelis and palestinians. that is a european formula, isn't it?
- a writer. the eu is committed to democracy, the rule of law, human rights and fundamental freedoms. however, the eu is less principled in its fight against terrorism. take the united nations blacklists, individuals or entities suspected of having ties to osama bin laden, the al qaeda network or the taliban, can be placed on these blacklists with the result of a travel ban and the freezing of their financial assets. it is a good measure when it comes to terrorism, because terrorism must be fought, there is no question about it. but the lawlessness with which terrorists operate must not creep into the way we fight terrorism. the current system is unfortunately characterized by violations of fundamental rights. without the intervention of a judicial authority, persons are placed on the list, often without being fully informed about it. the information on which blacklisting is based is regularly sourced from intelligence services. this information is not readily available to the suspect, who consequently does not know why he is on the list. this is not only a denial of the right to information, but also of the right to a defence. once on the list, it's extremely difficult to get off. there are already enough cases where people have been wrongly listed for years and had to fight for a proper trial. they are condemned to poverty, simply cannot afford to go shopping, and are not allowed to leave their country. i am not advocating today more rights for terror suspects, but only that they, like everyone else, can claim their rights. i am in favour of transparent procedures and a proper trial for everyone. the side effects of the lists are also far-reaching. since governments can decide to put persons or organisations on the list, this can also be used as a political tool. for example, ngos that fight for human rights and are a thorn in the side of the government can be designated as terrorist organizations in order to stop their work. a ruling by the european court of justice has forced the commission to revise its current procedures. the commission has given a good start, but the council has then let it go. the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon has created a new situation. if it becomes article 215 or article 75 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union, in other words, the european parliament will not be involved at all in the treatment of the proposals or will be involved in the normal procedure, namely the codecision procedure. the legal affairs committee, as well as parliament's legal service, have indicated article 75 as the correct legal basis. i would like to hear from both the council and the commission today how they see the progress of these proposals for restrictive measures. what role is envisaged for parliament in this? it is time for transparent and democratic procedures. the question is, will you cooperate?
mr president, what document are you looking for? what's the document number? these are just a few of the reactions that you can expect as a citizen when you put a question to the european institutions. but citizens want information, not promotional stories or unreadable policy papers. the rudder must be turned around. the citizen must be the centre of attention. that is the starting point of the questions from the rapporteur, mr cashman, whom i would like to thank for the excellent - and i am only saying this to mrs sommer, who has unfortunately already left the chamber - extremely transparent cooperation in recent months. i would like to see part of the european information budget used to ensure that citizens are given real and complete information and answers to their questions, both through digital information media and through human contact, such as 'can you tell me exactly how european decision-making on european engagement went at the copenhagen climate summit?' the citizens want to know this, and i therefore call on the commission, the council and in particular the members of the european parliament to support our initiatives to increase transparency. that seems to be the only really clear message.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, the somewhat misleading title of today’s debate includes the word ‘subsidiarity’. subsidiarity, as i understand it, means taking decisions at the level closest to the citizen, and that level is the citizen – the individual citizen. what you are saying in your resolutions is that the rights of a national state are above the rights of individual citizens. it is our job in this house to protect the rights of citizens, not the rights of states. secondly, if we consider – as you claim – that this is not a matter for the european union, i would like to understand why these matters are included in the copenhagen criteria, and why we demand from candidate countries that they respect the separation of church and state when we cannot demand that of our own member states. thirdly, colleagues, if you say that the european parliament is not competent to talk about these matters, i wonder why we are competent to discuss matters like the shape of cucumbers but not the fundamental rights of our citizens. as to the european convention on human rights and the court ruling, we are now two weeks after the entry into force of the lisbon treaty, which requires that the eu accede to the convention of human rights. would it not be incomprehensible if we at the same time refused to accept the authority of the court of strasbourg? we cannot explain that to our citizens. secondly, i think – and this has been said by mr lópez before – politicians have no business interfering in court rulings. let the judges do their jobs. we may have an opinion – we may like the ruling or we may not like it – but we should not interfere in their work. my group, the alde group – the european liberals – is the champion of a europe that is for all citizens. alde believes in a europe of diversity, where everybody has the right to their own conscience, their own religion and even the freedom from religion. i will now conclude. the states – not the courts, the states – must ensure an environment where all citizens can live freely, according to their own conscience. they should be protected by the state, and i think the situation is very serious if they feel the need to go to court in order to defend themselves from the states. colleagues, reject the epp and ecr resolutions.

author. − mr president, last september a member of the ugandan parliament introduced an ‘anti-homosexuality bill’. this bill would criminalise homosexuality, leading to punishment by imprisonment and even the death penalty. this would not only apply to these alleged criminals but also to those who failed to report these so-called crimes, or who support human rights and other work in uganda. our concern is that this would violate freedom of sexual orientation as well as freedom of expression, which are human rights: they are not just european values but universal values. we also reject the death penalty as a whole and want to join the international community in its concern for ngos which may have to cancel their activities working in uganda. we therefore join president obama and others in the international community who have called for this bill not to be approved and for the review of any laws that would criminalise homosexuality in uganda.
author. − mr president, azerbaijan is signatory to a number of partnerships with the eu. it is active in the neighbourhood policy as well as in the eastern partnership. this does not only relate to trade; azerbaijan has also signed up to respect democracy, human rights and the rule of law. however, currently these are all under serious threat with the present regime. today we highlight the case of emin milli and adnan hajizade, which represents a crackdown on free media, free expression and civil society that is much deeper and wider than the case of merely two bloggers, as they have been called. they did indeed use new media like facebook and twitter for their youth organisation’s work, but the fact is that we do not even know why they are imprisoned as evidence to defend their case has not been admitted in the trial, and the trial did not meet international standards – and, indeed, looks fabricated. if we cannot rely on the azerbaijani government to stay true to its commitment to democracy, human rights and the rule of the law through the various agreements it has itself signed with the eu, then europe cannot consider azerbaijan to be a credible partner at all; this is also true of trade relations. this resolution urges the azerbaijani government to adhere to its own promises and to start building its legitimacy in the international community by respecting its own citizens, granting them democratic and human rights and allowing for respect of the rule of law. yesterday we awarded the sakharov prize in this house of the european people, and we saw a very impressive speech by the laureate, mr kovalev, who said that fear can only be met by freedom of thought, and freedom of thought can only be expressed when freedom of expression is actually guaranteed and when we listen to those people who are simply addressing issues such as opposition to their government, which we, as europeans, have to guarantee in partnership with azerbaijan in all areas.
